\begin{exercise}
What is the order of growth of the run time of a 
deterministic Finite State machine?
\begin{answer}\verified
Linear.
Finite State machines consume one character at each step.
So they run in time equal to the length of the input.
In the notation of \zcref{def:TMTime},
$t_{\mathcal{M}}(\sigma)=\lh{\sigma}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
% \begin{exercise} \label{exer:LogSmallerThanPower}
% Do the converse half of \zcref{ex:CompareLogToPower}:
% fix a logarithmic base $b>0$ and
% show that for any exponent $d>0$ the power function
% $x^d$ is not~$\bigOh(log_b(x))$
% \end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Verify that $f(x)=7$ is $\bigOh(1)$.
\item Verify that $f(x)=7+\sin(x)$ is $\bigOh(1)$.
  So if a function is in $\bigOh(1)$, that does not mean that it is 
  a constant function. 
\item Verify that $f(x)=7+(1/x)$ is also $\bigOh(1)$.
\item
  Show that a complexity function~$f$ is $\bigOh(1)$ if and
  only if it is bounded above by a constant, that is, 
  if an only if there exists
  $L\in\R$ so that $f(x)\leq L$ for all inputs $x\in\R$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
Let $g$ be the function $g(x)=1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item We can directly apply the definition of Big~$\bigOh$, \zcref{def:BigO}.
Take $N=1$ and $C=8$.
If $x>N=1$ then $C\cdot \sizeof{g(x)}=8\cdot 1$ while $\sizeof{f(x)}=7$, 
so $\sizeof{f(x)}\leq C\sizeof{g(x)}$.
\item 
The sine function has $-1\leq \sin(x)\leq 1$ for all~$x$.
Take $N=1$ and $C=9$.
If $x>N$ then $C\cdot \sizeof{g(x)}=9$ while  $\sizeof{f(x)}=7+\sin(x)\leq 8$.
\item Fix $N=1$ and~$C=8$.
If $x\geq N=1$ then $C\cdot \sizeof{g(x)}=8$ is greater than 
or equal to $\sizeof{f(x)}=7+(1/x)$.
\item We first show that if it is bounded then it is~$\bigOh(1)$.
Suppose that~$f$ is bounded by $K\in\R$.
If it is bounded by a smaller number then it is bounded by a larger so
we can take~$K\in\R^+\!$.
Fix $N=1$ and~$C=K$.
If $x\geq N=1$ then $C\cdot \sizeof{g(x)}=K\geq \sizeof{f(x)}$,
so $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$.

For the converse, suppose that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$.
Then there exists constants $N,C\in\R^+$ so that $x\geq N$ implies that
$C\cdot \sizeof{g(x)}=C\geq \sizeof{f(x)}$.
Choose $L=\max(C,f(0), \ldots f(N-1))$.
Then for any $x\in\N$ we have $f(x)\leq L$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Where does $g(x)\leq x^{\bigOh(1)}$ place the function
$g$ in the order of growth hierarchy?
\hint~see the last item in the prior question.
\begin{answer}\verified
  By the last item in the prior question,
  a function $\map{f}{\R}{\R}$ is $\bigOh(1)$ iff it is bounded by
a constant.
So $g(x)\leq x^{\bigOh(1)}$ says that the function~$g$ 
has at most a polynomial growth rate.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

%   \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
  Prove that $2^n$ is~$\bigOh(n!)$.
  \hint~because of the factorial, directly use \zcref{def:BigO} 
  and find suitable $N,C\in\N$.
  \begin{answer}\verified
    Let $N=4$ and~$C=1$.
    Note that $4!=24 > 2^4=16$.
    With that, if $n\geq N=4$ then 
    $C\cdot n! =n! 
         =4!\cdot 5\cdot 6\cdots (n-1)\cdot n 
         \geq 2^4\cdot 2^{n-4}=2^n$.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}
    
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:PowersOfLogSmallerThanAnyPoly}
Use L'H\^{o}pital's Rule as in
\zcref{ex:CompareLogToPower} to verify these for any~$d\in\R^+\!$:
\begin{exparts*}
\item $(\log_b(x))^2$ is $\bigOh(x^d)$ 
\item $(\log_b(x))^3$ is $\bigOh(x^d)$ 
\item for any~$k\in\N^+$, $(\log_b(x))^k$ is $\bigOh(x^d)$. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This limit equals~$0$
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^2}{x^d}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2\log_b(x)\cdot\frac{1}{x\ln(b)}}{dx^{d-1}}
  =\frac{2}{d\ln(b)}\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log_b(x)}{x^{d}}
\end{equation*}
by the calculation in \zcref{ex:CompareLogToPower}.
\item This limit equals~$0$
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^3}{x^d}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{3\big(\log_b(x)\big)^2\cdot\frac{1}{x\ln(b)}}{dx^{d-1}}
  =\frac{3}{d\ln(b)}\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^2}{x^{d}}
\end{equation*}
by the calculation in the prior item.
\item The prior item suggests an argument by induction.
So
assume that the statement holds for the powers $n=1$, $n=2$, \ldots $n=k$
and consider the $n=k+1$ case.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^{k+1}}{x^d}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(k+1)\big(\log_b(x)\big)^k\cdot\frac{1}{x\ln(b)}}{dx^{d-1}}
  =\frac{k+1}{d\ln(b)}\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^k}{x^{d}}
\end{equation*}
Finish by applying the $n=k$~inductive hypothesis.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
% What is the connection between computable functions and 
% feasibly computable functions?
% \begin{exparts*}
% \item Show that there is a computable function whose output values
%  grow at a rate that is $\bigOh(1)$, one whose values grow at
%  a rate that is $\bigOh(n)$, one for $\bigOh(n^2)$, etc.
% \item 
The \HP{} function $K$ is uncomputable.
  Place its rate of growth in the order of growth hierarchy, 
  \zcref{table:HardyHierarchy}.
% \item Produce a function that is not computable because its output 
%  values are larger than those of any computable function.
%  (You need not show that the rate of growth is greater, only
%  that the outputs are larger.)
% \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
% \begin{exparts}
% \item The function $f_0(x)=0$ is clearly computable and has
%   output values that grow at a rate that is $\bigOh(1)$.
%   Similarly, the output values of $f_1(x)=x$ grow
%   at a rate that is $\bigOh(x)$,
%   and $f_2(x)=x^2$ is $\bigOh(x^2)$, etc.
% \item 
The function (which is uncomputable)
  \begin{equation*}
    K(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      0  &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\diverges$}  \\
      1  &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is bounded and so is $\bigOh(1)$.
% \item Let $M(n)$ be the maximum value of $\TMfcn_i(j)$ where $0\leq i,j\leq n$
%   and $\TMfcn_i(j)\converges$ (if there is no such maximum because none of 
%   the $\TMfcn_i(j)$ converge then define $M(n)=0$).
%   This is not a computable function, but it is a function.
%   Then the function $\hat{M}(n)=M(n)+1$ has the property that for any
%   computable function $\TMfcn_e$, 
%   for all $n>e$ if $\TMfcn_e(n)\converges$ then $\hat{M}(n)>\TMfcn_e(n)$. 
% \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  Show that $2^x$ is in $\bigOh(3^x)$
  but $3^x$ is not in $\bigOh(2^x)$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Use \zcref{lem:LHBigO}.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2^x}{3^x}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\bigg(\frac{2}{3}\bigg)^x
  =0
\end{equation*}
For the second, $3^x=2^{\lg(3)\cdot x}$
where $\lg(3)$ is the base~$2$ logarithm of~$3$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}  

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Stack Exchange user anon, 
  \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/61741}
\end{credit}
\zcref{table:HardyHierarchy} states that $n!$ has a slower order of growth
than~$n^n$.
Verify this. \hint~although $n!$ is a natural number function,
  \zcref{lem:LHBigO} still applies.
\begin{answer}\verified
Consider the ratio.
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{n!}{n^n}=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2}{n}\cdots\frac{n}{n}
                \leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot 1\cdots 1
\end{equation*}
Clearly the limit of the ratio $n!/n^n$ is~$0$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\autocite{GATE21}
Consider the functions $f_1=10^n$, $f_2=n^{\ln n}$, 
and $f_3=n^{\sqrt{n}}\!$.
Which of these arranges the functions in increasing order of asymptotic
growth rate?
\begin{exparts*}
\item $f_3$, $f_2$, $f_1$,  
\item $f_2$, $f_1$, $f_3$,  
\item $f_1$, $f_2$, $f_3$,  
\item $f_2$, $f_3$, $f_1$.  
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
It is \textsc{(D)}.

To establish $\bigOh(f_2)<\bigOh(f_3)$ we have
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^{\ln x}}{x^{\sqrt{x}}}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}x^{\ln x-\sqrt{x}}=0
\end{equation*}
because $\lim_{x\to\infty}(\ln x-\sqrt{x})=-\infty$.
(One way to prove this limit is to use 
\zcref{ex:CompareLogToPower}.
Another way is to multiply $\ln x-\sqrt{x}$ by the fraction
$(\ln x+\sqrt{x})/(\ln x+\sqrt{x})$.
That gives this.
\begin{align*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(\ln x)^2-x}{\sqrt{x}+\ln(x)}
  &=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2\ln x\cdot(1/x)-1}{1/(2x^{1/2})+1/x}
    =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2x}{2x}\cdot\frac{2\ln x\cdot(1/x)-1}{1/(2x^{1/2})+1/x}  
  =2\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2\ln x-x}{\sqrt{x}+2}  \\  
  &=2\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(2/x)-1}{1/(2x^{1/2})}
   =4\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}x^{1/2}\cdot(\frac{2}{x}-1)
   =4\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty} 2x^{-1/2}-x^{1/2}
   =4\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2-x}{\sqrt{x}}   \\
   &=4\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{-1}{1/(2x^{1/2})}
    =4\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}-2\sqrt{x}
\end{align*}
That's $-\infty$.)

For $\bigOh(f_3)<\bigOh(f_1)$ 
take the limit of the natural logarithm of the numerator and denominator.
Since the natural logarithm is a
strictly increasing function, this suffices for the result.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\ln(f_3(x))}{\ln(f_1(x))}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\ln(x^{\sqrt{x}})}{\ln(10^x)}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{x}\ln(x)}{x\ln(10)}
  =\frac{1}{\ln(10)}\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\ln(x)}{\sqrt{x}}
\end{equation*}
Here also \zcref{ex:CompareLogToPower} applies, or note that 
this is an ``$\infty/\infty$'' type limit and
apply L'H\^{o}pital.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1/x}{1/(2x^{1/2})}
  =2\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{x}}{x}
  =2\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}
  =0
\end{equation*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Two complexity functions $f,g$ are 
\definend{asymptotically equivalent, $f\sim g$},\index{asymptotically equivalent}\index{similar@$\sim$, asymptotically equivalent} 
if $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)=1$.
Show that each pair is asymptotically equivalent:
\begin{exparts*}
\item $f(x)=x^2+5x+1$ and~$g(x)=x^2\!$,
\item $\lg(x+1)$ and~$\lg(x)$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Apply L'H\^{o}pital's Rule twice, after verifying that they are 
both ``$\infty/\infty$'' limits.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^2+5x+1}{x^2}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2x+5}{2x}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2}{2}=1
\end{equation*}
\item Apply L'H\^{o}pital's Rule also here, remembering to use the Chain Rule 
for the numerator.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\lg(x+1)}{\lg(x)}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(1/x\ln(2))\cdot 1}{1/x\ln(2)}
  =1
\end{equation*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
Is there an~$f$ so that $\bigOh(f)$ is the set of all polynomials?
\begin{answer}\verified
No.
If $\bigOh(f)$ is the set of polynomials then, because it is a member
of that set,~$f$ must be a polynomial.
However, then~$f$ has some degree and 
no polynomial of higher degree is a member of~$\bigOh(f)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that $x^{\lg x}$ is quasipolynomial.
\begin{answer}\verified
We have 
$x^{\lg x}=(2^{\lg x})^{\lg x}=2^{(\lg x)^2}\in\bigOh(2^{\poly(\lg x)})$
(the first equality comes from the identity 
$x=2^{\lg x}$).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:OrdersBetweenPolyAndExp}
The prior exercise shows that $f(x)=x^{\lg x}$ is quasipolynomial.
Here we will show that the order of growth of~$f$ 
is strictly between polynomial and exponential.
\begin{exparts*}
\item As a preliminary for the next item,
  show that for any constant $k\in\R$ we have
  $k\cdot \lg x$ is $\bigOh((\lg x)^2)$ but
  $(\lg x)^2$ is not $\bigOh(k\cdot\lg x)$.
\item Show that $f\mkern1mu$'s growth is strictly greater than polynomial
  by arguing that for any constant power~$k$, we have $x^k\in\bigOh(x^{\lg x})$
  but $x^{\lg x}\notin\bigOh(x^k)$.
  \hint~take the ratio, rewrite using the identity $a=2^{\lg a}\!$,
  and consider the limit of the exponent.
\item As a preliminary for the next item, show that $(\lg x)^2$ is 
  $\bigOh(x)$ but $x$ is not $\bigOh((\lg x)^2)$.
% \item Show that $x^{\lg x}=2^{(\lg x)^2}\!$.
%  \hint~take the logarithm of both halves.
\item Show that $f\mkern1mu$'s growth is strictly less than exponential by 
  arguing that $x^{\lg x}$ is $\bigOh(2^x)$ but 
  $2^x$ is not $\bigOh(x^{\lg x})$.
  \hint~again take the ratio, again rewrite using the identity,
  and again consider the limit of the exponent.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
Recall the logarithm rules that $\log_b(x^a)=a\cdot\log_bx$ 
and $b^{\log_b(x)}=x$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Fix a constant $k\in\R$.  
Consider the ratio.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{k\lg x}{(\lg x)^2}
  =
  k\cdot \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lg x}
  =0
\end{equation*}
\zcref{lem:LHBigO} gives the desired conclusion.
\item By \zcref{lem:LHBigO}, it suffices to show that this is~$0$. 
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^k}{x^{\lg(x)}}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty} x^{k-\lg(x)}
\end{equation*}
Rewrite using the identity that $a=2^{\lg(a)}\!$. 
\begin{equation*}
  % \lim_{x\to\infty}x^{k-\lg(x)}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}2^{\lg(\,x^{k-\lg(x)}\,)}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}2^{(k-\lg(x))\cdot \lg(x)}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}2^{k\lg(x)-(\lg(x))^2}
\end{equation*}
The prior item gives that the limit of the exponent is~$-\infty$,
and so the limit of the entire expression is~$0$.
\item L'H\^{o}pital's Rule gives this.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(\lg x)^2}{x}
  =    
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2\lg x\cdot(1/x)}{1}
  =    
  2\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\lg x}{x}
  =    
  2\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(1/x)}{1}
  =    
  2\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{x}
  =0
\end{equation*}
% Then \zcref{lem:LHBigO} gives that
% $\lim_{x\to\infty}(\lg x)^2-x=-\infty$.  
\item Apply \zcref{lem:LHBigO} along with L'H\^{o}pital's Rule
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^{\lg x}}{2^x}
  =    
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(2^{\lg x})^{\lg x}}{2^x}
  =    
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2^{(\lg x)^2}}{2^x}
  =    
  \lim_{x\to\infty}2^{(\lg x)^2-x}
\end{equation*}
(the first equality uses the identity $a=2^{\lg(a)}$). 
The prior item gives that the limit of the exponent is
$\lim_{x\to\infty}(\lg x)^2-x=-\infty$,
and so the limit of the entire expression is~$0$.
% \item Taking $\lg$ of $x^{\lg(x)}$ gives 
% $\lg(x^{\lg x})=\lg(x)\cdot\lg(x)$.
% Taking $\lg$ of the other expression gives $\lg(2^{(\lg x)^2})=(\lg(x))^2\cdot \lg(2)$.
% Since the logarithm and exponential are inverse functions,
% that is $(\lg x)^2\!$.
% \item Rewrite $x^{\lg(x)}$ as $2^{(\lg(x))^2}\!$.
% \zcref{lem:LHBigO}
% \begin{equation*}
%   \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2^{(\lg(x))^2}}{2^x}
%   =
%   \lim_{x\to\infty} 2^{(\lg(x))^2-x}
%   =
%   0
% \end{equation*}
% gives the desired conclusion because the limit of the exponent is $-\infty$
% by \zcref{table:HardyHierarchy}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:BigOAlgProps}
  Verify the clauses of \zcref{lem:BigOAlgProps}.
  Take all functions to be complexity functions.
  \begin{exparts*}
    \item
      If $a\in\R^+$ then $a f$ is also~$\bigOh(g)$.
    \item If $f_1$ is $\bigOh(f_0)$ 
      then the functions $f_0+f_1$ and $f_0-f_1$ are also $\bigOh(f_0)$.
    \item Even if $f_1$ is~$\bigOh(f_0)$,
      we cannot conclude that $f_0f_1$ is $\bigOh(f_0)$
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified 
We will use the notation from the lemma statement.
\begin{exparts}
\item 
Suppose that $a\in\R$ is nonnegative.
Since $f$ is a complexity function there is an $N\in\R$ so that
$x\geq N$ implies that $f$ is defined and nonnegative.
Fix $C=a$.
If $x\geq N$ then
$\sizeof{af(x)}=af\,(x)\leq a\cdot f(x)=a\sizeof{f(x)}$,
as required.
\item 
Suppose that $f_1$ is $\bigOh(f_0)$.
By definition there are constants $N,C\in\R$ so that if $x\geq N$
then $\sizeof{f_1(x)}\leq C\cdot \sizeof{f_0(x)}$.
Because these two functions are complexity functions we can assume that
$N$ is so large that both are nonnegative for all inputs~$x$.
That gives $f_0(x)+f_1(x)\leq f_0(x)+Cf_0(x)=(1+C)\cdot f_0(x)$,
and hence there is a constant, $1+C$, giving that $f_0+f_1$ is in $\bigOh(f_0)$.

The argument for $f_0-f_1$ is the same except that the constant is
$1-C$.
\item Take $f_0(x)=x^2$ and $f_1(x)=x$.
Then the function $f_0f_1$ is given by $f_0f_1(x)=x^3\!$,
so $f_0f_1$ is not in $\bigOh(f_0)$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:BigThetaEquivalence}
  Verify these clauses of \zcref{lem:BigThetaEquivalence}.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item The Big-$\bigOh$ relation is reflexive.
  \item It is also transitive.
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item We must show that~$f$ is in~$\bigOh(f)$.
  The function $f$ is a complexity function, so there is some number
  beyond which it is defined and positive.
  Take $N$ to be that number and take $C=1$.
\item Suppose that $f_0$ is~$\bigOh(f_1)$ 
and that $f_1$ is~$\bigOh(f_2)$.
Then there exists $N_0,N_1,C_0,C_1\in\R$ such that 
if $x\geq N_0$ then $C_0\cdot f_1(x)\geq f_0(x)$, 
and 
if $x\geq N_1$ then $C_1\cdot f_2(x)\geq f_1(x)$.
Take $\hat{N}=\max(N_0,N_1)$ and $\hat{C}=C_0\cdot C_1$.
Then $x\geq \hat{N}$ implies that 
$\hat{C}\cdot f_2(x)=C_0C_1\cdot f_2(x)\geq C_0\cdot f_1(x) \geq f_0(x)$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/925053/using-limits-to-determine-big-o-big-omega-and-big-theta
% (given an example where the limit doesn't exist?)
% http://web.mit.edu/broder/Public/asymptotics-cheatsheet.pdf
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:LHBigO}
\begin{credit}
  Stack Exchange user Ilmari Karonen,
  \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/925053/using-limits-to-determine-big-o-big-omega-and-big-theta}
\end{credit}
  We will prove the first of \zcref{lem:LHBigO}'s three items;~the other two 
  are similar.
  (This requires the precise definition of a limit, which may be 
  outside of the prerequisites for this course:~$\lim_{x\to\infty}h(x)=0$ if for
  all $\varepsilon>0$ there is an $M\in\R$ such that 
  $x\geq M$ implies that $|h(x)|<\varepsilon$.)
  Assume that
  $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)$ exists and equals~$0$.
  \begin{exparts*} 
    \item 
      Show that $f$ is $\bigOh(g)$.
    \item Show that $g$ is not $\bigOh(f)$.
    % \item We can give an example where $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$ 
    %    even though $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)$
    %    does not exist.
    %    Verify that, where $g(x)=x$ and where
    %    $f(x)=x$ when $\floor{x}$ is odd and
    %    $f(x)=2x$ when $\floor{x}$ is even.
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
Recall that by definition $f$ is $\bigOh(g)$ if and only if there exists
numbers $N,C\in\R$ such that if $x\geq N$ then
$|f(x)|\leq C\cdot|g(x)|$.
\begin{exparts}
\item If $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)$ exists and equals~$0$ then 
  for any $\varepsilon>0$ there is an~$M\in\R$ such that $x\geq M$ implies that 
  $|f(x)/g(x)|<\varepsilon$, and therefore
  $|f(x)|<\varepsilon\cdot |g(x)|$.
  Taking $N$ to be~$M$ and $C$ to be $\varepsilon$, this 
  is exactly what is required to conclude that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$.
\item
  Suppose otherwise, that $g$ is~$\bigOh(f)$.
  Then by definition there are constants $K,P\in\R$ such that
  if $x\geq K$ then $|g(x)|\leq P\cdot |f(x)|$, which gives
  $1/P\leq |f(x)/g(x)|$.

  The question's assumption is that
  $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)$ exists and equals~$0$.
  Fix $\varepsilon=1/(P+1)$.
  Then there exists a constant $M\in\R$ so that if $x\geq M$ then
  $|f(x)/g(x)|\leq 1/(P+1)$.

  Those two paragraphs conflict for $x$'s larger than the maximum of $K$ 
  and~$M$.
% \item To show that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$, take $N=1$ and~$C=4$.

%   On the other hand, 
%   the limit doesn't exist, as the ratio oscillates between $1$
%   and~$2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:PolySlowerThanExp}
  Prove \zcref{lem:PolySlowerThanExp}.
\begin{answer}\verified
First use L'H\^{o}pital's Rule to get that 
if $g(x)=a_mx^m+\cdots+a_0$
then $g$ is~$\bigTheta(x^m)$.
Then \zcref{ex:CompareLogToPower} shows that any logarithmic function
is $\bigOh(g)$.

For the other half, fix a base~$b>1$ and consider the exponential function
$h(x)=b^x\!$.
L'H\^{o}pital's Rule gives this
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^m}{b^x}
  =
  \frac{m}{\ln(b)}\cdot \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^{m-1}}{b^x}
  =
  \frac{m(m-1)}{(\ln(b))^2}\cdot \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^{m-2}}{b^x}
  =\cdots =
  \frac{m!}{(\ln(b))^m}\cdot \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{b^x}
  =0
\end{equation*}
and \zcref{lem:LHBigO} gives the desired conclusion.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{order of growth|)}






% =============================================
\section{A problem miscellany}\index{problem miscellany|(}
Much of today's work in the Theory of Computation is driven by problems
that originate outside of the subject.
We will describe some of these problems to get a sense of the ones that 
people work on and also to use for examples and exercises.
All of these problems are well known to anyone in the field.




\margingraphic{0.18\textwidth}{complexity/pix/hamilton.png}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rowan_Hamilton}{William Rowan Hamilton 1805--1865}}\index{Hamilton, W R!picture} % By William_Rowan_Hamilton_portrait_oval.png: Unknown William_Rowan_Hamilton_portrait_oval_2.png: Unknown derivative work: Quibik [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
\subsection{Problems with stories}
We start with a few that come packaged with a background.
These anecdotes are fun and 
\citetext{an important part of the field's culture}{%
  That is, these are storied problems.
}.
They
also give a sense of how problems arise in general.

W\,R~Hamilton was a polymath whose genius was recognized early, and he was 
given a sinecure as Astronomer Royal of Ireland.  
He made important contributions to classical mechanics, where his 
reformulation of Newtonian mechanics is now called Hamiltonian mechanics. 
Other work of his in physics helped develop classical field theories such as 
electromagnetism and laid the groundwork for
the development of quantum mechanics.  
In mathematics, he is best known as the
\citetext{inventor of the quaternion number system}{See \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_quaternions}.}.

One of his ventures was a game, \citetext{\textit{Around the World}}{% 
  Another version was
  called \textit{The Icosian Game}.
  See \url{http://puzzlemuseum.com/month/picm02/200207icosian.htm}.}.
The vertices in the graph below 
were holes in a wooden board, imagined as world cities.
Players put pegs in the holes, looking for a circuit that visits each 
city once and only once. 
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{Hamilton's \textit{Around the World} game}\centering
    % \includegraphics[height=0.75in]{complexity/icosehedron.png}
    % \hspace{2em}
    \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems13}
  \end{fig} 
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{Hamilton's \textit{Around the World} game}\centering
    % \includegraphics[height=0.75in]{complexity/icosehedron.png}
    % \hspace{2em}
    \animategraphics[poster=first]{2}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{13}{14}
  \end{animatedfigure} 
}

\noindent It did not make Hamilton rich.
But it did get him associated with a great problem.

%<*problem:HamiltonianProblem>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}}]\index{Hamiltonian Circuit problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}\label{prob:HamCircuit}
Given a graph, decide if it contains 
a cyclic path that includes each vertex once and only once.
\end{problem}
%</problem:HamiltonianProblem>

% This is stated as a type of problem called a decision problem 
% because it asks for a `yes' or `no' answer.
% % In this section we will see a
% % a variety of types of problems.
% The next section will say more about problem types. 

%<*discussion:KnightsTouri>
A special case
is the \definend{\probname{Knight's Tour}} problem,\index{Knight's Tour@\probname{Knight's Tour} problem}
to use a chess knight to make a circuit of the squares on the board.
(Recall that a knight moves three squares at a time,
with the first two squares in one direction 
and then the third one perpendicular to that direction.)
%</discussion:KnightsTouri>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1in]{complexity/pix/knight.jpg}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.2in]{complexity/pix/knightstour.png}}
\end{center}
%<*discussion:KnightsTourii>
\citetext{This is the solution given by L~Euler}{%
  The figure is from \autocite{EulerKnightsTour}.}.
In graph terms, there are sixty four vertices,
representing the board squares.
An edge goes between two vertices if they are connected by a single knight move.
\probname{Knight's Tour} asks for a Hamiltonian circuit 
of that graph.
%</discussion:KnightsTourii>

\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} has another famous variant.

%<*problem:TravellingSalesman>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Traveling Salesman}}]\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem}  \label{ex:TravellingSalesman}
Given a weighted undirected graph, 
where we call the vertices $S=\set{c_0,\ldots c_{k-1}}$
`cities' and we call the edge weights  $d(c_i,c_j)\in\N$ for
$i\neq j$ % (subject to $d(c_i,c_j)=d(c_j,c_i)$) 
the `distance' between those cities, 
find the shortest-distance circuit that visits every city
and returns back to the start.
\end{problem}
%</problem:TravellingSalesman>

\begin{wrapfigure}{r}[3em]{0.32\textwidth}  % "r" for right side 
  \includegraphics[width=0.14\textwidth]{complexity/pix/menger.png}% 
  % \vspace*{1ex}
  \hspace*{1em}
  \includegraphics[width=0.14\textwidth]{complexity/pix/Julia_Robinson_1975.jpg}% 
  \caption{\protect\citetext{\label{fig:menger}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Menger}{Karl Menger\ 1902--1985} and \label{fig:robinson}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Robinson}{Julia Robinson 1919--1985}}{%
  Photo credits: unknown and  George M. Bergman from the  Oberwolfach Photo Collection, via Wikimedia Commons}}\index{Menger, K!picture}\index{Robinson, J!picture}
\end{wrapfigure} 
\citetext{We can start with a map of the state capitals of the forty eight contiguous US~states}{%
  For much more, see \url{https://www.math.uwaterloo.ca/tsp/}.
}
and the distances between them: Montpelier~VT to Albany~NY is $254$
kilometers, etc.
From among all trips that visit each city and return back to the start,
% such as 
% $\text{Montpelier}\rightarrow\text{Albany}\rightarrow\text{Harrisburg}\rightarrow \cdots \rightarrow\text{Montpelier}$,
we want the shortest.
This was
\citetext{posed by K~Menger and named by J~Robinson}{%
  The \probname{Traveling Salesman} was first posed by Menger in an
  article that appeared in the same journal and issue as
  G\"odel's Incompleteness Theorem.
  Menger and G\"odel were close friends, with the same advisor.
  Robinson's publication using the phrase
  ``travelling [or traveling] salesman problem'' was a 1949 RAND Corporation,
  \textit{On the Hamiltonian game (a traveling salesman problem)}.
  In addition to this, she made many other contributons to the
  Theory of Computation.
}.

% \ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
%   \margingraphic{0.17\textwidth}{complexity/pix/euler.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonhard_Euler}{Leonhard Euler 1707--1783}}\index{Euler, L!picture} % Jakob Emanuel Handmann [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3ALeonhard_Euler.jpg
% }{%
%   \relax
% }

\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \relax
}{%
\margingraphic[l]{0.35\textwidth}{complexity/pix/xkcdtsp.png}{\label{fig:xkcdTravellingSalesman}\href{https://xkcd.com/399/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
}
We can recast this from an optimization problem to a `yes' or `no' decision 
problem.
Introduce a parameter~$B$ and change the problem statement 
to:~decide if
there is a circuit of total distance less than~$B$.
With an algorithm to quickly solve this decision problem
we could also quickly solve the optimization problem, by first asking 
whether there is a trip bounded by length $B=1$, then asking if there is a 
trip of length $B=2$, etc.
When we get a `yes' then we know the shortest length.

% \ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
%   \relax
% }{%
%   \margingraphic{0.19\textwidth}{complexity/pix/euler.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonhard_Euler}{Leonhard Euler 1707--1783}}\index{Euler, L!picture} % Jakob Emanuel Handmann [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3ALeonhard_Euler.jpg
% }
The next problem sounds much like \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}, in that it
involves exhaustively
traversing a graph.
But it proves to act very differently.

Today the city of 
\citetext{Kaliningrad is a Russian enclave between Poland and Lithuania}{%
 \protect\begin{center} \protect\small % Credit: https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Kaliningrad_Oblast_within_Europe.svg
    \protect\includegraphics[height=1.25in]{complexity/pix/kaliningrad_in_europe.png}
  \\
  \protect\label{fig:kaliningrad}\protect\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaliningrad}{Kaliningrad, on the Baltic Sea between Poland and Lithuania} %
  \protect\end{center} }.
But in 1727 it was in Prussia and was called 
\citetext{K\"onigsberg}{%
  This happens to be the hometown of David Hilbert.
  }.\index{Konigsberg@K\"onigsberg}
The Pregel river divides the city into four areas,
connected by seven bridges.
The citizens used to promenade,
to take leisurely walks or drives where they could see and be seen.
The question arose:~can 
a person cross each bridge
once and only once,
and arrive back at the start?
No one could think of a way but no one could think of a reason that there was
no way.
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  A local mayor wrote to Euler
}{%
\citetext{A local mayor wrote to Euler}{%
  XKCD, as usual, is on it.
    \protect\begin{center} \protect\small
    \protect\includegraphics[height=1in]{complexity/pix/konigsberg.png}
  \\
  \protect\label{fig:xkcdkonigsberg}\protect\href{https://xkcd.com/2694/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}
  \protect\end{center}
}, 
}
who proved that
\citetext{no circuit is possible}{%
  Consider a land mass. 
  For each bridge in
  there must be an associated bridge out.
  So an at least necessary condition is that the land masses have an even
  number of associated edges.
  It that is not true for this city.}.
This paper founded Graph Theory.
\begin{center}  \setlength{\fboxsep}{0pt} \setlength{\fboxrule}{0.8pt}
  % By Merian-Erben [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
  % https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AImage-Koenigsberg%2C_Map_by_Merian-Erben_1652.jpg
  % \autocite{EulerBridges}
  % \vcenteredhbox{\fcolorbox{boldcolor}{white}{\includegraphics[height=1.40in]{complexity/pix/koenigsberg.jpg}}}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems15.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=0.6in]{complexity/pix/eulerbridges.jpg}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.2\textwidth}@{}}
    \includegraphics[width=0.16\textwidth]{complexity/pix/euler.jpg} \\
    \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonhard_Euler}{Leonhard Euler 1707--1783}\index{Euler, L!picture} % Jakob Emanuel Handmann [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3ALeonhard_Euler.jpg
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
Euler's summary sketch is in the middle and the associated graph is on the right.

%<*prob:EulerCircuit>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Euler Circuit}}]\index{Euler Circuit@\probname{Euler Circuit} problem} \label{prob:EulerCircuit}
Given a graph, find a circuit that traverses each edge once
and only once, or find that no such circuit exists.
\end{problem}
%</prob:EulerCircuit>

Next is a problem that sounds hard.
But all of us see it solved every day, for instance when we 
ask our smartphone for the
shortest route to some place.

%<*prob:ShortestPath>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Shortest Path}}]\index{Shortest Path problem@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} \label{prob:ShortestPath}
Given a weighted graph and two vertices, 
find the least-weight path between them, or find that no path exists.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ShortestPath>

There is an algorithm that solves this problem quickly.\footnote{%
  Dijkstra's algorithm is at worst quadratic in the number of vertices.}
For instance, with the graph below we could look for 
the path from $A$ to~$F$ of least cost.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs08.pdf}
\end{center} 

The next problem was proposed in 1852 by a young mathematician, F~Guthrie,
who was drawing a map of England's counties.
He wanted to color them
with different colors when the counties shared a border. 
His map required only four colors and he
conjectured that for any map, four colors were enough.

\margingraphic[l]{0.15\textwidth}{complexity/pix/demorgan.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus_De_Morgan}{Augustus De Morgan 1806--1871}}\index{De Morgan, A!picture} % By Sophia Elizabeth De Morgan (Memoir of Augustus De Morgan) [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2c/De_Morgan_Augustus.jpg
Guthrie imposed the condition that 
\citetext{the countries must be contiguous}{%
  A notable example of a non-contiguous country in the wold today
  is that Russia is separated from Kaliningrad, the city that 
  used to be known as K\'{o}nigsberg.}
and he defined `sharing a border' to mean sharing an interval,
not just a point (see \zcref{exer:FiveColors}).
Below is a map and a graph version of the same problem.
In the graph, counties are vertices and edges connect ones that share a border.
It is important that the graph is 
\definend{planar}\Dash\index{graph!planar}\index{planar graph}
\citetext{we can draw the graph in the plane}{%
  This is because the graph comes from a planar map.} 
so that its edges do not cross.

The \definend{\probname{Four Color}}\index{Four Color@\probname{Four Color} problem} 
problem is to \citetext{start with a planar graph}{%
  The graph is undirected and without loops.} 
and end with
the vertices partitioned into no more than four sets, called 
\definend{colors},\index{colors}\index{graph!colors}
such that adjacent vertices are in different colors.
%It is a special case of the \probname{Graph Colorability} problem,  
%given next.
Guthrie consulted his former professor, A~De~Morgan,
who was also unable to either prove or disprove the conjecture.
But he did make the problem famous by promoting it among his
friends.
% From https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:English_ceremonial_counties_2010.svg
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{\protect\citetext{Counties of England and the derived planar graph}{%
      This is today's map.
      At the time, some counties were not contiguous.}}\centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.65in]{complexity/pix/counties/counties01}}
    \hspace{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.65in]{complexity/asy/problems/problems16.pdf}}
  \end{fig} 
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{\protect\citetext{Counties of England and the derived planar graph}{%
      This is today's map.
      At the time, some counties were not contiguous.}}\centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\animategraphics[height=1.65in,poster=last]{2}{complexity/pix/counties/counties}{00}{01}}
    \hspace{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.65in]{complexity/asy/problems/problems16.pdf}}
  \end{animatedfigure} 
}

\margingraphic[l]{0.27\textwidth}{complexity/pix/fourcolorssuffice.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_color_theorem}{Appel and Haken's post office celebrating}} % from http://mathyear2013.blogspot.com/2013/04/dr-kenneth-appel-and-four-color-map.html
It remained unsolved until 1976, when
K~Appel and W~Haken reduced the proof to 1\,936 cases and got a
computer to check those cases.
This was the first major proof that was done on a computer and 
\citetext{it was controversial}{
  See \url{https://www.maa.org/sites/default/files/pdf/upload_library/22/Ford/Swart697-707.pdf}.}.
Many mathematicians felt that the purpose of the subject was to 
understand things and not just be satisfied when a 
computer program (that conceivably had bugs)
assures us that theorems are verified.\footnote{%
  This is in contrast to the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem}.}
However, today's generation of mathematicians is more 
comfortable with this and  
now computer proofs are routine.

%<*prob:GraphColorabilityi>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Graph Colorability}}]\index{Graph Colorability problem@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem}\index{k Coloring problem@\probname{$k$ Coloring} problem} \label{prob:GraphColorability}
\citetext{Given a graph and a number $k\in\N$}{%
  In the name of the problem we often omit the $k$.}, decide whether the 
graph is \definend{$k$-colorable}, 
whether we can partition its vertices into $k$-many sets, 
$\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{C}_0\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{C}_{k-1}$, such
that no two same-set vertices are connected.
\end{problem}
%</prob:GraphColorabilityi>

%<*prob:GraphColorabilityii>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Chromatic Number}}]\index{Chromatic Number@\probname{Chromatic Number} problem} \label{prob:ChromaticNumber}
Given a graph, find the smallest number $k\in\N$ such that the 
graph is $k$-colorable.
\end{problem}
%</prob:GraphColorabilityii>

\margingraphic{0.16\textwidth}{complexity/pix/boole.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Boole}{George Boole 1815--1864}}\index{Boole, G!picture} % See page for author [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AGeorge_Boole_color.jpg
Our final story introduces a problem that  
will be a benchmark to which we compare other problems.
In 1847, G~Boole outlined what we today call Boolean algebra.
%  in
% \citetext{\textit{An Investigation of the Laws of Thought on Which are Founded the Mathematical Theories of Logic and Probabilities}}{%
%   A brief biography is at \protect\url{https://www.3quarksdaily.com/3quarksdaily/2018/02/george-boole-and-the-calculus-of-thought-5.html}.}.
% It provided a systematic mathematical treatment
% of simple logical relations.
%
A variable is Boolean\index{Boolean}\index{Boolean!variable} 
if it takes only the values 
\true{} or~\false, for `true' or `false'.
% A \definend{Boolean function}\index{Boolean!function}\index{function!Boolean} 
% inputs and outputs tuples of those. 
We focus on Boolean expressions\index{Boolean!expression} 
that connect variables using the 
\definend{and operator~$\wedge$},\index{logical operator!and}\index{conjunction}
the \definend{or operator~$\vee$},\index{logical operator!or}\index{disjunction}
and the \definend{not operator~$\neg$}.\index{logical operator!not}\index{negation}
(For more, see Appendix~\ref{sec:PropLogic}.)
This Boolean function\index{Boolean!function}\index{function!Boolean}
is given by an expression with three variables.
\begin{equation*}
  f(P,Q,R)=
  (P\vee Q)\wedge (P\vee \neg Q)\wedge (\neg P\vee Q)
  \wedge (\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R) % \wedge (\neg P\vee R)
\end{equation*}
An expression is \definend{satisfiable}\index{satisfiable Propositional logic expression} 
if some combination
of input \true's and \false's makes it evaluate to~\true\!.
It is in 
\citetext{\definend{Conjunctive Normal form}}{%
  Any Boolean function can be expressed in that form; see the Appendix.},\index{Conjunctive Normal form}\index{CNF} 
\definend{CNF},
if it consists of clauses that are connected with $\wedge$'s, 
where inside each clause are variables or negations connected by $\vee$'s.
The expression above is in that form.
The \definend{truth table}\index{truth table} 
below shows the input-output behavior of the
function defined by the expression.
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\vee Q$ &$P\vee \neg Q$ &$\neg P\vee Q$ &$\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R$ &$f(P,Q,R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
That \true{} in the final column witnesses that this formula is satisfiable.

%<*prob:SAT>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Satisfiability}}, \definend{$\SAT$}]\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}\index{SAT@$\SAT$|see {\probname{Satisfiability} problem}}  \label{prob:SAT}
Decide if a given Boolean expression
is satisfiable.
\end{problem}
%</prob:SAT>

%<*prob:ThreeSAT>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{3-Satisfiability}}, \definend{\ThreeSAT}]\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}\index{3SAT@\ThreeSAT{} problem}\index{3SAT@\ThreeSAT{} problem|see {3-Satisfiability problem}}\label{prob:3SAT}
Given a propositional logic formula in 
Conjunctive Normal form\index{CNF}\index{Propositional logic!Conjunctive Normal form}
in which each clause has at most three variables,
decide if it is satisfiable.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ThreeSAT>

If the number of input variables is~$v$ 
then the number of rows in the truth table is $2^v\!$. 
So solving $\SAT$ appears to require exponential time.
Whether that supposition is right is a very important question, 
as we will see in later sections.







% =========================================
\subsection{More problems, omitting the stories}
We will list more example problems but leaving out the background
(although for some of them the motivation is clear even without a story).
These problems are also widely known in the field. 

%<*prob:reachability>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}}]\index{Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem@\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem}\label{prob:vertextovertexpath}
Given a graph and two vertices,
find if the second is reachable\index{reachable vertex}\index{vertex!reachable} 
from the first.\footnotemark[1]
\end{problem}\footnotetext[1]{%
  % There are lots of problems about paths so 
  % just calling this the Path problem would be confusing.
  The name
  \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} is nonstandard.
  It is usually known as 
  \probname{$st$-Path},\protect\index{st Path@\probname{$st$-Path} problem|see {\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem}} 
  \probname{$st$-Connectivity},\protect\index{st Connectivity@\probname{$st$-Connectivity} problem|see {\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem}}
  or \probname{STCON}\protect\index{STCON@\probname{STCON} problem|see {\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem}}
  ($s$ and $t$ stand for vertices).}
%</prob:reachability>

\begin{example}
These are two Western-tradition constellations, Ursa Minor
and Draco.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems26.pdf}
\end{center}
Here we can solve the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem by eye.
For any two vertices in Ursa Minor there is a path and
for any two vertices in Draco there is a path.
But if the two vertices are in different constellations 
then there is no path.
\end{example}

For a graph with many thousands of nodes, such as a computer network,
the problem is harder than in the prior example.
A close variant problem is to decide, given a graph, 
whether all vertex pairs are connected. 

%<*prob:minspanningtree>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Minimum Spanning Tree}}]\index{Minimum Spanning Tree@\probname{Minimum Spanning Tree} problem} \label{prob:minspanningtree}
Given a weighted undirected graph,
find a subgraph containing
all the vertices of the original graph such that its edges have a minimum
total.
\end{problem}
%</prob:minspanningtree>

This is an undirected graph with weights on the edges.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity21.pdf}
\end{center}
The highlighted subgraph
includes all of
the vertices, that is, it 
\definend{spans}\index{span a graph}\index{spanning subgraph}\index{graph!spanning subgraph} 
the graph.
In addition, its weights total to a minimum from among all
of the spanning subgraphs.
From that it follows that this subgraph is a 
\definend{tree},\index{tree}\index{graph!tree} 
meaning that it has no cycles, or else we could eliminate
an edge from the cycle and thereby lower the edge weight total without
dropping any vertices.

This looks somewhat like the \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem
in that the sought-for subgraph contains all of the vertices.
However, for the \probname{Minimum Spanning Tree} 
problem we know algorithms that are quick, $\bigOh(n\lg n)$.

%<*prob:VertexCover>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Vertex Cover}}]\index{Vertex Cover problem@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a 
size~$B$\index{vertex cover}\index{graph!vertex cover}
set of vertices
such that for any edge, at least one of its ends
is a member of that set.
\end{problem}
%</prob:VertexCover>

\begin{example}
A museum posts guards to watch their exhibits.
There are eight halls, laid out as edges below.
They will
put the guards at some of the corners $w_0$, \ldots{} $w_5$.
What is the smallest number of guards that suffices to watch
all of the hallways?
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs15.pdf}
\end{center}
Checking each corner shows that one guard will not suffice.
The two-element set $C=\set{w_0,w_4}$ is a vertex cover:~every hallway has
at least one end in~$C$.
\end{example}

%<*prob:Clique>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Clique}}]\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem}
\label{prob:Clique}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a 
size~$B$\index{clique, in a graph}\index{graph!clique}
set of vertices such that any two are connected.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Clique>

The term `clique' comes from social networks; 
if the nodes represent people
and the edges connect friends then a clique is a set of 
people who are all friends.

A graph with a $4$-clique has a subgraph like the one below on the left
and any graph with a $5$~clique has a subgraph like the one the right.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity02.pdf}
  \hspace{3em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity12.pdf}
\end{center}

\begin{example}
This graph has a $4$-clique.
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{Instance of the Clique problem}\centering 
    \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems09}
  \end{fig} 
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{Instance of the Clique problem}\centering 
    \animategraphics[poster=last]{1}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{08}{09}
  \end{animatedfigure} 
}
\end{example}

%<*prob:MaxCut>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Max Cut}}]\index{Max Cut@\probname{Max Cut} problem}  \label{prob:maxcut}
  A \definend{graph cut} 
  partitions the vertices into two disjoint subsets. 
  The \definend{cut set}
  contains edges that have a vertex in each 
  subset. 
The \probname{Max Cut} problem is to find the partition 
with the largest cut set.
\end{problem}
%</prob:MaxCut>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:MaxCut}
  The graph cut $\set{\set{v_0,v_1,v_4,v_5},\set{v_2,v_3}}$, shown with
  differently-colored vertices, 
  gives the cut set $\set{e_1,e_2,e_3,e_4,e_5,e_6}$ of size six, 
  the largest possible.\footnotemark
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
    \begin{fig}{A partition for the graph with a maximum cut set.}\centering 
      \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems48}
    \end{fig} 
}{%
    \begin{animatedfigure}{A graph partition with a maximum cut set.}\centering 
      \animategraphics[poster=last]{1}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{46}{48}
    \end{animatedfigure} 
}
\end{example}\footnotetext{%
   One way to verify this is with a script that 
   checks all two-set partitions of the vertices.}

%<*prob:ThreeDMatching>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Three Dimensional Matching}}]\index{Three Dimensional Matching problem@\probname{Three Dimensional Matching} problem}  
\label{Prob:ThreeDMatching}
Let the sets $X,Y,Z$ all have the same number of elements, $n$.
Given as input a set $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$, 
decide if there is a \definend{matching},\index{matching, three dimensional}
a set $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ with~$n$ elements that perfectly covers $X$, $Y$,
and~$Z$ in that no
two of the triples in $\hat{M}$ agree on 
their first coordinates, or their second, or their third.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ThreeDMatching>

\begin{example}
Let $X=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$, $Y=\set{\str{b},\str{c}}$,
and~$Z=\set{\str{a},\str{d}}$, so that $n=2$.
Below is a subset of $X\times Y\times Z$
(it actually equals $X\times Y\times Z$). % $\sequence{x,y,z}$.
\begin{equation*}
  M=\set{
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{d}},
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{d}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{d}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}
        }
\end{equation*}
% \begin{center} \small
% \begin{tabular}{cc}
%   \multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{a}$}
%   &\multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{d}$}    \\[-0.5ex]
%   \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
%     \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{b}$}
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{c}$}  \\ \cline{2-3}
%     \rule{0pt}{9pt} $x=\str{a}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{a}}$  \\
%     $x=\str{b}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{a}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{a}}$  \\
%   \end{tabular}
%   &\begin{tabular}{r|cc}
%     \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{b}$}
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{c}$}  \\ \cline{2-3}
%     \rule{0pt}{9pt}  $x=\str{a}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{d}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{d}}$  \\
%     $x=\str{b}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{d}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$  \\
%   \end{tabular}
% \end{tabular}
% \end{center}
The matching $\hat{M}=\set{\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}},
\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}}$
has $2$ elements. 
They disagree in their first coordinates,
and their second, and their third.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
\begin{credit}
  Sean McCulloch,  
  \url{https://npcomplete.owu.edu/2014/06/03/3-dimensional-matching/}
\end{credit}
Fix $n=4$ and consider $X = \set{1,2,3,4}$, $Y = \set{10,20,30,40}$, 
and $Z = \set{100,200,300,400}$, all four-element sets.
Also fix this subset of $X\times Y\times Z$.
\begin{align*}
  M &=\{\,
        \sequence{1,10,200}, 
        \sequence{1,20,300}, 
        \sequence{2,30,400}, 
        \sequence{3,10,400},   \\ 
        &\qquad\sequence{3,40,100}, 
        \sequence{3,40,200}, 
        \sequence{4,10,200},
        \sequence{4,20,300}
        \,\}
\end{align*}
A matching is 
$\hat{M}=\set{\sequence{1,20,300}, \sequence{2,30,400}, 
  \sequence{3,40,100}, \sequence{4,10,200}}$.
\end{example}

%<*prob:SubsetSum>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Subset Sum}}]\index{Subset Sum problem@\probname{Subset Sum} problem}  \label{prob:SubsetSumDecision}
Given a multiset of natural
numbers $S=\set{n_0,\,\ldots\, n_{k-1}}$ and a target $T\in\N$,
decide if a subset of~$S$ sums to the target.\footnotemark
\end{problem}\footnotetext{%
  Recall that in a multiset\index{multiset}
  repeats do not
  collapse, so the multiset $\set{1,2,2,3}$ is different than the 
  multiset $\set{1,2,3}$. 
  But a multiset is like a set in that the order of the elements
  is not significant, so the multiset $\set{1,2,2,3}$ is the same
  as the multiset $\set{1,2,3,2}$.
  In short, a multiset is an unordered list.}
%</prob:SubsetSum>


\begin{example}  \label{ex:SubsetSum}
Do some of the numbers
$\set{911,22,821,563,405,986,165,732}$ add to $T=1173$?
The answer is `yes';~one such collection is $\set{165, 986, 22}$.
% sage: [randint(1, 999) for i in range(8)]
% [911, 22, 821, 563, 405, 986, 165, 732]
% sage: 165+986+22
% 1173
% \begin{equation*}
% 911
% \quad 22
% \quad 821
% \quad 563
% \quad 405
% \quad 986
% \quad 165
% \quad 732
% \end{equation*}
%\end{example}

%\begin{example} 
In contrast,
no subset of
$\set{414, 495, 168, 468, 978, 186, 489, 345}$ adds to $T=2105$.
All of the multiset's 
numbers are multiples of three while the target is not.
An exhaustive search with a program also shows that no subset adds to $1173$,
although there doesn't seem to be a simple reason for that.
% sage: [3*randint(1, 333) for i in range(8)]
% [414, 495, 168, 468, 978, 186, 489, 345]
% sage: S = Subsets([414, 495, 168, 468, 978, 186, 489, 345], submultiset=True)
% sage: S
% SubMultiset of [414, 495, 168, 468, 978, 186, 489, 345]
% sage: T = [414, 495, 168, 468, 978, 186, 489, 345]
% sage: def sum_list(lst):
% ....:     tot = 0
% ....:     for i in lst:
% ....:         tot = tot+i
% ....:     return(tot)
% ....: 
% sage: for k in S.list():
% ....:     if (sum_list(k) == 1173):
% ....:         print("found: ",k)
% ....: 
% sage: 414+168+186
% 768
% sage: for k in S.list():
% ....:     if (sum_list(k) == 768):
% ....:         print("found: ",k)
% ....: 
% found:  [414, 168, 186]
\end{example}

%<*prob:Knapsack>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Knapsack}}]\index{Knapsack problem@\probname{Knapsack} problem}
Given a finite multiset $S$ whose elements~$s$
have a natural number weight~$w(s)$ and value~$v(s)$, and also given a
weight bound $B$ and a value target~$T\!$, find a submultiset
$\hat{S}\subseteq S$ whose elements have a total weight less than or equal
to the
bound and total value greater than or equal to the target.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Knapsack>

% The name of the problem is the best explaination of what it is about.

\begin{example}
Our knapsack can carry at most $B=10$ pounds.
Can we pack items with total worth at least~$T=100$?
\begin{center}\small 
\begin{tabular}{r|cccc}
  \tabulartext{Item}
    &$i_0$              &$i_1$ &$i_2$ &$i_3$  \\
  \cline{2-5}
  \rule{0pt}{9pt}  % scooch more room
  \tabulartext{Weight}  &$3$  &$4$  &$5$   &$6$  \\
  \tabulartext{Value}   &$50$ &$40$ &$10$  &$30$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
No.
The highest value that we can get without exceeding the weight $B=10$
is from taking $i_0$ and~$i_1$,
which does not meet the target.
% sage: D = [(3,50), (4,40), (5,10), (6,30)]
% sage: S = Subsets(D, submultiset=True)
% sage: S.list()
% [[],
%  [(3, 50)],
%  [(4, 40)],
%  [(5, 10)],
%  [(6, 30)],
%  [(3, 50), (4, 40)],
%  [(3, 50), (5, 10)],
%  [(3, 50), (6, 30)],
%  [(4, 40), (5, 10)],
%  [(4, 40), (6, 30)],
%  [(5, 10), (6, 30)],
%  [(3, 50), (4, 40), (5, 10)],
%  [(3, 50), (4, 40), (6, 30)],
%  [(3, 50), (5, 10), (6, 30)],
%  [(4, 40), (5, 10), (6, 30)],
%  [(3, 50), (4, 40), (5, 10), (6, 30)]]
% sage: def check_knapsack_limits(list_of_pairs):
% ....:     weight_total,value_total = 0,0
% ....:     for (w,v) in list_of_pairs:
% ....:         weight_total = weight_total+w
% ....:         value_total = value_total+v
% ....:     if (weight_total<=10):
% ....:         return value_total
% ....:     else:
% ....:         return -1
% sage: max_value = 0
% ....: for k in S.list():
% ....:     v = check_knapsack_limits(k)
% ....:     if v>max_value:
% ....:         max_value = v
% ....:         print("max_value=",max_value,"list: ",k)
% ....: 
% max_value= 50 list:  [(3, 50)]
% max_value= 90 list:  [(3, 50), (4, 40)]
\end{example}

%<*prob:Partition>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Partition}}]\index{Partition problem@\probname{Partition} problem}
Given a finite multiset~$A$ such that each element
has an associated natural number size $s(a)$,
decide if the set splits into two,
$\hat{A}$ and $A-\hat{A}$, so that the total of the sizes is the same, 
$\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}s(a)=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}s(a)$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Partition>

\begin{example}
The set
$A=\set{\str{I},\str{a},\str{my},\str{go},\str{rivers},\str{cat},\str{hotel},\str{comb}}$
has eight words.
The size of a word is the number of letters.
Then $\hat{A}=\set{\str{I},\str{go},\str{rivers},\str{cat}}$
and $A-\hat{A}=\set{\str{a},\str{my},\str{hotel},\str{comb}}$
gives $\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}s(a)=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}s(a)=12$.
\end{example}

% Recall that if we have an exponential relation $b^n=a$ then we say that
% the exponent~$n$ is the logarithm, base~$b$, of~$a$, written $\log_b(a)=n$.
% For instance, $2^7=128$ so we say that $\log_{2}(128)=\lg(128)=7$.

% Discrete logarithms are the discrete analogue of those.
% Fix a prime~$p$ and consider the numbers $G=\set{0,1,\ldots\, p-1}$.
% We can multiply two of these and then take the answer module~$p$.


% \begin{problem}[Discrete Logarithm]
% Let $p\in\N$i be a prime.
% Given a base $b\in\set{0,1,\ldots\,p-1}$ and
% a number $a\in\set{0,1,\ldots\,p-1}$, find an exponent~$n$ such that
% $b^n\equiv a\pmod{p}$.
% \end{problem}

% We can obviously brute force the problem by trying all exponents up to
% $p-1$. (Why suffices?)
% But that is slow.
% As far as we know, the Discrete Logarithm problem is
% very hard to solve quickly.

%<*prob:LinearProgramming>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Linear Programming}}]\index{Linear Programming problem@\probname{Linear Programming} problem}
\label{prob:LinearProgramming}
Optimize a linear function 
$F(x_0,\ldots x_n)=c_0x_0+\cdots+c_{n}x_n$ % (either maximize or minimize it),
subject to linear constraints, ones of the form 
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_{n}\leq b_i$ 
or
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_{n}\geq b_i$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:LinearProgramming>


%<*ex:LinearProgramming>
\begin{example}
Maximize $F(x_0,x_1)=x_0+2x_1$ subject to 
$4x_0+3x_1\leq 24$, $x_1\leq 4$, $x_0\geq 0$ and $x_1\geq 0$.
The shaded region has the points that satisfy all the inequalities;
these are said to be `feasible' points.
\begin{center}
   \includegraphics{\complexityproblemsdir problems44.pdf}
\end{center}
\noindent The level lines of~$F$ indicate that the maximum is 
at $(x_0,x_1)=(3,4)$.
\end{example}
%</ex:LinearProgramming>

%<*prob:Crossword>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Crossword}}]\index{Crossword@\probname{Crossword} problem}
Given an $n\times n$ grid and a set of $2n$-many strings, each
of length~$n$, decide if the
\citetext{words can be packed into the grid}{%
  The earliest known example is the Sator square,\index{Sator square} 
  five Latin words that pack into a grid.
  \begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=.75in]{complexity/pix/sator.jpg}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{tabular}{ccccc}
  S &A &T &O &R  \\
  A &R &E &P &O  \\
  T &E &N &E &T  \\
  O &P &E &R &A  \\
  R &O &T &A &S
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  % https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sator_Square
  % By M Disdero (Taken at Oppede, Luberon, France) [GFDL (http://www.gnu.org/copyleft/fdl.html) or CC BY-SA 3.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/3.0)], via Wikimedia Commons
  % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/71/Sator_Square_at_Opp%C3%A8de.jpg
  It appears in many places in the Roman Empire, often as graffiti.
  For instance, it
  was found in the ruins of Pompeii.
  Like many word game solutions it sacrifices comprehension for form but
  it is a perfectly grammatical sentence that translates as something like,
  ``The farmer Arepo works the wheel with effort.''
  % See https://www.academia.edu/5111444/_Magic_Squares_Alphabet_Jumbles_Riddles_and_more_The_culture_of_word-games_among_the_graffiti_of_Pompeii_in_The_Muse_at_Play._Riddles_and_Wordplay_in_Greek_and_Latin_Poetry_eds._J._Kwapisz_D._Petrain_and_M._Szymanski_Munich_De_Gruyter_2012_65-80
}.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Crossword>

\begin{example} % http://cgi.csc.liv.ac.uk/~ped/teachadmin/COMP202/annotated_np.html
Can we pack the words
\str{AGE}, \str{AGO}, \str{BEG}, \str{CAB}, \str{CAD}, and \str{DOG}
into a $3\times 3$ grid?

%<*prob:FifteenGame>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Fifteen Game}}]\index{Fifteen Game@\probname{Fifteen Game} problem}
Given an $n\times n$ grid holding tiles numbered $1$, \ldots\,~$n^2-1$,
and a blank,
find the minimum number of moves that will put the tile numbers into
ascending order.
A move consists of switching a tile with an adjacent blank.
\end{problem}
%</prob:FifteenGame>

\noindent This game became
\citetext{popular with $n=4$ as a toy}{%
  It was invented by Noyes Palmer Chapman, a postmaster in Canastota, New York.
  As early as 1874 he showed friends a precursor puzzle.
  By December~1879
  copies of the improved puzzle were circulating in the northeast
  and students in the American School for the Deaf and other
  started manufacturing it.
  They become popular as the ``Gem Puzzle.''
  Noyes Chapman had applied for a patent in February, 1880. 
  By that time the game had became a craze in the US,
  somewhat like Rubik's Cube a century later.
  It was also popular in Canada and Europe.
  See~\autocite{wiki:FifteenPuzzle}.}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics[width=0.225\textwidth]{complexity/pix/15game.png}
\end{center}

The final three problems about primes and divisibility
have an impeccable history.
No less an authority than Gauss said,
``The problem of distinguishing prime numbers from composite numbers
and of resolving the latter into their prime factors is known to be one
of the most important and useful in arithmetic.
It has engaged the industry and wisdom of ancient and modern geometers
to such an extent that it
would be superfluous to discuss the problem at length \ldots{}\sentencespace
Further, the dignity of the science itself
seems to require that every possible means be explored for the solution of a
problem so elegant and so celebrated.''

The three may be hard to tell apart at first glance. 
But as we understand them today, 
they differ in the
Big-$\bigOh$ behavior of the algorithms to solve them. 

%<*prob:Divisors>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Divisor}}]\index{Divisor@\probname{Divisor} problem}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, find a nontrivial divisor.\label{prob:Divisor}
\end{problem}
%</prob:Divisors>

\citetext{We know of no efficient algorithm to find divisors}{% https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integer_factorization
  An effort in 2009 to factor a $768$-bit number ($232$-digits) used
  hundreds of machines and took two years.
  The researchers estimated that a $1024$-bit number would take about
  a thousand times as long.}.\footnote{%
  No efficient algorithm is known, that is, on a non-quantum computer.}
However, as is so often the case, at this moment
we also have no proof that no such algorithm exists.\footnote{%
  % There is no proof despite centuries of ingenious attacks on the problem
  % by many of the brightest minds of the past, and of today.
  The presumed difficulty of this problem is at the heart of widely used
  algorithms in cryptography.}
Not all numbers of a given length are equally hard to factor.
The hardest numbers to find divisors of
are semiprimes,\index{semiprime}
products of two prime numbers.

%<*prob:PrimeFactorization>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Prime Factorization}}]\index{Prime Factorization problem@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}  \label{prob:Factorization}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, produce its decomposition into a product of primes.
\end{problem}
%</prob:PrimeFactorization>

\citetext{Factoring seems to be hard}{%
  Finding factors has for many years been thought hard.
  For instance, a number is called a Mersenne prime
  if it is a prime number of the form $2^n-1$.
  They are named after M~Mersenne, a French friar and important figure in
  the early sharing of scientific results,
  who studied them in the early 1600's.
  He observed that
  if $n$ is prime then $2^n-1$ may be prime, for instance with $n=3$,
  $n=7$, $n=31$, and $n=127$.
  He suspected that others of that form were also prime, in particular
  $n=67$.

  On \ymd{1903}{10}{31}{} F~N Cole,
  then Secretary of the American Mathematical Society,
  made a presentation at a math meeting.
  When introduced, he went to the chalkboard and in complete silence
  computed $2^{67}-1=147\,573\,952\,589\,676\,412\,927$.
  He then moved to the other side of the board, wrote
  $193\,707\,721$ times $761\,838\,257\,287$, and worked through the
  calculation, finally finding equality.
  When he was done Cole returned to his seat, having not uttered a word
  in the hour-long presentation.
  His audience gave him a standing ovation.

  Cole later said that finding the factors had been a significant effort,
  taking ``three years of Sundays.''}.
But what if you only want to know whether a number is prime
and don't care about its factors?

%<*prob:Composite>
\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Primality}}]\index{Primality problem@\probname{Primality} problem}\label{prob:Composite}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, determine if it is prime;
that is, decide if there are no numbers~$d$ that divide~$n$ and
such that $1<d<n$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Composite>

For many years the consensus among experts was that finding a
primality testing algorithm that was polytime 
in the number of digits of the input was very unlikely.
After all, for centuries, many of the smartest people in the world
had worked on composites
and primes, and none of them had produced a fast test.\footnote{%
  There are a number of probabilistic algorithms that are often
  used in practice that can test
  primality very quickly, with an extremely small chance of error.}

\margingraphic{0.28\textwidth}{complexity/pix/aks.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AKS_primality_test}{Nitin Saxena (b~1981), Neeraj Kayal (b~1979), Manindra Agrawal (b~1966)}}\index{Saxena, N!picture}\index{Kayal, N!picture}\index{Agrawal, M!picture} % http://www.horizons-2000.org/9.%20Active%20Projects/read/PRIMES%20is%20in%20P.htm; came from MAA focus?
However, in 2002 M~Agrawal, N~Kayal, and N~Saxena
produced such an algorithm, the AKS algorithm.\footnote{%
  At the time that they did most of this research, Kayal and Saxena were 
  undergraduates.}\index{AKS primality test}\index{primality}
Soon after their result appeared, refinements of their technique
lowered the running time to
$\bigOh(n^6)$,
where $n$ is the number of bits in the integer's binary representation.

This dramatically illustrates
that even though a problem is high profile
and many well-respected experts have worked on it without success,
nonetheless it could have a solution that is fast.
Although opinions of experts have value, they can be proved wrong.
People producing a result that gainsays established orthodoxy
has happened before and will happen again.
