\begin{exercises}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Each of the five
\citetext{Platonic solids}{%
  See \autocite{wiki:PlatonicSolid}.}
has a Hamiltonian circuit, 
\citetext{as shown}{%
  Some PDF readers cannot do opacity, so you may not see the entire
  Hamiltonian path.}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems31.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems32.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems33.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems38.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems36.pdf}
\end{center}
Hamilton used the fourth, the dodecahedron, for
his game.
Find a Hamiltonian circuit for the third and the fifth,
the octahedron and the icosahedron.
To make the connections easier to see, below we have expanded a face in the back
of each solid 
until we could squash the entire shape down into the plane
without any edge crossings.
\begin{center}
 \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems19.pdf}}
\hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems20.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
For the octahedron, the path 
$\sequence{v_0,v_1,v_5,v_4,v_3,v_2,v_0}$ will do.
For the icosahedron, one path is 
$\sequence{v_0,v_{10},v_{11},v_8,v_9,v_6,v_7,v_2,v_3,v_4,v_5,v_1,v_0}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
How many edges are there in a Hamiltonian path?
\begin{answer}\verified
The same as the number of vertices.
Traversing the Hamiltonian path means that there is one and only one edge 
leaving each vertex.   
So if a graph has $n$ vertices then a Hamiltonian
path has the same number of edges,~$n$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
    Private communication from P~Rombach.
\end{credit}
On some \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem graphs we can
change the edge weights to ensure that an edge is used but on
some we cannot.  
\begin{exparts}
\item 
A circuit for a \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem instance 
is a Hamiltonian path.
Produce an undirected graph without loops
on which there is at least one Hamiltonian circuit, but containing an edge that 
belongs to no such circuit.
\item 
Consider an undirected graph with an edge~$e$ through which at 
least one Hamiltonian circuit runs.  
Fix edge weights for all other edges.
Show that there is an edge weight for $e$ such that any solution for
the \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem includes~$e$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This graph has a Hamiltonian circuit, around the outside. 
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems53.pdf}
\end{center}
To include the vertices $v_0$ and~$v_3$, any 
circuit must include those four outside edges.
In every Hamiltonian circuit the number of edges equals the number
of vertices
and hence no Hamiltonian circuit can contain the edge $v_1v_2$, because 
that would make five edges.
\item
Assume that all the edge weights are positive
(if the given graph does not have that then to all weights, add a number large
enough to make them all positive). 
To the edge~$e$ 
assign this weight.
\begin{equation*}
  -(\text{number of vertices})\cdot(\text{max weight})-1
\end{equation*}
% (Or, we could assign the weight~$1$ and raise the other edge weights).
Every Hamiltonian path has the same number of edges, 
specifically the number of edges equals the number of vertices.
So a path through~$e$ has a negative total weight, and 
this is clearly the only way to get a negative total weight,
so the Hamiltonian path solving the  \probname{Traveling Salesman}
must include~$e$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Find the shortest path in this graph
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems28.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item from $q_2$ to~$q_7$,
\item from $q_0$ to~$q_8$,
\item from $q_8$ to~$q_0$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
We can do these by eye.
\begin{exparts}
\item The shortest is $q_2$ to~$q_1$ to~$q_7$, which has length~$14$.
\item The shortest is $q_0$ to~$q_3$ to~$q_4$ to~$q_8$, of length~$23$.
\item There is no path between these two vertices.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a planar map that requires four colors.
\begin{answer}\verified
This is the natural one.
Every region borders all three others so three colors does not suffice. 
\begin{center}
 \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems21.pdf}}
\end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:FiveColors}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Some countries have separated components;
for instance, where K\"{o}nigsberg used to be is today 
a part of Russia called the Kaliningrad Oblast that is unconnected to rest
of the country. 
The \probname{Four Color} problem disallows such countries.
Give a planar map where countries have non-contiguous components that requires
five colors.
\item The problem also takes `adjacent' to mean sharing a border that is an 
interval, not just a point.
Give a planar map that without that restriction 
would require five colors.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This map shows five countries. 
They are not contiguous; for instance, country~$A$ has five separate
components
(the first component contains $B$, $C$, $D$, and~$E$).
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems22.pdf}
\end{center}
Because of the portion in the upper left, 
country~$A$ must be a different color than any of the others.
The other portions similarly 
give that all the other countries are different colors than each other.
In total there are five countries, each a different color than all the
others.
\item Make a circle divided into five wedges.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:CourseScheduling}
The \definend{\probname{Course Scheduling}}\index{Course Scheduling@\probname{Course Scheduling} problem}  
problem 
starts with a list of students and the 
classes that they wish to take.
If a student wishes to take two classes then those two will not be scheduled 
to meet at the same time.
The goal is to find the minimum number of time slots needed
to schedule the classes (scheduling every class at a 
different time would give no conflicts).
The school below offers Astronomy, Biology, 
Computing, Drama, English, French, Geography, History, and~Italian.
After students sign up,
the graph shows overlaps.
For instance
Astronomy and Biology share at least one student, while Biology and Drama
do not.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs09.pdf}
  % \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs10.pdf}
\end{center}
What is the minimum number of class times that we must use?  
In graph coloring terms, we declare that if classes meet at the same time
then they are the same color and we ask for the minimum number of
colors needed so that no two same-colored vertices share an edge.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Show that no three-coloring suffices.
\item Produce a four-coloring.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
For reference here is the graph.
\begin{display}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs09.pdf}
\end{display}
\begin{exparts}
\item
Suppose that a three-coloring suffices.
The  
three vertices $A$, $C$, and~$D$ form a triangle so they must be different
colors.
Since $CDF$ also is a triangle, we conclude that $A$ and $F$ are the same
colors. 
So far we have:
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D}$.

Next, consider triangle $DFI$. It's presence gives that $C$ and~$I$ are the same
colors.
So we have 
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D}$.
Then triangle $EFI$ leads to 
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D,E}$.

Finally, triangle $CFG$ gives that $D$ and~$G$ are the same color, giving
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D,E,G}$.
But that's impossible because $G$ is connected to~$E$.
\item
This is a four-coloring.
\begin{display}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs10.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{l}
    $C_1=\set{A,F}$  \\
    $C_2=\set{B,C,E}$  \\
    $C_3=\set{D,G,H}$  \\
    $C_4=\set{I}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Decide if each formula is satisfiable.
\begin{exparts}
\item $(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$
\item $(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
A formula is satisfiable if there is a $\true$ in the truth table's 
final column.
\begin{exparts}
\item The formula $(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$ is satisfiable. 
\begin{display} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg Q$ &$\neg Q\wedge R$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$      
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
\item The formula $(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$
  is not satisfiable. 
\begin{display} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{cc|ccccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$ &$P\to Q$ &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg P$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P$ &$\neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  &$(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
% sage: import sage.logic.propcalc as propcalc
% sage: s = propcalc.formula("(p&q)|(~q&r)")
% sage: s.truthtable()
% p      q      r      value
% False  False  False  False  
% False  False  True   True   
% False  True   False  False  
% False  True   True   False  
% True   False  False  False  
% True   False  True   True   
% True   True   False  True   
% True   True   True   True   
% sage: s = propcalc.formula("(p->q)&~((p&q)|~p)")
% sage: s.truthtable()
% p      q      value
% False  False  False  
% False  True   False  
% True   False  False  
% True   True   False  

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Instead of going from the propositional logic statement to its truth table,
we can specify a behavior in a 
truth table and then produce a statement with that behavior. 
That statement can be in Conjunctive Normal form.\index{CNF}\index{Propositional logic!Conjunctive Normal form}
Appendix~\ref{sec:PropLogic} says more but here we give an algorithm
to find the statement.
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R$}  &$f(P,Q,R)$  \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$  \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$  \\[0.5ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$  \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$        
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item We will target the lines whose final column is $\false$.
  The first is the $\false$-$\true$-$\false$ line.
  With it associate the clause $P\vee\neg Q\vee R$,
  where for $\false$ take the variable name 
  and for $\true$ take the negation of the
  name, and join them with $\vee$'s.
  For each $\false$ line, produce such a clause that targets it. 
\item Argue that each clause is $\false$ only on its targeted line.
\item Produce the Boolean expression that consists of the 
  joins those clauses with $\wedge$'s (it is the conjunction of the
  clauses, which is where this form gets its name).
  Argue that that it must have the desired truth table.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item There are four lines where the input causes the expression
to evaluate to~$\false\!$.
\begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|l}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Line}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Clause}}  \\ \hline
    $\false$-$\true$-$\false$ &$P\vee\neg Q\vee R$  \\
    $\false$-$\true$-$\true$  &$P\vee\neg Q\vee \neg R$  \\
    $\true$-$\false$-$\true$  &$\neg P\vee Q\vee \neg R$  \\
    $\true$-$\true$-$\true$   &$\neg P\vee\neg Q\vee \neg R$ 
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
\item Any clause where the elements are joined by $\vee$'s will evaluate
to $\true$ unless every single element is $\false\!$.
These clauses are constructed to make every element
false on the targeted line.
On any other line at least one element is true.
\item The Conjunctive Normal form is this.
\begin{equation*}
  (P\vee\neg Q\vee R)
  \wedge(P\vee\neg Q\vee \neg R)
  \wedge(\neg P\vee Q\vee \neg R)
  \wedge(\neg P\vee\neg Q\vee \neg R)    
\end{equation*}
This statement evaluates to $\false$ if and only if any of its constituent
clauses is~$\false\!$.
By the prior item, that happens if and only if the input is a targeted line. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% sage: s.truthtable()
% p      q      r      value
% False  False  False  True   
% False  False  True   True   
% False  True   False  False  
% False  True   True   False  
% True   False  False  True   
% True   False  True   False  
% True   True   False  True   
% True   True   True   False  
% sage: s.convert_cnf_table()
% sage: s
% (p|~q|r)&(p|~q|~r)&(~p|q|~r)&(~p|~q|~r)
% sage: s.truthtable()
% p      q      r      value
% False  False  False  True   
% False  False  True   True   
% False  True   False  False  
% False  True   True   False  
% True   False  False  True   
% True   False  True   False  
% True   True   False  True   
% True   True   True   False  

\begin{exercise}
If a Boolean expression is satisfiable, does that imply that its
negation is not satisfiable?
\begin{answer}\verified
It does not imply that.  
For an example, let the Boolean expression be $P\vee Q$.
\begin{display}
\begin{tabular}{cc|cc}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{$P$} 
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$Q$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$P\vee Q$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\neg(P\vee Q)$} \\
  \hline
  $F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ \\
  $F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\
  $T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ \\
  $T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
It is satisfiable by taking both variables to be~$T$.
Its negation is also satisfiable, by taking both variables to be~$F$ 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Some authors define the \probname{Satisfiability}\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem} 
problem as:~given a finite set of propositional logic statements,
not just one statement,
find if there is a single input tuple $\sequence{b_0,\ldots\, b_{j-1}}$,
where each $b_i$ is either $\true$ or $\false$, that satisfies them all.
Show that this is equivalent to the definition given in \zcref{prob:SAT}.
\begin{answer}\verified
A finite set of propositional logic statements $\set{S_0,\ldots S_{k-1}}$
is satisfiable if and only if the single statement
$S_0\wedge S_1\ldots \wedge S_{k-1}$ is satisfiable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  % based on http://www.herinst.org/BusinessManagedDemocracy/introduction/interlocking.html
This shows interlocking corporate directorships.
The vertices are corporations and 
they are connected if they share a member of their Board of Directors
(the data is from 2004).
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems27.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Is there a path from AT\&T to Ford Motor?
\item Can you get from Haliburton to Ford Motor?
\item Can you get from Caterpillar to Ford Motor?
\item JP Morgan to Ford Motor?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item AT\&T connects to Citigroup, which connects to Ford Motor.
\item Haliburton connects to Citigroup, which connects to Ford Motor.
\item There is no connection between Caterpillar and Ford Motor.
\item Also no connection.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
A popular game extends the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem 
by counting the degrees of separation.
Below is a portion of the movie connection graph, 
where actors are connected if they have ever been 
together in a movie. %\footnote{%
%   Information from \protect\url{https://oracleofbacon.org/}}
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems24.pdf}
\end{center}
A person's
\definend{Bacon number} is the number of edges 
connecting them to Bacon, or infinity if they are not connected.
The game \citetext{\definend{\textit{Six Degrees of Kevin Bacon}}}{%
  One night, three college friends,
  Brian Turtle, Mike Ginelli, and Craig Fass,
  were watching movies. 
  \textit{Footloose} was followed by \textit{Quicksilver}, 
  and between was a commercial for a third Kevin Bacon movie. 
  It seemed like Kevin Bacon was in everything!\index{Bacon, Kevin} 
  This prompted the question of whether Bacon had ever worked with 
  De Niro? 
  The answer at that time was no, but De Niro was in 
  \textit{The Untouchables} with Kevin Costner, who was in 
  \textit{JFK} with Bacon. 
  The game was born.
  It became popular when they wrote to Jon Stewart about it and appeared
  on his show.
  (From \autocite{blog:Blanda}.)
  See  \protect\url{https://oracleofbacon.org/}.} 
asks:~is everyone connected to Kevin Bacon by at most six movies?
\begin{exparts}
\item Based on this graph, what is Elvis's Bacon number?
\item John Kennedy's (no, it is not that John Kennedy)?
\item Bacon's?
\item How many movies separate me from Meryl Streep?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts*}
\item 2
\item 2
\item 0
\item 4 (I was only in deleted scenes.  But all this is just for fun.)
\end{exparts*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The 
\probname{Vertex Cover}\index{Vertex Cover problem@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem} 
problem inputs a graph
$\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}}$ and a bound~$B\in\N$,
and asks if there is a subset~$S$ of at most $B$~vertices 
such that for each edge $e\in\mathcal{E}$ 
at least one endpoint is an element of~$S$.
The \definend{\probname{Independent Set}}\index{Independent Set problem@\probname{Independent Set} problem}
problem inputs a graph and a 
bound~$\hat{B}\in\N$ (a lower bound this time)
and asks if there is a subset~$\hat{S}$ with at least $\hat{B}$~vertices 
such that for each edge at most one endpoint is in~$\hat{S}$.
The two are related.
\begin{exparts}
\item
In this six-vertex graph
find a vertex cover~$S$ with $B=2$ elements.
Find an independent set with $\hat{B}=4$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems29.pdf}
\end{center}
\item
In this six-vertex graph
find a vertex cover with $B=3$ elements,
and an independent set with $\hat{B}=3$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems30.pdf}
\end{center} 
\item
Find a vertex cover~$S$ with $B=4$ elements in this ten-vertex graph.
Find an independent set~$\hat{S}$ with $\hat{B}=6$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity22.pdf}
\end{center}
\item Prove that $S$ is a vertex cover if and only if
its complement~$\hat{S}=\mathcal{N}-S$ is an independent set.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item A vertex cover with $B=2$ elements is $S=\set{v_1,v_3}$.
An independent set with $\hat{B}=4$ elements is 
$\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_4,q_5}$.
\item A vertex cover with $B=3$ elements is $S=\set{v_1,v_2,v_4}$.
An independent set with $\hat{B}=3$ elements is 
$\hat{S}=\set{v_0,v_3,v_5}$.
\item A vertex cover with $B=4$ elements is $S=\set{v_2,v_5,v_8,v_9}$.
An independent set with $\hat{B}=6$ elements is 
  $\hat{S}=\set{v_0,v_1,v_3,v_4,v_6,v_7}$.
\item For any edge, if it has at least one endpoint in
$S$ then it has at most one endpoint in the complement $\hat{S}=\mathcal{N}-S$.
Conversely, if an edge has at most one endpoint in~$\hat{S}$ 
then it has at least one endpoint in the complement~$S=\mathcal{N}-\hat{S}$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What shape is a $3$-clique?
A $2$-clique?
\begin{answer}\verified
A $3$-clique is a triangle.
A $2$-clique is an edge.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
How many edges does a $k$-clique have?
\begin{answer}\verified
A $k$-clique has $k(k-1)/2$ edges, one for each combination of two vertices.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  https://stackoverflow.com/q/55051253  
\end{credit}
\zcref{ex:MaxCut} exhibits a cut set with six members, as shown on the left.  
But on the right there are eight cut edges; what's wrong with it?
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems47.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems49.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The one on the right does not partition the vertices into two disjoint sets,
  it partitions into five sets: $\set{v_0}$, $\set{v_1}$, $\set{v_2,v_3}$,
  $\set{v_4}$, and $\set{v_5}$.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Find the maximum cut set for this graph.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems50.pdf}}  
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The graph cut
  $\set{\set{v_0,v_1,v_4,v_5}, \set{v_2,v_3}}$
\begin{display}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems51.pdf}}  
\end{display}
gives a cut set with eight elements,
$\set{v_0v_2, v_0v_3, v_1v_2, v_1v_3, v_2v_5, v_3v_4, v_3v_5}$.  
This graph cut is maximal because its cut set contains all of the edges.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Jan Verschelde,
  \url{http://homepages.math.uic.edu/~jan/mcs401/partitioning.pdf}
\end{credit}
A college department has instructors $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$, and $E$ who
will each teach one class section.
The sections are $0$, $1$, $2$, $3$, and~$4$. 
The time slots for the available room are $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, $\delta$,
and~$\varepsilon$.
This shows which instructors can teach which classes,
and which classes can run in which slots.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems39.pdf}
\end{center}
For example, instructor~$A$ can only teach courses $1$, $2$, and~$3$.
And, course~$0$ can only run in slots~$\alpha$ or~$\delta$.
Verify that this is an instance of the 
\probname{Three-dimensional Matching} problem and find a match.
\begin{answer}\verified
The definition of a \probname{Three-dimensional Matching} problem
requires three sets, here the instructors, the courses, and the time slots, 
and that they all have the same size, here five.
We must be given as input a set $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$,
and here that set consists of all triples that the graph shows
as a possibility, so $M=\set{(A,1,\beta), (A,1,\varepsilon), (A,2,\gamma), \ldots}$.
We must 
decide if there is a set $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ 
containing~$n$ elements (here, five elements) such that no
two of the triples in $\hat{M}$ agree on 
their first coordinates, or their second or third coordinates either.
That fits this problem because we cannot have the same instructor
teaching two sections, or two instructors teaching the same section,
or two sections using the room during the same time slot.

A suitable matching is 
$\hat{M}=\set{
              \sequence{A,2,\varepsilon},
              \sequence{B,0,\alpha},
              \sequence{C,3,\gamma},
              \sequence{D,1,\beta},
              \sequence{E,4,\delta}
              }$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider \probname{Three Dimensional Matching}, \zcref{Prob:ThreeDMatching}.
Let
$X=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$, $Y=\set{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$,
and~$Z=\set{\str{a},\str{d},\str{e}}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item List the elements of $M=X\times Y\times Z$.
\item Is there a three element subset $\hat{M}$ whose triples
have the property that no two entries agree on any coordinate?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
The set $M=X\times Y\times Z$ contains these twenty seven 
triples $\sequence{x,y,z}$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
  \multicolumn{1}{l}{\underline{$z=\str{a}$}:}
  &\multicolumn{1}{l}{\underline{$z=\str{d}$}:}
  &\multicolumn{1}{l}{\underline{$z=\str{e}$}:}    \\[0.5ex]
  \begin{tabular}{ccc}
    $\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{d},\str{a}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{d},\str{a}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{c},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{c},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{a}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  &\begin{tabular}{ccc}
    $\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{d},\str{d}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{d},\str{d}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{c},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{c},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{d}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  &\begin{tabular}{ccc}
    $\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{d},\str{e}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{d},\str{e}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{c},\str{b},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{c},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{e}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}  
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item
  One correct answer is $\hat{M}=\set{
                       \sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}},
                       \sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}},
                       \sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{e}}
                       }$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
% sage: M = []
% sage: for x in ['a', 'b', 'c']:
% ....:     for y in ['b', 'c', 'd']:
% ....:         for z in ['a', 'd', 'e']:

% ....:

% \begin{exercise}
% In \zcref{ex:broadcast} the broadcast takes four steps.
% Can it be done in fewer?
% \end{exercise}
% \begin{answer}
% No, it cannot be done in three steps.
% The longest path is to~$v_{10}$, of three edges.
% Even if we start on that path by broadcasting to~$v_4$ at the
% first stage, $v_0$ still must initiate broadcasts to $v_6$ and~$v_3$, 
% each of which will take two stages. 
% But $v_0$ can only initiate one at a time.
% \end{answer}

\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Subset Sum} instance with 
$S=\set{21, 33, 49, 42, 19}$
and target~$T=114$ has no solution.
Verify that by brute force, by checking every possible combination.

\begin{exercise}
This \probname{Knapsack} instance has no solution when the weight bound is 
$B=73$ and the value target is $T=140$.
\begin{center}\small 
\begin{tabular}{r|ccccc}
  \tabulartext{Item}
    &\tabulartext{a} &\tabulartext{b} &\tabulartext{c} &\tabulartext{d} &\tabulartext{e}  \\
  \cline{2-6}
  \rule{0pt}{9pt}  % scooch more room
  \tabulartext{Weight}  &$21$ &$33$ &$49$  &$42$  &$19$  \\
  \tabulartext{Value}   &$50$ &$48$ &$34$  &$44$  &$40$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Verify that by brute force, by checking every possible packing attempt.
% (Probably the fastest way would be to write a program.)
\begin{answer}\verified
\figureref{fig:KnapsackProblemExercise} shows the result of writing
a small script.

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:ElectoralCollegeQuestion}
Using the data in \zcref{ex:ElectoralCollege}, decide if there
could be a tie in the 2024 Electoral College.
\begin{answer}\verified
There are a total of $538$ electoral votes.
A $269$-$269$ tie is easy to concoct.
For example, the electoral votes of CA, TX, FL, NY, IL, PA, 
OH, GA, MI, NC, VA, and VT add to $269$.
\end{answer}
% sage: 55+38+30+28+19+19+17+16+15+16+13+3
% 269
\end{exercise}

and $97$.

\begin{exercise}
Decide if each is prime.
\begin{exparts}
\item $5\,477$
\item $6\,165$
\item $6\,863$
\item $4\,207$
\item $7\,689$ 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Yes, it is prime.
\item No, it is not prime. 
Besides the trivial ones, the divisors of $6\,165$ are
$3$, $5$, $9$, $15$, $45$, $137$, $411$, $685$, $1\,233$, and~$2\,055$.
\item Yes.
\item No.
Besides itself and~$1$, this number is also divisible by 
$7$ and~$601$.
\item No.
Besides the trivial divisors of itself and~$1$, this number also has
the divisors $3$, $11$, $33$, $233$, $699$, and~$2\,563$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
% sage: P = Primes()
% sage: 5477 in P
% True
% sage: 6165 in P
% False
% sage: 6823 in P
% True
% sage: 4207 in P
% False
% sage: 7689 in P
% False
% sage: divisors(6165)
% [1, 3, 5, 9, 15, 45, 137, 411, 685, 1233, 2055, 6165]
% sage: divisors(4207)
% [1, 7, 601, 4207]
% sage: divisors(7689)
% [1, 3, 11, 33, 233, 699, 2563, 7689]

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Find a divisor of each that is nontrivial 
(meaning not $1$ or the number itself), if one exists.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $31\,221$
\item $52\,424$
\item $9\,600$
\item $4\,331$
\item $877$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item One is $3$.
  The list of nontrivial divisors is 
  $3$, $9$, $3\,469$, and $10\,407$.
\item One is $2$.
  The list of nontrivial ones is 
  $2$, $4$, $8$, $6\,553$, $13\,106$, and $26\,212$.
\item One is $2$.
  The list of nontrivial divisors is
  $2$, $3$, $4$, $5$, $6$, $8$,
  $10$, $12$, $15$, $16$, $20$,
  $24$, $25$, $30$, $32$, $40$,
  $48$, $50$, $60$, $64$, $75$,
  $80$, $96$, $100$, $120$, $128$,
  $150$, $160$, $192$, $200$, $240$,
  $300$, $320$, $384$, $400$, $480$,
  $600$, $640$, $800$, $960$, $1\,200$,
  $1\,600$, $1\,920$, $2\,400$, $3\,200$, and $4\,800$.
\item One is $61$.
  The list of nontrivial divisors is $61$, $71$.
\item The only ones are $1$ and~$877$.
  It is prime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
