\begin{exercise}
Your friend asks, 
``Doesn't the polytime solution of \probname{Primality} automatically give
us one for \probname{Divisor}?
Just take the divisor from the first problem's solution 
and use it to solve the second problem.''
Help them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
The AKS algorithm that solves \probname{Primality}
answers `yes' or `no' without finding divisors.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \recommended
% \begin{exercise}
% Solve \zcref{ex:SubsetSum}.
% \begin{answer}
% The answer is `yes' since $22+986+165=1173$.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{problem miscellany|)}







% =======================================================
\section{Problems, algorithms, and programs} \label{sec:ProbsAlgosPrograms}
Now, with many examples in hand,
we will briefly reflect on problems, and their solutions.
This discussion will be on an intuitive level only\Dash indeed, 
many of these things have no widely accepted
precise definition.

%<*discussion:Problem>
A problem is a job, a task.\index{problem}
Most of the ones we will consider are a
\citetext{uniform family of tasks}{From \autocite{Jones}.}, 
with infinitely many instances. 
For a sense of `family', 
contrast the problem of finding the shortest path between 
Los Angeles and New York with the 
general \probname{Shortest Path} problem. 
The first is an instance of the second, which inputs a weighted graph and two
vertices and so is a collection of related problems.
We are more likely to talk about the family,
because any conclusion about it 
subsumes the instances.\footnote{There are interesting problems
  with only one task, such as
  computing the digits of $\pi$.}
In the Theory of Computation 
we are focused on problems that can be solved with a mechanism,
including how efficiently that can be done.\footnote{%
  We continue to be interested to learn 
  that a problem cannot be solved mechanically at all.}
%</discussion:Problem>

%<*discussion:Algorithm>
An algorithm is an effective way to solve a 
problem.\footnote{% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/31953
  \citetext{There is no widely-accepted formal definition of `algorithm'}{%
    This discussion derives from  \autocite{pseudonym}.}.
  Whatever it is, 
  it fits between `mathematical function' and `computer program'.
  For example, a `sort' routine takes in a set of 
  items and returns the sorted sequence.
  This task, this input-output behavior, could be accomplished using different 
  algorithms:~merge sort, heap sort, etc.
  % In turn, each algorithm could be implemented by many programs,
  % written in different languages and for different platforms.
  So the best handle that we have is informal\Dash an `algorithm' is
  an equivalence class of programs (i.e., Turing machines),
  where two programs are equivalent
  if they do a task in essentially the same way, whatever ``essentially'' 
  means.\index{algorithm!definition}
  % Any two programs which implement the same algorithm must compute the same
  % function, but the converse is not true.
  % Similarly, there is an equivalence class between algorithms, where two
  % algorithms are equivalent if they compute the same mathematical function.  
  % The hard part in all this is trying to capture what we mean by
  % ``essentially the same thing.''
  % There are some things that we obviously should include.
  % For example, fix a program that does a merge sort on sequences of strings
  % and that uses \str{x} for a variable but not \str{y}.
  % The program that you get by simultaneously replacing all the
  % \str{x}'s with \str{y}'s is essentially the same.
  % And, there are some things that we may want not to include; an
  % operating system kernel is a single program but implements many
  % algorithms.
  % But in any event, giving a completely satisfactory
  % definition of `algorithm' is an open problem.
}\index{algorithm}
An algorithm is not a program,
although it should be described in a way that is 
detailed enough that implementing it is
routine for an experienced professional.
That description should suffice to analyze its
Big~$\bigOh$ behavior.

One subtle point about algorithms 
is that while they are abstractions, they are nonetheless based on
a computing model.
An algorithm that is based on a Turing machine model
for adding one to an input 
would be very different than
an algorithm to do the same task on a model that is like
an everyday computer.\index{algorithm!reliance on model}
%</discussion:Algorithm>

\margingraphic*{0.25\textwidth}{complexity/pix/science_united.png}
An example of an unusual computing model that an algorithm could target
is distributed computation.\index{distributed computation}\index{computation!distributed}\index{Science United}
For instance, Science United is a way for anyone with a computer and 
an Internet connection 
to help scientific projects by donating computing time. 
These projects do research in astronomy, physics, biomedicine, 
mathematics, and environmental science.
Contributors install a free program that runs jobs 
in the background.
This is massively parallel computation.\footnote{%
  There are now coming up on a million volunteers offering computing time.
  To join them, visit \protect\url{https://scienceunited.org/}.}

%<*discussion:Program>
A program\index{program} is an implementation of an algorithm,
expressed in a formal computer language and typically 
designed to be executed on a specific computing platform.
%</discussion:Program>

Here is an illustration of the differences between the problems, algorithms, and 
programs.
We've discussed the
\probname{Primality} problem,\index{Primality problem@\probname{Primality} problem}
of deciding whether a number is prime.
One algorithm is:~given an input~$n>1$,
successively try every $k\in\open{1}{n}$ to see whether it divides~$n$.
We could implement that algorithm with a program written in Racket.




\subsection{Problem types}
We have already seen
\definend{function problems}.\index{function problem}\index{problem!function}
These have  
a single output for each input. 
A example is the 
\probname{Prime Factorization} problem,\index{Prime Factorization problem@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}
which takes in a natural number and returns its prime decomposition.
Another example is the problem of finding the greatest common divisor, 
where the input is a pair of natural numbers
and the output is a natural number.

Another problem type 
is the 
\definend{optimization problem}.\index{optimization problem}\index{problem!optimization}
These ask for a solution that is best according to some metric.
The \probname{Shortest Path} problem\index{Shortest Path problem@\probname{Shortest Path} problem}
is one, as is \probname{Linear Programming}.\index{Linear Programming problem@\probname{Linear Programming} problem}

A perhaps less familiar problem type is the
\definend{search problem}.\index{search problem}\index{problem!search}
For these, there may be many
solutions and 
the algorithm can stop when it has found any one.
An example inputs a Propositional
Logic statement and outputs any truth table line
witnessing that the statement is satisfiable.
A second is the problem that 
inputs a weighted graph, two vertices, and a bound $B\in\R$, and
finds a path between the vertices that costs less than the bound. 
Another example is that of finding a $k$-coloring for a graph.
Still another is the \probname{Knapsack} problem.\index{Knapsack problem@\probname{Knapsack} problem} 
% and the
% \probname{Travelling Salesman} problem.\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem}
In all of these, we want to find if there is a way to solve the problem, 
such as a way to pack the knapsack, and if we exhibit at least one then
we are done.

A  
\definend{decision problem}\index{decision problem}\index{problem!decision}
is one with a `Yes' or `No' answer.\footnote{%
  Although a decision problem calls for producing a function of a kind,
  a Boolean function,
  they are important enough to be a separate category.
  } 
The first problem that we saw, the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem},\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}}
is one of these.\footnote{%
  Recall that the word is German for ``decision problem.''
  It asks for an algorithm to decide, given a mathematical statement,
  whether that statement is true.}
We have also seen decision problems 
in conjunction with the \HP{}, such as the problem of
determining, given an index~$e$, whether there is an input~$y$
such that $\TMfcn_e(y)=7$.
In this chapter we saw the \probname{Primality} problem,\index{Primality problem@\probname{Primality} problem}
the problem of deciding whether a given natural number is prime,
as well as the 
% \probname{Clique} problem,\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem}
% the \probname{Partition} problem,\index{Partition problem@\probname{Partition} problem} 
% or the
\probname{Subset Sum} problem.\index{Subset Sum problem@\probname{Subset Sum} problem}

Often a decision problem is expressed as a
\definend{language decision problem}.\index{language decision problem}\index{problem!language decision}\index{language!decision problem}
% or \definend{language recognition problem},\index{language recognition problem}\index{problem!language recognition}
We are given a language and asked for an algorithm to
decide whether or not an input is a member of that language.
We will see many examples later in this chapter.
One example:~we can express the task
of deciding the primality of a natural number, 
the \probname{Primality} problem,\index{Primality problem@\probname{Primality} problem}
as that of producing an algorithm that decided membership
in the set of bitstrings
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is the 
    binary representation of a prime $n\in\N$}}$.
Another example is that we can express the task of finding the shortest path
between two vertices in a graph as that of finding an algorithm
to determine membership in
$\lang_{B}=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,w}\suchthat
          \text{$\mathcal{G}$ has a path from $v$ to $w$ of weight no more than~$B$}}$, where $B\in\N$ is a parameter.

We are using `decision' in the sense of \zcref{def:MachineDecidesLanguage} from the Languages chapter, on
page~\pageref{discuss:LanguageDecidesRecognizes}.
As a convenience we repeat that here, specialized to Turing machines.
% \begin{definition} \label{def:langDecidedByTM}
A language is 
\definend{decided} by a Turing machine,\index{Turing machine!language decided}\index{language!accepted by Turing machine}\index{decided language}\index{language!decided}
or is \definend{Turing machine decidable},\index{Turing machine!decidable}\index{decidable}
if that machine computes the characteristic function of the language. 
Restated, the Turing machine $\TM$ decides the language $\lang$ if
$\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)=\charfcn{\lang}(\sigma)$, so that
$\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)=1$ when $\sigma\in\lang$ and
$\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)=0$ otherwise.
In particular, the language decider $\TM$ halts for all inputs.
The language is 
\definend{recognized}\index{Turing machine!language recognized}\index{language!recognized by Turing machine}\index{recognized language!of a Turing machine}\index{language!recognized}
by the Turing machine~$\TM$
when for each input~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\!$, if $\sigma\in\lang$ 
then $\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)=1$,
while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then either $\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)\diverges$
or else $\TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)\neq 1$.
% \end{definition}

Distinctions between problem types can be fuzzy.
If we want the sthortest path between two graph vertices,
it may be that two are tied and we are content to give either one.
So this optimization problem has some elements of a search.

In addition, often if we have a task then we could
describe it with more than one problem type.
Consider the task of determining the evenness of a natural number. 
We could express it as the function problem `given~$n$, return $1$ if it
is even and $0$ if it is odd'
We could also express it as the language decision problem
of determining membership in $\lang=\set{2k\suchthat k\in\N}$. 

When we have a choice of problem types,
\citetext{we prefer language decision problems}{%
  Because of this,
  some authors modify the definition of a Turing machine
  to have it come with a subset of accepting states.
  Such a machine solves a problem
  if it halts on all input strings,
  and when it halts it is in an accepting state exactly when that
  string is in the language.
}.
It is our \citetext{default interpretation of `problem'}{%
  Not every computational problem is naturally expressible as a 
  language decision problem 
  Consider the task of sorting the characters of strings
  into ascending order.
  We could try to express it as the language of sorted strings
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ is sorted}}$. 
  But recognizing a correctly-sorted string  
  does not require that we find a good way 
  to sort an unsorted input.
  Another thought is to consider the language of pairs
  $\sequence{\sigma,p}$ where $p$ is a permutation of the numbers
  $0,\ldots \lh{\sigma}-1$ that brings the string into ascending order.
  Here also the formulation 
  seems to not capture the sorting problem, in that recognizing a
  correct permutation feels different than generating one from scratch.} 
and we will focus on them in the rest of the book.
In addition, we will be sloppy about the distinction between the
decision problem for a language and the language itself; for instance, we
may write, ``Let $\prob$ be a problem \ldots''

\begin{fig}[fig:LangsBlob]{%
   \protect\citetext{Both of these diagrams  
      show the collection of languages}{%
      One misleading aspect of this picture is that there are 
      uncountably many languages but only countably many 
      Turing machines, and hence only countably many computable 
      or computably enumerable languages.
      Shown to scale, the computably enumerable area of the blob would be 
      an infinitesimally small speck at the very bottom.
      Such a picture would not display the features that we want to
      illustrate, so these drawings take a graphical license.    
    } $\powerset(\kleenestar{\B})$,
    which we often call the `problems'. 
    On the left, showing points
    emphasizes that this is a collection of separate 
    sets, not a continuum.
    \par\hspace{1em}
    Both diagrams are arranged with quickly-solvable problems at the bottom.
    But there is a catch:~some languages are not decidable. 
    The diagram on the right makes this point.
    It has a 
    \protect\citetext{shaded collection 
      \protect\definend{\protect\compclass{Rec}}\protect\index{REC@\string\compclass{REC}, computable sets}  % for use of \string see https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/88357/339
      consisting of the computable languages, those with a Turing machine decider}{%  
      The name \protect\probname{Rec} is because these used to be known as the `recursive' languages.
    }.
    It also has a superset collection
    \protect\definend{\protect\compclass{RE}}\protect\index{RE@\string\compclass{RE}, computably enumerable sets}  % for use of \string see https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/88357/339
    of the computably enumerable languages,
    which have a Turing machine recognizer.}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2001.pdf}
  \qquad
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2002.pdf}
\end{fig}

\begin{remark}
One reason that we are interested in language membership decisions
comes from practice.
An example is that a language compiler must recognize whether
a given source file is a member of the language.

Recall also that Finite State machines decide languages.
For instance we produced one that 
decides whether an input string is a member of the language 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ contains at least two \str{b}'s}}$.
% and such as proving that no Finite State machine can determine membership
% in $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
The same holds for Pushdown machines.
Thus, we can compare machine types
by comparing the list of languages that they can decide.

Still another reason % to express problems in terms of deciding a language
is that in many contexts stating a problem in this
way is natural, as we saw with the \HP.
\end{remark}

Many problems of other types can be recast as language decision problems.

\begin{example}
The \probname{Satisfiability} 
problem,\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem!as a language recognition problem} 
as we have stated it, is a decision problem.
We can recast it as the problem of deciding membership in the language
$
  \SAT=
  \set{E\suchthat
    \text{$E$ is a satisfiable propositional logic statement}}
$.
This restatement is trivial, witnessing that the language decision
problem form is often a natural way to describe the underlying task.
\end{example}

A pattern that we have seen is  
recasting optimization problems as language decision problems by
using bounds as parameters.

\begin{example}
The \probname{Chromatic Number} problem\index{Chromatic Number@\probname{Chromatic Number} problem} 
inputs a graph and returns a minimal~$k\in\N$ such that the graph is 
$k$-colorable.
As stated it is an optimization problem.
Recast it by considering the family of languages,
$
  \lang_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat \text{$\mathcal{G}$ that has a $B$-coloring}}
$
parametrized by $B\in\N^+\!$.
Observe that we have not forgone minimality.
If we could solve the the $\lang_B$ problems 
then given a graph~$\mathcal{G}$
we could find its chromatic number by 
testing $B=1$, $B=2$, etc., until we find the smallest one for which
$\mathcal{G}\in\lang_B$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
The \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem}
is another optimization problem.
Like the prior example we can recast it as a family of language decision 
problems 
$
  \TS_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat
    \text{the graph~$\mathcal{G}$ has a circuit  
          of length no more than $B$}}
$ for the parameter~$B\in\N$.
\end{example}

What is important about these recastings of optimizations 
is that they preserve polytime solvability.
For instance, for the \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem,
if there is a $k\in\N$ so that for each~$B$ we could solve $\mathcal{TS}_B$ in 
time~$\bigOh(n^k)$, then
looping through $B=1$, $B=2$, etc.,\ will solve
the optimization problem in polytime, 
namely time~$\bigOh(n^{k+1})$.

Sometimes it is convenient
to recast using the bound as a parameter, as in the prior example.
% such as if we
% want to know whether there is a Traveling Salesman path through the US lower 48
% state capitals of total length at most $B=10\,000$.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_{B}=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat\text{the graph has a circuit of length at most $B$}}
\end{equation*}
But sometimes it is convenient to instead consider all of the bounds at once.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat\text{the graph has a circuit of length at most $B$}}
\end{equation*}

% Our last example is a preview of the work that we will do later in the chapter
% to relate one problem to another.

% \begin{example}
% Consider the 
% \definend{\probname{Satisfying Assignment}}\index{Satisfying Assignment problem@\probname{Satisfying Assignment} problem} 
% problem 
% that takes in a Boolean expression $E(P_0,P_1\ldots P_k)$ 
% and returns an assignment of truth values for $P_0$, $P_1$, \ldots{} $P_k$
% that makes $E$ evaluate to \true\!, or returns a flag
% if there is no such assignment.
% This is a search problem in that there may be many such assignments
% but we stop if we find even one.

% We will show that this problem is equivalent to the 
% \probname{Satisfiability} language decision problem\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}
% in this sense:~if we could solve one in polytime
% then we could also solve the other in polytime.
% The easy direction is that if we had an algorithm that solves 
% \probname{Satisfying Assignment} in polytime 
% then given an expression~$E$
% we can apply that algorithm to it and know in polytime 
% whether $E$ is satisfiable.

% For the other direction suppose that we have a polytime algorithm for 
% $\SAT$.
% Given an expression~$E$, 
% apply $\SAT$'s algorithm to determine whether $E$ is satisfiable at all.
% If so then we need to return an assignment.
% First fix $P_0$ at \false{} and run $\SAT$'s algorithm.
% If $E(P_0=\false,P_1,P_2\ldots P_k)$ is not satisfiable then
% our assignment needs $P_0=\true$, and otherwise $P_0=\false$ suffices.
% Now with a suitable $P_0$ we iterate, next fixing $P_1=\false$, etc.
% This builds an assignment by wrapping a loop of length~$k$ around
% the polytime algorithm for $\SAT$, and therefore the
% algorithm as a whole is polytime.
% \end{example}

One more point.
Some shifts between problem types are not as smooth as the ones
above.
Consider the function problem 
% of returing rational number roots of a polynomial.
% As a function problem it is
`given a polynomial~$p$, return its rational roots'.
In restating it as a decision problem 
the most natural language is
$ % \begin{equation*}
  % \lang=
  \set{\sequence{p,r}\suchthat\text{$p$ is a polynomial, $r$ is rational, 
                               and $p(r)=0$}}
$. % \end{equation*}
However,
determining membership in this language
just asks us to
plug $r$ into~$p$. 
That
does not
capture the essential difficulty, which is to 
produce the root in the first place.





\subsection{Statements and representations}
To be complete, the description of a problem should include the form of the
inputs and outputs.
For instance, if we state a problem 
as:~`\citetext{input two numbers and output their average}{% 
  See \protect\url{https://hal.archives-ouvertes.fr/file/index/docid/576641/filename/computing-midpoint.pdf}.}'
then we have not fully specified what needs to be done.
% The input or output might use 
% strings representing decimal numbers, 
% or might be floating point representations,
% or even might be integers written in unary.
% \footnote{%
% % where the number $n$ is represented with $n$-many \str{1}'s.
%   An experienced programmer may have the reaction that unary is
%   not useful.
%   % if the input
%   % is one thousand then this representation would require the 
%   % machine to take a thousand steps just to read the input, 
%   % while in binary notation the input is $\str{1111101000}$, which 
%   % takes only ten steps to read.
%   But unary is not completely useless;~we have found that it
%   suited our purpose when we simply wanted to illustrate
%   Turing machines.
%   In any event, it certainly is possible.}
% % Or we could specify something more exotic
% % such as putting the sequence numbers as 
% % powers of primes, so that~$\sequence{2,1,3}$ becomes 
% % the single number $2^23^15^3=1500$ and then we represent that in a string of
% % decimal digits.

% Typically in practice the costs of different representations
% don't change the Big~$\bigOh$ runtime behavior.
% For example we might have a graph algorithm whose run time is~$\bigOh(n\lg n)$.
% Even for this minimal runtime behavior, typically we  can find a 
% representation for the input graphs,
% such as where inputting takes $\bigOh(n)$ time,
% that leaves the algorithm analysis conclusion unchanged
% at~$\bigOh(n\lg n)$.


\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{}{%
  \margingraphic{0.22\textwidth}{complexity/pix/norm.png}{\label{fig:xkcdnorm}\href{https://xkcd.com/2116/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}} }
The representation  
matters because the input's form can change the algorithm.
Suppose that we must decide whether a number is
divisible by four.
For binary input 
the algorithm is immediate:~a number is divisible by four 
if and only if in its 
\citetext{final two bits are \str{00}}{%
  Decimal representation is not much harder since
  a decimal number is divisible by four if and only if the final two digits
  are in the set $\set{\str{00},\str{04}, \,\ldots\,\str{96}}$.}.\footnote{%
    On a Turing machine, 
    if the machine starts with its I/O head under
    the final character then the machine only needs to read the final two 
    characters.
    This is notable because it is an algorithm for a
    nontrivial problem that comes to a decision without reading its
    entire input.
    Its runtime is constant, $\bigOh(1)$.
  }
  If the input is in unary then we need a different algorithm.
  % Another example is that an algorithm for
  % finding the average of two integers represented
  % in decimal is very much less complicated than
  % one for two floating point numbers.

However, the representation doesn't matter in that 
if we have an algorithm for one representation
then we can solve the problem for others by translating.
For example, 
we could do the divisible-by-four problem with unary input by converting
to binary and then applying the binary algorithm.\footnote{%
  That is, the unary case reduces to the binary one.}

One thing that changing 
the representation can significantly change is the run time.
Suppose that a Turing machine inputs and outputs numbers.
If the input representation is binary and the output representation is unary
then the time that the machine takes is exponential in the input size,
just from the time to write output to the tape.\label{disc:BinaryInputUnaryOutputRunsExp}

However, if we are circumspect about representations 
then we usually will not see something like exponential blowup.
Most notably, our default is to represent integers in binary 
and rational numbers as pairs of integers.
% the representation details do not affect the outcome of the analysis,
% much.\footnote{%
%   A caveat:~we have seen that 
%   % conflating 
%   % $\protect\set{n\in\N\suchthat \protect\text{$n$ is prime}}$
%   % with  
%   % $\protect\set{\sigma\in\protect\smash{\protect\kleenestar{\B}}\protect\suchthat \protect\text{$\sigma$ represents a prime number}}$
%   % can cause confusion.
%   the distinction between thinking of an algorithm
%   as inputting a number and thinking of it as inputting the 
%   string representation of a number is the basis for describing 
%   the Big~$\protect\bigOh$ behavior of that algorithm as
%   pseudopolynomial.
%   This is because the binary representation of
%   a number~$n$ takes $\protect\bigOh(\protect\lg n)$ bits.}
Consequently, in practice  
\citetext{researchers often do not mention representations}{%
  This is similar to a programmer saying, ``My program
  inputs a number'' rather than, ``My program inputs the
  binary representation of a number.''
  It is also like a person saying, ``That's me on the card'' rather
  than ``That's a picture of me.''
  It is just a question of how natural language streamlines communications}.
We may describe
the \probname{Shortest Path} problem as, 
``Given a weighted graph and two vertices \ldots{}''
in place of the more 
complete, ``Given
the following reasonably efficient bitstring representation of a 
weighted graph~$\mathcal{G}$ 
and vertices $v_0$ and~$v_1$, \ldots{}''
That is, the proscription that
the description of a problem should include the form of the
inputs and outputs is often ignored.
People use bitstrings unless there is a strong reason to do something
else.
Outside of this section, we also will usually do this. 
% \citetext{leaving implementation details to a programmer}{%
%   \autocite{article:MarsOrbiter}}.

With these reflections in mind we will take the view,
which we call \definend{Lipton's Thesis},\index{Lipton's Thesis} that 
\citetext{everything of interest can be represented with reasonable efficiency by bitstrings}{%
  See
  \url{https://rjlipton.wordpress.com/2010/11/07/what-is-a-complexity-class/}.
  Of course, a wag may say that
  if it cannot be represented by 
  bitstrings then it isn't of interest\Dash that just as when a person has
  a hammer then everything looks like a nail, so also with a computer in hand
  everything looks like bits.
  But we mean something less tautological:~we mean that in everyday experience
  if we could 
  want to compute with it then it can be put in bitstrings.
  For example, we find that we can process speech, 
  adjust colors on an image, 
  or regulate pressure in a rocket fuel tank, all in bitstrings,
  even though at first encounter these seem to be the inherently 
  analog.}.\footnote{%
  `Reasonable' means that it is not so inefficient as to greatly
  change the big-$\bigOh$ behavior.}\index{representation, of a problem}
This applies to 
all of the mathematical problems stated earlier.
But it also applies to cases that may seem less natural, such as that we 
can use bitstrings to represent with sufficient fidelity 
\citetext{Beethoven's 9th Symphony}{%
  The story is that
  CD's are $72$ minutes long so that they can hold this piece.
  See \url{https://www.snopes.com/fact-check/roll-over-beethoven/}.}
or an exquisite Old Master.\footnote{
  This is in a way like Church's Thesis.
  We cannot prove it but our 
  experience with digital reproduction of music, movies, 
  etc.,\ argues that it is so.}
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
\begin{fig}[basketoffruit]{\textit{Basket of Fruit} by Caravaggio (1571--1610)} % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e5/Basket_of_Fruit-Caravaggio_%28c.1595%29.jpg
  \includegraphics[width=0.55\textwidth]{complexity/pix/caravaggio.jpg}
\end{fig}
}{%
\begin{fig}[basketoffruit]{\textit{Basket of Fruit} by Caravaggio (1571--1610)} % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e5/Basket_of_Fruit-Caravaggio_%28c.1595%29.jpg
  \includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{complexity/pix/caravaggio.jpg}  % best is .9\textwidth
\end{fig}
}

When we do discuss representations, we 
use \definend{$\strng(x)$}\index{Ste function@$\strng$ function} 
to denote a convenient, reasonably efficient,
bitstring representation of~$x$.\footnote{%
  Some authors use diamond 
  brackets to stand for a representation,
  as in $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1}$.
  In this book we reserve them for sequences.}

% As another consequence, in the remaining sections of this chapter we can take the
% alphabet to be $\Sigma=\B$.
% It is a convenience and the only consequence will be small changes
% in the statement of a few results.


 % ......................................
\begin{exercises}
% \item[\parbox{\textwidth}{\it These exercises here discuss 
%   represenations  
%   more than is usual elsewhere in the book.}]

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each of these, list three examples and then\Dash speaking informally, 
since some of them
do not have formal definitions\Dash describe the 
difference between them and an algorithm.
\begin{exparts*}
\item heuristic
\item pseudocode
\item Turing machine
\item flowchart
\item source code
\item executable
\item process
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\textit{Note:}~the meanings give here are common usage.
But because these terms are not formally defined, different authors
may use them in slightly different ways.
\begin{exparts}
\item A heuristic is a shortcut, 
an approach to a problem that is more reliable than simple guessing
and is faster than a full solution
(or perhaps no full solution is possible).
But the connotation of `heuristic'  is that it
is not a theoretically correct full solution.

One example is approaching the Traveling Salesman problem 
by using a greedy algorithm, that is, always going next to the nearest
not-yet-visited city.
Another example is that antivirus programs use heuristics to try
and determine if an executable is malicious, such as comparing the
first few bytes to a list of known-bad ones.

A third example is based on the data 
in \figureref{fig:USPresidentsHeights}.
In the contests for US President since 1900, the taller candidate
has won $21$ out of $31$ times, for a sample proportion of~$0.68$
(not counting 1992).
The chance of such a result by happenstance is less than four percent. % 0.03538
You could decide to bet in the next election on the taller candidate.
This isn't logical, but it is a method and it is used in practice.
\begin{figure}
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}[t]{lll|c}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Year}}
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Winner}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Other}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Tallest?}} \\
      \hline
      1900 &W McKinley     &W J Bryan      &N  \\
      1904 &T Roosevelt    &A B Parker     &Y   \\
      1908 &W H Taft       &W J Bryan      &Y    \\
      1912 &W Wilson       &--2 others--   &N    \\
      1916 &W Wilson       &C E Hughes     &Y    \\
      1920 &W G Harding    &J M Cox        &Y    \\
      1924 &C Coolidge     &J W Davis      &N    \\
      1928 &H Hoover       &A Smith        &Y    \\
      1932 &F D Roosevelt  &H Hoover       &Y    \\
      1936 &F D Roosevelt  &A Landon       &Y    \\
      1940 &F D Roosevelt  &W Willkie      &N    \\
      1944 &F D Roosevelt  &T Dewey        &Y    \\
      1948 &H S Truman     &T Dewey        &Y    \\
      1952 &D Eisenhower   &A Stevenson II &Y    \\
      1956 &D Eisenhower   &A Stevenson II &Y    \\
      1960 &J F Kennedy    &R Nixon     &Y    \\
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{tabular}[t]{lll|c}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Year}}
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Winner}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Other}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Tallest?}} \\
      \hline
      1964 &L B Johnson &B Goldwater &Y    \\
      1968 &R Nixon     &H Humphrey  &Y    \\
      1972 &R Nixon     &G McGovern  &N    \\
      1976 &J Carter    &G Ford      &N    \\
      1980 &R Reagan    &J Carter    &Y    \\
      1984 &R Reagan    &W Mondale   &Y    \\
      1990 &G H W Bush  &M Dukakis   &Y    \\
      1992 &W Clinton   &G H W Bush  &--same--    \\
      1996 &W Clinton   &R Dole      &Y    \\
      2000 &G W Bush    &A Gore      &N    \\
      2004 &G W Bush    &J Kerry     &N    \\
      2008 &B Obama     &J McCain    &Y    \\
      2012 &B Obama     &M Romney    &N    \\
      2016 &D Trump     &H Clinton   &Y    \\
      2020 &J Biden     &D Trump     &N    \\
      2024 &D Trump     &K Harris    &Y    \\
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Height comparison of US Presidential winner and main opponent, 
             since 1900.} 
    \label{fig:USPresidentsHeights}
\end{figure}

So, a heuristic is a method that, while it may be used in practice, 
is not guaranteed to be optimal, perfect, or even rational.
In contrast, an algorithm for a problem is guaranteed to solve that problem.
\item 
Pseudocode is a description of an algorithm. 
It gives
a sketch or or outline, usually at a quite high level.
It is often laid out as though it were compilable code but
usually it does not conform to any specific language's syntax; for instance
it typically does not include details such 
as declaration of variables.

This is pseudocode for the recursive depth-first traversal
of a binary tree.
\begin{display}
  \begin{minipage}{0.4\textwidth}
\begin{lstlisting}
process_subtree(v)
  process_subtree(v's left child)
  process_vertex(v)
  process_subtree(v's right child)
\end{lstlisting}
  \end{minipage}
\end{display}
Another example describes the most simple algorithm for checking 
whether a number is prime,
by dividing it by each natural smaller than it but bigger than one. 
\begin{display}
  \begin{minipage}{0.4\textwidth}
\begin{lstlisting}
is_prime(n)
  for 2 <= i < n
    if i divides n  
      return False
  return True
\end{lstlisting}
  \end{minipage}
\end{display}
We have seen many other examples in this book, often given as flowcharts.
% including 
% the descriptions in the proof of Chapter Two's
% \zcref{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}.

So pseudocode is close to a specification of an algorithm.
But in ordinary usage, an algorithm is completely specified so that an 
implementing programmer does not need to work out any significant details,
while pseudocode may leave some details unspecified.
(An analogy:~an experienced cook may describe making Scalloped Potatoes as,
``bake thin-sliced potatoes in a white sauce with breadcrumbs on top.''
That is not complete in that it depends on the implementer's understanding 
a number of details, but it is headed in the direction of a recipe.)
\item 
Of course, in the first section of the first chapter we saw a number
of Turing machines, such as one that computes the predecessor function,
another that computes the sum of two inputs, and a third that never halts.

A Turing machine is more like a program than an algorithm, in that
it is an implementation of an algorithm.
For instance, we could have the Bubble Sort algorithm in mind and 
then implement it as a Turing machine.
\item A flowchart is pseudocode given in graphical form.
It describes an algorithm, typically at a high level
(sometimes even higher than that usually used for pseudocode).
We have seen a number of flowcharts in this book, including in this chapter.
Flowcharts differ from algorithms in the same way that pseudocode does.
\item Source code differs from an algorithm in that is an
implementation of an algorithm
(sometimes a number of algorithms are bundled together to make one source).
Source is targeted at a specific compiler or interpreter, and perhaps at a
specific hardware and software platform.

For example, there is
source code in Racket that implements the algorithm
to determine if an input
natural number~$n$ is prime by trying to divide by all numbers less than~$n$.
% Another example is that
% there is source that gives this book as output, on compilation with
% \LaTeX{} and related programs. 
\item An executable is a binary file that can be run on an operating system.
It is usually the result of compiling source code, and so is an implementation
of an algorithm.
Three examples are the executable that results from compiling
(and linking) the ``Hello World!'' program in C, 
the executable for your browser, and
the executable that typesets input \LaTeX{} code. 
\item A process is an instance of a program that has been 
has been loaded into a machine's memory for execution.
As such, it is an implementation of an algorithm (or perhaps a number
of algorithms).
Three examples are the result of calling the executable of 
the ``Hello World!'' program, 
of calling the executable for your browser, and
of invoking the executable for \LaTeX. 

Some processes, such as an operating system shell or a web server, are 
be designed to run forever, while
typically in computational complexity discussions 
the term `algorithm' is used for something that performs some task
and then halts.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  A.A. at \url{https://rjlipton.wordpress.com/2010/11/07/what-is-a-complexity-class/#comment-8872}
\end{credit}
Your friend asks,
``So, if a problem is essentially a language, a set of strings, 
then what constitutes a solution?''
Answer them.
\begin{answer}\verified
A solution is an algorithm that acts as the
characteristic function of the  string set.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What is the difference between a decision problem and a language
decision problem?
\begin{answer}\verified
A decision problem is any one that requires a `yes' or `no' answer.
A language decision problem is a specialization of that, to
`yes' or `no' answers about membership in the given language.
(However, we often take languages as precise descriptions of the 
problems that we are working on, and the two terms may sometimes be casually
conflated.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
As an illustration of the thesis that even surprising things 
can be represented reasonably efficiently and with reasonable fidelity 
in binary, we can do a simple calculation of the limits of human perception.
\begin{exparts*}
\item 
  Reasonably close viewing distance is \citetext{$30$~cm}{%
  About a foot.}.
  At that distance the human eye has a resolution of about $0.01$~cm.
How many such pixels are there in a photograph that is 
$21$~cm by $30$~cm
(the dimensions of an ordinary piece of paper)?
\item
We can see about a million colors.
How many bits per pixel is that?
\item How many bits for the photo, in total?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
The multiplication is easy:
$(1/0.01)^2\cdot 21\cdot 30= \scinot{6.30}{6}\!$.
\item
$1+\floor{\lg(1\,000\,000)}=20$
% sage: 1+floor(log(1e6,2))
% 20
\item Multiply $(\scinot{6.30}{6})\cdot 20=\scinot{1.26}{8}$.
There are eight million bits in a megabyte.
So the total is less than $16$ megabytes, about eight books from 
Project Gutenberg or a ten minute phone video
(in H.264 at 1080).
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
% sage: 1+floor(log(1e6,2))
% 20
% sage: 6.30e6*20
% 1.26000000000000e8
% sage: 6.30e6*20/8000000
% 15.7500000000000
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  A search problem has a single input for each output.
  How does that differ from a function problem?
  \begin{answer}
    The output is not determined from the input.
    For instance, if the input query asks to
    produce a US state that contains a city
    named Springfield then one correct output is Alaska while another
    is Vermont.
    But a function must be well-defined, so the output is uniquely
    determined by the input.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Name something important that cannot be represented in binary.
% \begin{answer}\verified
% One is the smell of a baby's head.
% They have a great smell,
% and nothing is more exciting than a baby,
% but it is hard to see how to compute that.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false:~any two programs that implement the same algorithm 
must compute the same function.
What about the converse?
\begin{answer}\verified
  True, the requirement that an algorithm is well-specified means that
  two implementations must have the same input/output behavior.
  
  The converse does not hold;
  two programs computing the same function need not implement
  the same algorithm. 
  For instance, two programs may both sort input
  strings but one uses Bubble Sort while the other uses
  Quicksort.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  \begin{credit}
    SE user Yuval Filmus \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/79729}
  \end{credit}
Some tasks are hard to express as 
language decision problems.
Consider sorting the characters of a string
into ascending order.
Briefly describe why each of these language decision problems 
fails to capture the task's essential difficulty.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ is sorted}}$
\item $\set{\sequence{\sigma,p}\suchthat \text{$p$ is a permutation 
      that orders $\sigma$}}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Recognizing a correctly sorted string is a different task than
  finding a good way (or indeed the best way) to sort an unsorted string.
\item Similarly, recognizing a correct permutation feels different\Dash and
  much easier\Dash than generating that permutation when given an unsorted 
  string.      
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each language decision problem, name three members of the set, if there are
three, and then sketch an algorithm solving it.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\lang_0=\set{\sequence{n,m}\in\N^2\suchthat
                  \text{$n+m$ is a square and is one greater than a prime}}$
\item
$\lang_1=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat\text{$\sigma$ represents in decimal a multiple of $100$}}$
\item
$\lang_2=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ has more \str{1}'s than \str{0}'s}}$
\item $\lang_3=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  These algorithms signal that the input is a member of the language
  by returning~$1$, 
  and signal that is it not by returning~$0$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Because the number $4$ is a square that is one greater than a prime,
three members are $\sequence{0,4}$, $\sequence{1,3}$,
and $\sequence{2,2}$,
An algorithm computing the characteristic function is to  
input the sequence and unpack it to get $n,m\in\N$,
Then add the two numbers.
If the sum is both square and one greater than a prime then return~$1$,
otherwise return~$0$.
\item
Three string members of $\lang$ are $\sigma_0=\str{200}$, $\sigma_1=\str{100}$, 
and $\sigma_2=\str{300}$.
The algorithm is:~if the input is a single~\str{0} 
or ends in two \str{0}'s, then return~$1$.
Otherwise return~$0$.
\item 
The language $\lang_2$ contains $\str{11}$, $\str{110}$, and $\str{1}$. 
A natural algorithm is to 
scan the input string, keeping a running count of the number of
\str{0}'s and \str{1}'s.
At the end if there are more \str{1}'s then return~$1$,
otherwise return~$0$.
\item
Recall that strings equal to their reverse are called palindromes.
Three palindromes over $\kleenestar{\B}$ are 
\str{101}, \str{11}, and~\str{1}.    
To compute the characteristic function, read and save the input string.
Reverse the string and compare it with the original.
If it matches then return~$1$ and
otherwise return~$0$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Solve the language decision problem for
\begin{exparts*}
\item the empty language,\index{empty language!decision problem} 
\item the language~$\B$, 
and
\item the language~$\kleenestar{\B}$\!.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  These algorithms return~$1$ if they accept the input as a
  member of the language, and~$0$ if they do not.
\begin{exparts}
\item The empty language is $\lang=\emptyset=\set{}$.
Its characteristic function is $\charfcn{\lang}(n)=0$.
The algorithm to solve this language decision problem is to, for all
input, return~$0$.
\item This language contains two strings,
$\B=\set{\str{0},\str{1}}$ (technically these are characters, 
not length one strings, but
in this book we feel free to discount the difference if that is convenient).
The characteristic function is this.
\begin{equation*}
  \charfcn{\B}(\sigma)=
  \begin{cases}
    1  &\case{if $\sigma=\str{0}$ or $\sigma=\str{1}$}  \\
    0  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
The algorithm for computing that function is obvious.
\item The language $\kleenestar{\B}$ contains all of the bitstrings.
The algorithm that solves the decision problem for this language will,
given a bitstring, return~$1$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language, sketch an algorithm that solves the language decision
problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
                  \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{a*ba*}}}$
\item 
The language defined by this grammar
\begin{grammar}
S \produces{} AB 

A \produces{} \trm{a}A | \emptystring

B \produces{} \trm{b}B | \emptystring
\end{grammar} 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  These algorithms signal they accept the input as a member of the language
  by returning~$1$
  and signal rejection with~$0$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
For the algorithm, fix a Finite State machine~$\mathcal{M}$ that
recognizes the language described by \re{a*ba*}.
Read the input
and simulate running~$\mathcal{M}$ on that input.
This will terminate in a finite number of steps.
If $\mathcal{M}$ ends in an accepting state then return~$1$, 
otherwise return~$0$.

Stated more simply, the algorithm outputs~$1$ if the input consists entirely
of \str{a}'s, with a single~\str{b}, and otherwise outputs~$0$.
\item
The grammar defines the language
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^nb^m\suchthat n,m\in\N}$.
The algorithm is:~input the string and if all of the \str{b}'s 
come after all of the \str{a}'s
then return~$1$.
Otherwise, return~$0$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user gnasher729 \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/106127/67754}
\end{credit}
Solve each decision problem about Finite State 
machines by producing an algorithm.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Given $\mathcal{M}$, decide if the language accepted by~$\mathcal{M}$
is empty.
\item Decide if $\lang(\mathcal{M})$
is the set of all strings,~$\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$. 
\item \textit{Difficult.}
  Decide if the language accepted by~$\mathcal{M}$
  is infinite. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
We will just suggest the algorithm.

First we observe that there is an algorithm that,
given a machine~$\mathcal{M}$ and a state~$q$ in that machine,
finds the set of states reachable from~$q$.
The algorithm proceeds in steps. 
Step~$1$ initializes by taking~$R_0=\set{q}$.
Step~$i+1$ begins with the set of states~$R_i$ that are 
reachable from~$q$ with $i$-many transitions or fewer.
In that step, to make $R_{i+1}$ start with~$R_i$.
Add to it any states
reachable in one transition from a state in~$R_i$.
If there are no such new states, so that $R_{i+1}=R_i$ then the algorithm stops.
This algorithm must eventually terminate because the number of states in the
machine is finite.
\begin{exparts}
\item The language is empty if and only if from the start state,
no accepting state is reachable.
\item Given $\mathcal{M}$,
obtain a new machine~$\hat{\mathcal{M}}$ that
recognizes the complement of $\lang(\mathcal{M})$ by 
copying~$\mathcal{M}$, except setting each accepting 
state of~$\mathcal{M}$ to be non-accepting in~$\hat{\mathcal{M}}$ 
and setting each non-accepting state of~$\mathcal{M}$ to be accepting
in~$\hat{\mathcal{M}}$.
Then $\lang(\mathcal{M})=\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
if and only if $\lang(\hat{\mathcal{M}})$
is empty, and we have reduced to this exercise's first item.
\item  % from SE user gnasher729, https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/106127/67754
The language is infinite if and only if 
there is a state~$\hat{q}$ that can be reached from the initial state,
and some accepting state can be reached from~$\hat{q}$,
and is such that $\hat{q}$ can be reached from itself by a nonempty string.
(That is, it can be pumped.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  For each language, give an algorithm for its decision problem
  that runs in~$\bigOh(1)$.
Assume that $\Sigma=\B$ and that the 
Turing machine starts with its I/O head pointing to the input's initial bit.
\begin{exparts}
\item The language of minimal-length binary representations of numbers 
that are nonzero.
\item The binary representations of numbers that exceed~$1000$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  Here we just suggest each algorithm.
\begin{exparts}
\item See if the input string starts with a~\str{1} bit.
\item The number represented in decimal as $1000$ when converted to
  binary is $1111101000$, which is ten bits.
  So if the input is eleven bits then it is in the language.
  If the input is fewer than ten bits then it is not in the language.
  If the input is exactly ten bits then there are a 
  fixed number of cases to do, such as checking whether the second bit
  is~\str{0}, but that 
  doesn't change that the algorithm is $\bigOh(1)$.
% sage: 1000.binary()
% '1111101000'
% sage: len(1000.binary())
% 10
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In a graph, a 
\definend{bridge edge}\index{bridge}\index{graph!bridge edge}
is one whose removal disconnects the graph.
More precisely, it is a bridge edge if the graph has two 
vertices that are connected by at least one path 
before that edge is removed but 
are not connected by any path after it is removed.
Consider this problem:~given a graph, find a bridge.
Is this a function problem, 
a decision problem, a language decision problem, a search
problem, or an optimization problem?
\begin{answer}\verified
This is a search problem since we need only produce a single bridge.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, give the categorization that best applies:~function problem, 
decision problem, language decision problem, 
search problem, or optimization problem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The \definend{\probname{Graph Connectedness}} problem,\index{Graph Connectedness problem@\probname{Graph Connectedness} problem}
which inputs a graph
and decides whether for any two vertices there is a path between them.
\item
The problem that inputs two natural numbers and returns their least
common multiple.
\item The 
\definend{\probname{Graph Isomorphism}} problem\index{Graph Isomorphism problem@\probname{Graph Isomorphism} problem} 
that inputs two graphs and determines
whether they are isomorphic.
\item The problem that takes in a propositional logic statement and 
returns an assignment of truth values to its inputs that makes
the statement true, if there is such an assignment.
\item
The \definend{\probname{Nearest Neighbor}} problem\index{Nearest Neighbor@\probname{Nearest Neighbor} problem} 
that inputs a weighted graph and a vertex, and 
returns a vertex nearest the given one that does not equal the
given one.
\item The 
\definend{\probname{Discrete Logarithm}}\index{Discrete Logarithm problem@\probname{Discrete Logarithm} problem} 
problem:~given 
a prime number~$p$ and two
numbers $a,b\in\N$, determine whether there is a power~$k\in\N$ so that
$a^k\equiv b \pmod p$.
\item The problem of determining membership in the set
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
    \text{when $\sigma$ is converted to decimal it contains only odd digits}}$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  It can be hard to tell a decision problem from a language decision problem.
  The difference is that the latter is stated in terms of a language.
\begin{exparts}
\item This is a yes-or-no question,  
  so it is a decision problem.
\item Function problem.
\item Decision problem.
\item Search problem.
\item Search problem.
\item Decision problem.
\item Language decision problem.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, give the characterization that best applies:~function problem, 
decision problem, language decision problem, search
problem, or optimization problem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The \ThreeSAT{} problem,\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
\zcref{prob:3SAT}
\item The \probname{Divisor} problem, \zcref{prob:Divisor}\index{Divisor@\probname{Divisor} problem}
\item The \probname{Prime Factorization} problem, \zcref{prob:Factorization}\index{Factorization@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}
\item The problem % definend{\probname{F-SAT}} problem,\index{F-SAT@\probname{F-SAT} problem} 
  where the input is a propositional logic
  expression and the output is either an assignment of \true{} and~\false{}
  to the expression's variables that makes it evaluate to~\true{},
  or the string \str{None}.
\item The \probname{Primality} problem, \zcref{prob:Composite}\index{Primality problem@\probname{Primality} problem}
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  It can be hard to tell a decision problem from a language decision problem.
  The difference is that the latter is stated in terms of a language.
\begin{exparts}
\item As stated in \zcref{prob:3SAT} it is a decision problem.
\item Search problem.
\item Function problem.
\item Search problem.
  % The `F' in \probname{F-SAT} stands for `functional'.
\item Decision problem.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Each of these inputs a square matrix~$M$
with at least $3$~rows, and relates to a $3\times 3$ submatrix 
(form the submatrix by picking three rows and three columns, which need
not be adjacent).
Give the best characterization:~function problem, 
decision problem, language decision problem, search
problem, or optimization problem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Find a submatrix that is invertible.
\item Decide if there is an invertible submatrix.
\item Return a submatrix 
that is invertible, or the string `\str{None}'.
\item Return a submatrix 
whose determinant has the largest absolute value.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Search problem.
\item Decision problem.
\item Search problem.
\item Optimization problem.
  (The differences between problem types can be fuzzy.
  A person could reason that there might be more than one such
  submatrix and call this a search problem.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  For each, give an appropriate language, a set of strings,
  to express the task as a language decision problem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Decide whether a number is a perfect square.
\item Decide whether a triple $\sequence{x,y,z}\in\N^3$ is a 
   Pythagorean triple, that is, whether $x^2+y^2=z^2\!$.
\item Decide whether a graph has an even number of edges.
\item Decide whether a path in a graph has any repeated vertices.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  For each we must give a set of strings.
\begin{exparts}
\item A language suited to machines with unary input is  
$\lang_0=\set{\str{1}^n\suchthat \text{$n\in\N$ is a square}}$.
A language suited to more everyday input is
$\lang_1=\set{d\in\set{\str{0},\ldots{}\str{9}}^+\suchthat
              \text{$d$ represents a perfect square in base~$10$}}$. 
We use $\set{\str{0},\ldots~\str{9}}^+$ to mean strings of at least
one decimal digit.
\item 
One language is this set of string triples.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sequence{x,y,z}\in(\set{\str{0},\ldots~\str{9}}^+)^3\suchthat
        \text{interpreted as decimal representations, $x^2+y^2=z^2$}}
\end{equation*}
We use $\set{\str{0},\ldots~\str{9}}^+$ to mean strings of at least
one decimal digit.
\item 
A language is 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a graph $\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}}$ where the number of edges in $\mathcal{E}$ is even}}$.
\item 
The language is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a pair $\sequence{\mathcal{G},p}$ where $p$ is a path with a repeated vertex}}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:RecastAsDecision}
  For each task, give an appropriate language, a set of strings,
  to express it as a language decision problem.
representation.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
\probname{Graph Colorability},\index{Graph Colorability problem@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem} 
  \zcref{prob:GraphColorability},
\item
\probname{Euler Circuit},\index{Euler Circuit@\probname{Euler Circuit} problem} 
  \zcref{prob:EulerCircuit},
\item
\probname{Shortest Path},\index{Shortest Path problem@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} 
  \zcref{prob:ShortestPath}.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  For each we must give a set of strings.
\begin{exparts}
\item 
The \probname{Graph Colorability} problem is stated as a decision problem,
so we just need to give a suitable language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents 
      $\sequence{\mathcal{G},k}$, where $\mathcal{G}$ is $k$-colorable}}
\end{equation*}
Often that language would be written in this way
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},k}\suchthat \text{$\mathcal{G}$ is $k$-colorable}}
\end{equation*}
although that description does not explicitly mention the string representation.
\item The version given in the text is a search problem.
This is a language for the language decision problem version.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a graph that has a circuit that traverses each edge exactly once}}
\end{equation*}
\item 
The \probname{Shortest Path} problem is given in the text as a function problem.
This is a language for the language decision problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents 
      $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,p}$, where $p$ is a minimal-length path between the vertices}}
\end{equation*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Recast each as a language decision problem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Given a natural number, do its factors add to more than twice the number?
\item
Given a Turing machine and input, does the machine halt on the input
in less than ten steps?
\item 
Given a propositional logic statement, are there three different assignments 
that evaluate to \true{}?
That is, are there more than three lines 
in the truth table that end in~\true{}?
\item 
Given a weighted graph and a bound~$B\in\R$, for any two vertices is there
a path from one to the other with total cost less than the bound?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  A language is a set of strings.
  So for each of these the members of the set are technically bitstrings.
  Thus in the first answer we could write the set of bitstrings that
  represent decimal numbers,
  but it is common to not emphasize that detail.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{n\in\N\suchthat\text{the factors of~$n$ sum to more than $2n$}}$.
\item $\set{\sequence{\TM,i}\suchthat\text{machine $\TM$ on input~$i$ halts in less than ten steps}}$
\item $\set{E\suchthat\text{the logic expression~$E$ can be satisfied by three distinct assignments}}$
\item $\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat\text{for all $v_0,v_1\in\mathcal{G}$ there is a path of cost less than~$B$}}$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Restate the \HP{}\index{Halting problem!as a decision problem} 
as a language decision problem.
\begin{answer}\verified
It is about deciding membership in the language
$\set{\sequence{\TM_e,e}\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input $e$}}$.
(To emphasize that a language is a set of strings we could write
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ represents a pair $\sequence{\TM_e,e}$ where $\TM_e$ halts on $e$}}$.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Recast each function problem as a decision problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem of inputting two natural numbers and returning their
product.
\item The \probname{Nearest Neighbor}\index{Nearest Neighbor@\probname{Nearest Neighbor} problem} 
problem
that inputs a weighted graph and a vertex, and
returns the vertex nearest the given one but not equal to it. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
Note that this exercise does not call explicitly for language decision
problems.
It calls for decision problems.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given a triple $\sequence{n_0,n_1,p}\in\N^3$, decide if $p=n_0\cdot n_1$.
\item Given a triple
$\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1}$, where $\mathcal{G}$ is a weighted graph 
and $v_0\neq v_1$ are vertices in that graph,
decide if $v_1$ is a vertex that is
nearest to~$v_0$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
As stated, the \probname{Shortest Path}\index{Shortest Path problem@\probname{Shortest Path} problem}
problem, \zcref{prob:ShortestPath}, 
is an optimization problem.
Convert it into a parametrized family of language decision problems
using the technique outlined following the
\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem, \zcref{ex:TravellingSalesman}.
\begin{answer}\verified
This family 
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_{B}=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1}\suchthat\text{There is a path in $\mathcal{G}$ from $v_0$ to $v_1$ of total weight less than or equal to~$B$}}
\end{equation*}
is parametrized by the bound~$B$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Express each optimization problem as a 
parametrized family of language decision problems.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Given a \probname{Fifteen Game}\index{Fifteen Game@\probname{Fifteen Game} problem} 
board, find the least number of slides that will
solve it.
\item Given a Rubik's cube configuration, find the least number of moves
to solve it.
\item Given a list of jobs that must be accomplished to assemble a car,
along with how long each job takes and which jobs must be done before
which other jobs, find the shortest time to finish the entire car.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item We use $\mathcal{B}$ to denote a game board, a $4\times 4$
array containing the numbers $1$ through $14$ along with a blank.
Take~$B\in\N$ as the parameter.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_B=\set{\mathcal{B}\suchthat
               \text{$\mathcal{B}$ can be solved in fewer than $B$ moves}}
\end{equation*}
\item Take~$B\in\N$ as a bound. 
Write $\mathcal{R}$ for a Rubik's cube arrangement, a sequence of six $3\times3$
arrays populated by nine members of the set $C_0$, \ldots{}
nine members of the set $C_5$.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_B=\set{\mathcal{R}\suchthat
               \text{$\mathcal{R}$ can be solved in fewer than $B$ moves}}
\end{equation*}
\item Take~$B\in\R^+\cup\set{0}$. 
  We can denote a way to finish a car while satisfying the constraints
  with a sequence whose
elements are jobs $C=\sequence{j_0,\ldots\,j_k}$.
With every such sequence there is a total time~$t(C)$.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_B=\set{C\suchthat
               \text{$t(C)\leq B$}}
\end{equation*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
As stated in \zcref{prob:HamCircuit},
the 
\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem\index{Hamiltonian Circuit problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}
is a decision problem.
Give a function version of this problem, a search version, and
an optimization version.
\begin{answer}\verified
A function version inputs a graph and
outputs the set of every Hamiltonian circuit.
A search version inputs a graph and
outputs a Hamiltonian circuit, if one exists,
and some nominal value such as \str{0} if no circuit exists.
An optimization version outputs a circuit that is best by some
criterion.
For instance, in a weighted graph it returns the circuit
of least weight; this is the \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
An \definend{independent set} in a graph is a collection of vertices
such that no two are connected by an edge.
Give a version of the task of finding an 
independent 
set\index{Independent Set problem@\probname{Independent Set} problem} 
that is a
\begin{exparts*}
\item decision problem,
\item language decision problem,
\item search problem,
\item function problem,
and
\item optimization problem.
\end{exparts*}
(For some parts there is more than one reasonable answer.)
\begin{answer}\verified
  Observe that
the empty set of vertices is independent, as is a set with one vertex.
So every graph has a subset of its vertices that are independent.
\begin{exparts}
\item 
Because of the observation we can't reasonably say, 
``Given a graph, decide if it has an independent set.''
% The answer would always be `Yes'.
We can instead use a parameter~$B$:~given a graph, 
decide if it has an independent set with $B$~vertices. 
\item For $B\in\N^+$ Use the language
  $\lang_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat
        \text{$\mathcal{G}$ has $B$ vertices that do not share an edge}}$.
\item Given a graph~$G$, find an independent set with $B$-many vertices.
  (There may be more than one, or none, but if we find one then we are done.)
\item Given a graph, return all independent sets of size~$B$,
  or the string \str{None}.
\item Given a graph, return an independent set that is maximal, that
  is, it is as large as possible for the graph.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give an example of a task
where the decision variant is solvable much more quickly
than the search variant.
\begin{answer}\verified
One is the task of finding the maximum integer in a nonempty list.
The natural decision problem, ``Decide if the list has a maximum'' is trivial
since every list has a maximum (there may be a tie), and so is $\bigOh(1)$.
But the natural search variant, ``Find the maximum'' 
requires looking through the list and so is $\bigOh(n)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
