\index{P@$\P$|)}






% ===========================================
% The \texorpdfstring avoids a hyperref error from math in the bookmarks
\section{\texorpdfstring{$\NP$}{NP}}\index{NP@$\NP$|(}
We have previously considered Finite State machines that are 
nondeterministic,\index{nondeterminism!for Finite State machines}\index{Finite State machine!nondeterminism} 
where from a present configuration and input
there may be more than one next state,
or one, or zero.
We now do the same for Turing 
machines.\index{nondeterminism!for Turing machines}\index{Turing machine!nondeterminism}  
This one has
two instructions starting
with $q_0$ and~\str{0}.
If it is in $q_0$ and it reads a~\str{0} on the tape then
it can go to both state~$q_2$ and state~$q_1$.
\begin{equation*}
  \TM=\set{        
        \tminstruction{0}{0}{1}{2},\,
        \tminstruction{0}{0}{R}{1},\,
        \tminstruction{1}{0}{B}{1},\,
        \tminstruction{1}{0}{1}{3},\,
        \tminstruction{2}{1}{1}{2},\,
        \tminstruction{3}{1}{0}{3}
}
\end{equation*}
For such a machine the computational history 
is more than a line, it is a tree. % \footnote{More is on \protect\zpageref{discussion:ComputationTree}.}
Below is part of that tree for the machine $\TM$ with input \str{00}. 
It is directed, meaning that edges go from left to right.
The picture shows three maximal paths.
The middle one is highlighted.
The top and middle one are finite, while the bottom path has infinitely many 
nodes.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/ndtm/ndtm001.pdf}
\end{center}




\subsection{Nondeterministic Turing machines}
We modify the definition of a Turing machine by 
omitting the restriction that it must be deterministic.
% \footnote{%
%   Recall that determinism is the requirement that different 
%   four-tuples instructions cannot begin with the same $q_pT_p$.
% }

%<*def:NondeterministicTM>
\begin{definition}  \label{def:NondeterministicTuringMachine}
A \definend{nondeterministic Turing machine}~$\TM$\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!definition} 
is a finite set of
instructions\index{instruction}\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!instruction} 
$q_pT_pT_nq_n\in Q\times \Sigma\times(\Sigma\cup\set{\str{L},\str{R}})\times Q$.
The set~$Q$ of \definend{states}\index{states!set of} is finite.
Some of them, $F\subseteq Q$, are \definend{accepting states}\index{accepting state}\index{state!accepting}\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!accepting state}.
The \definend{tape alphabet}\index{tape alphabet}\index{alphabet!tape}\index{Turing machine!tape alphabet}
set $\Sigma$ is finite, 
contains at least two members, including
blank\index{blank},
and does not contain the characters $\str{L}$ or~$\str{R}$.
\end{definition}
%</def:NondeterministicTM>

As always, the association between the pair
$\sequence{\text{present state}, \text{input character}}$
% pair $\sequence{\text{present state},\text{present character}}$
and what the machine does next is given by the 
\definend{transition function}.\index{transition function}\index{function!transition}\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!transition function}
% or \definend{next-state function}\index{next-state function}\index{function!next-state}\index{Turing machine!next-state function}
The 
difference here is that the transition function outputs a set of pairs 
$\sequence{\text{action},\text{next state}}$ instead of just one,
so it has the signature $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{\powerset((\Sigma\cup\set{\str{L},\str{R}})\times Q)}$.

After the definition of a deterministic Turing machine
we gave a full description of how they act
as a sequence of `$\yields$'~steps.
\zcref{exer:DefnYieldsNondeterministicTM}
asks for the same for these machines. 
The nodes are configurations~$\mathcal{C}$
and the root node is the initial configuration~$\mathcal{C}_0$.
As always, $\mathcal{C}\yields\hat{\mathcal{C}}$
means that the second configuration immediately succeeds the first.
A maximal path\index{maximal path}\index{computation tree!maximal path} 
has the form $\mathcal{C}_0\yields \cdots\,\mathcal{C}_h$, where
$\mathcal{C}_h$ has no immediate successors,
or is of the form $\mathcal{C}_0\yields\mathcal{C}_1\yields\cdots\,$,
with infinitely many vertices.
As illustrated above, the key difference is that here the computation
is a tree.\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!computation tree}

Having a computation tree instead of a sequence 
\citetext{adds a few wrinkles}{%
  But it avoids a wrinkle that we needed for Finite State machines
  and Pushdown machines,
  $\varepsilon$ transitions, since Turing machines are not 
  required to consume their input one character at a time.}.
It might be that some maximal paths\footnote{
  Recall from the discussion of nondeterministic Finite State machines
  that some authors say `branch' but that term means
  something else in Graph Theory.}
terminate while others
do not\Dash of course, a deterministic Turing machine cannot
halt half of the time and not halt the other half\Dash
and some might terminate with~$\str{1}$ on the tape 
while others end with~$\str{11}$.
The most natural approach here is not to work with
\citetext{functions computed by these machines}{%
  One thing that we could do is to 
  define that the nondeterministic machine computes
  $\map{f}{\kleenestar{\B}}{\kleenestar{\B}}$ is that if 
  on an input $\sigma$, all maximal tree paths halt and they all leave the
  same value on the tape, which we call $f(\sigma)$.
  Otherwise, the value is undefined, $f(\sigma)\diverges$.
  }
but instead to use them as language deciders.

\label{disc:NondeterminismTMsMentalModels}
From the Finite State machine discussion we 
inherit two mental models for how nondeterministic machines act.
One is that the machine is unboundedly parallel and simultaneously 
computes all of the paths.
It accepts the input if 
it computes at least one
maximal path ending in a configuration with an accepting state,
otherwise it rejects.
The other model is that the machine guesses the maximal path to choose,
or is told by a demon.
The machine accepts its input if there is a maximal
path ending in an accepting configuration that it could guess,
otherwise it rejects.

The ``otherwise'' seemingly could mean that 
in a non-accepting computation the tree contains some sequences of configurations
that don't terminate, as in the bottom of the picture above.
However, to use these machines as language deciders 
we shall want to time them, 
including how long they take to not accept.
So we shall require of nondeterministic language deciders that  
their computation trees have no infinite paths.

%<*def:NondeterministicDecidersAllBranchesFinite>
\begin{definition} \label{def:NondeterministicDecides}
A nondeterministic Turing machine $\TM$ \definend{accepts} an input string
if its computation tree has at least one maximal path 
that ends with a configuration having an accepting state,
otherwise it \definend{rejects} that input.
Let $\TM$ be such that for any
input $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
the computation tree has no infinite path.
Then it
\definend{decides} the language~$\lang$\index{decided language!of a nondeterministic Turing machine}\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!decided language} 
when 
if $\sigma\in\lang$ then $\TM$ accepts it,
while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then~$\TM$ rejects it.\footnotemark
\end{definition}\footnotetext{%
  Recall how we have been handling having deterministic Turing machines act
  as language deciders.
  In line with our approach that these machines compute functions,
  we have taken it to mean that for any string input,
  the machine halts and signals
  `Accept' or `Reject' by having $1$ or $0$ on the tape.}
%</def:NondeterministicDecidersAllBranchesFinite>

% \noindent (The point of the `no infinite sequence of configurations' 
% requirement is to ensure that we know in a finite time when a 
% string is rejected.)

With Finite State machines, nondeterminism does not make a
difference in that a language is decided by 
some nondeterministic Finite State machine if and only if it is 
decided by a deterministic Finite State machine.
Pushdown machines are the opposite since there are languages that
a nondeterministic Pushdown machine can decide but that no deterministic one
can. 
What about Turing machines?
First we need a technical result.

\begin{lemma}
If a computation tree has no infinite sequence of configurations then
it contains only finitely many nodes. 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In a computation tree, every vertex has only finitely many immediate 
successors because the number of machine instructions is finite.
K\"{o}nig's Lemma\index{Konig's lemma@K\"{o}nig's lemma} 
on \zcpageref{lem:KonigsLemma} says that for such a 
graph to have infinitely many vertices, it must have an infinite path.
\end{proof}

%<*lem:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>
\begin{theorem}  \label{lem:NonDeterAndDeterTMsHaveSameLangs}
Deterministic Turing machines and nondeterministic Turing machines 
decide the same set of languages.
% They also recognize the same languages.
\end{theorem}
%</lem:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>

%<*pf:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>
\begin{proof}
One direction is easy.
A deterministic Turing machine is a special case of a
nondeterministic one. 
So if a language is decided by a deterministic machine
then it is decided by a nondeterministic one also.

For the converse let the nondeterministic Turing
machine~$\TM$ decide the language.
Fix an input~$\sigma$.
Consider a deterministic machine~$\TM[Q]$ that 
does a breadth-first search of $\TM$'s computation tree.
If $\TM$ accepts~$\sigma$ then there is a terminal configuration 
in the tree where the state is accepting, and~$\TM[Q]$ will find it.
If $\TM$ does not accept~$\sigma$ then every terminal configuration
has a rejecting state.
In a nondeterministic decider the computation tree has only
finitely many vertices so the search will
eventually exhaust all of them, and then $\TM[Q]$ rejects~$\sigma$.
\end{proof}
%</pf:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>

That proof is, basically, time-slicing.
The computers that we have on our desks
simulate an unboundedly-parallel machine
by having the CPU cycle among processes.
For example, if we are editing a
program and also showing a video
then the computer may update the editor for a time, 
the update the video, then switch back to the editor, etc.
This is a kind of dovetailing, 
a way of doing a breadth-first traversal of the computation tree.





% ..................
\subsection{Speed}
The excitement about 
nondeterministic Turing machines is that they
\citetext{might be much faster}{%
  R~Hamming gives this example to demonstrate that the change of even
  a single order of magnitude 
  in speed can change who work gets done:~we 
  walk at $4$~mph, 
  we drive at $40$~mph, 
  and we fly at~$400$~mph. 
  This relates to the picture that opens this chapter.  
} than deterministic ones.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:SatisfiabilityfastOnNDTM}
The \probname{Satisfiability} problem\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem!on a nondeterministic Turing machine}
inputs a Propositional Logic expression and determines whether there 
are truth values for the variables that make
the expression evaluate to~\true\!.

%<*discussion:SpeedOfNFSMi>
Is this propositional logic expression satisfiable?
\begin{equation*}
 E=
 (P\vee Q)\wedge (P\vee \neg Q)\wedge (\neg P\vee Q)
  \wedge (\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R) \wedge (Q\vee R)
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*} 
The natural approach is to compute a truth table.
The one below shows that~$E$ is satisfiable because the $\true\true\false$ row 
ends in~$\true\!$.
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\vee Q$ &$P\vee \neg Q$ &$\neg P\vee Q$ &$\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R$ &$Q\vee R$ &\raisebox{0.75pt}{($*$)} \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
%</discussion:SpeedOfNFSMi>
\end{example}
%<*discussion:SpeedOfNFSMii>
The number of table rows grows  
exponentially since it is $2$ raised to the number of input variables.
Going through the rows sequentially is very slow.
%</discussion:SpeedOfNFSMii>

%<*discussion:SpeedOfNFSMiii>
Each row is easy; it is just that 
there are lots of rows.
This is perfectly suited to an unboundedly parallel machine.
For each line we could fork a child process that would
finish in polytime
and if they are working in parallel then the whole thing is polytime.
If at the end any child found a \true{} then 
the expression is satisfiable.
In short, while a serial machine appears to require
exponential time, a nondeterministic machine does the job in polytime.
%</discussion:SpeedOfNFSMiii>

\begin{example}
The 
\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem}
is similar.
Searching through every graph circuit in turn appears to be slow.
But a nondeterministic machine 
could do all of the circuits in parallel, or 
just guess a circuit that meets the bound\Dash
or get it from some oracular demon\Dash and then verify it.
That would be fast. 
\end{example}

So while adding nondeterminism to Turing machines doesn't
compute anything new, we can reasonably 
conjecture that it computes faster.




\subsection{Definition}
Next we define the most important class of language decision problems
associated with nondeterministic Turing machines.

%<*def:NP>
\begin{definition} \label{def:NP} % https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/2887/50343
The complexity 
class~\definend{$\compclass{NP}$}\index{NP@$\NP$}\index{complexity class!NP@$\NP$} 
is the set of languages for which there is a 
nondeterministic Turing machine decider~$\TM$ that runs
in polytime, 
meaning that there is a polynomial~$p$ such that on input $\sigma$,
all maximal paths of $\TM$'s computation 
terminate in at most~$p(\lh{\sigma})$ steps.
\end{definition}
%</def:NP>

%<*lem:PSubsetNP>
The first result is immediate because 
a deterministic Turing machine is a special case
of a nondeterministic one.

\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:PSubsetNP}
$\P\subseteq\NP$ 
\end{lemma}
%</lem:PSubsetNP>

Very important:~no one knows whether $\P$ is a strict subset. 
That is, no one knows whether 
$\P\neq\NP$ or $\P=\NP$.\index{P versus NP@$\P$ versus $\NP$}
% This is the biggest open problem in the Theory of Computing and
We will say much more in later sections.
\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeansPNP]{The two possibilities: $\P\subset\NP$
  and $\P=\NP$}
  % \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  % \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2004.pdf}}
  \hspace{5em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2005.pdf}}
\end{fig}

A pattern in mathematical presentations is to have
a definition that is conceptually clear, 
followed by a result that makes the definition easier to apply.
We now give that second one.
%<*intro:Verifieri>

This is where we use the mental model
of the machine guessing or of being given a hint.
Consider again our touchstone problem, \probname{Satisfiability}.
Imagine that in \zcref{ex:SatisfiabilityfastOnNDTM} above
we are given the expression and the demon whispers, 
``Psst! Try $\str{TTF}$.'' 
With this we can verify the expression's satisfiability in polytime using
a deterministic machine (so achieving polytime does not require
nondeterminism if we are simply verifying).
The verifier below inputs~$\sigma$, a string representation of a
Boolean expression,
and also a hint~$\omega$, representing
a line of $\sigma$'s truth table, that witnesses
$\sigma$'s satisfiability.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts000.pdf}}
  \tag{$**$}
\end{equation*}
If $\sigma$ is satisfiable then  
there is a suitable~$\omega$ that~$\TM[V]$
can check gives a result of~\true\!.
% For the expression in \zcref{ex:SatisfiabilityfastOnNDTM}'s~($*$) from above, 
% $\omega=\str{\true\true\false}$.
(Clearly this check runs in polytime.)
But if $\sigma$ is not satisfiable, such as  
if $\sigma=P\wedge\neg P$, then there is no $\omega$
that will cause $\TM[V]$ to accept.
% It inputs $\sigma=E$ from~($*$)
% and also the hint $\omega=\str{TTF}$.
% It reaches `\texttt{Accept}' in polytime.
%</intro:Verifier>

%<*def:Verifier>
\begin{definition}  \label{def:Verifier}
A \definend{verifier}\index{verifier}\index{language!verifier} 
for a language~$\lang$
is a deterministic Turing machine~$\TM[V]$ that inputs
$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}\in\B^2$ and is such that 
$\sigma\in\lang$
if and only if
there exists an~$\omega$ so that   % "there exists" wording needed because it is referenced below
$\TM[V]$ accepts $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
The string $\omega$ is the 
\definend{witness}\index{witness}
or \definend{certificate}.\index{certificate}
\end{definition}
%</def:Verifier>

%<*lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv>
\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
A language is in $\NP$ 
if and only
if it has a verifier that runs in time polynomial in 
$\lh{\sigma}$.\index{verifier!polytime}
That is, $\lang\in\NP$
if and only if there is a polynomial~$p$
and a deterministic Turing machine~$\TM[V]$ that halts on all 
inputs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
in $p(\sizeof{\sigma})$ time, and is such that
$\sigma\in\lang$ exactly when there is at least one witness~$\omega$
where $\TM[V]$ accepts
$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv>

% So to show that a language~$\lang$ is in~$\NP$, we will produce a 
% verifier~$\TM[V]\!$. 
% It takes as input
% a candidate for language membership~$\sigma$
% and a witness~$\omega$.
% If $\sigma\in\lang$ then the verifier will use the witness to confirm that
% in polytime.
% If $\sigma\notin\lang$ then no witness can cause the verifier to 
% falsely report that $\sigma$ is in the language.
% If our verifier runs in polytime then $\lang\in\NP$.

Before the proof we will comment on
some aspects of both the definition and lemma, and give a few examples.

% \begin{example}
%<*ex:SATIsNPi>
First, the lemma elucidates something about
$\NP$\kern+0.5pt:~while $\P$ contains problems 
where we can deterministically find the answer
in polytime, $\NP$ contains the problems where we can at least deterministically 
verify answers in polytime.
%</ex:SATIsNPi>

%<*disc:Vefieri>
The second comment is to acknowledge that
the most striking thing about the definition is that 
it says there exists a witness~$\omega$
but it does not say where that witness comes from.
A person with a computational mindset may well ask, 
``but how will we find the~$\omega$'s?'' 
The point is not how to compute a witness. 
The point of a verifier is that it is a deterministic 
Turing machine that leverages the hint~$\omega$ 
to certify in polytime that $\sigma\in\lang$.
\citetext{The verifier doesn't find the $\omega$'s, it just uses them}{%
  This is like a Mechanical Turk
  \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanical_Turk}
  in that the machine
  $\TM[V]$ does not need the smarts, it is the 
  person, or the demon, who provides that.}.
In a phrase, where the job is verifying we should not be
surprised to simply be given the thing to verify.
%</disc:Vefieri>

%<*disc:Vefierii>
Third,
what's the point of the emphasis that the verification must run in polytime?
If we are given an~$\omega$ then isn't checking always just trivial?
Imagine for contrast that a demon hands you some papers and claims
that they contain an unbeatable
\citetext{strategy for chess}{% 
  Chess is known to be a solvable game.
  This is Zermelo's Theorem \autocite{wiki:ZermelosTheorem}\Dash there 
  is a strategy for one of the two players that forces a win or a draw, no
  matter how the opponent plays}.
Verifying requires stepping through
the responses to each move, and responses to the
responses, etc.
It appears to
\citetext{require exponential time}{%
  In the terminology of a later section, chess is known to be 
  $\EXP$ complete.
  See \autocite{Fraenkel}.}.
That would make
the demon's papers, 
\citetext{in a sense, useless}{%
  Being given an answer with no accompanying justification is a problem.
  This is like the Feynman algorithm for doing Physics: 
  ``The student asks \ldots{} what are Feynman's methods? 
  [M]~Gell-Mann leans coyly against the blackboard and says: Dick's 
  method is this. You write down the problem. You think very hard. 
  (He shuts his eyes and presses his knuckles parodically to his forehead.) 
  Then you write down the answer.''
  \autocite{Gleick}
  It is also like the mathematician S~Ramanujan, who stated that
  the advanced formulas that he produced came in
  dreams from the god Narasimha.
  Some of these formulas were startling and amazing, but some of them
  were wrong. \autocite{IndiaToday}
  Another such story has to do with G~Hardy about to board a ferry crossing 
  rough seas from Denmark to Britain.  
  He sent a postcard to another mathematician stating that he had 
  solved the Riemann hypothesis
  (this is still one of the most famous unproven hypothesis in mathematics).
  And of course, the most famous example of a failure to provide backing
  is Fermat writing in a book he was reading that that there are 
  no nontrivial instances of
  $x^n+y^n\neq z^n$ for $n>2$ and then saying, 
  ``I have discovered a truly marvelous proof of this, 
  which this margin is too narrow to contain.''}.
So we require that the verification be tractable.
%</disc:Vefierii>

%<*disc:Vefieriii>
Fourth, if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then the definition
does not require a witness to that.
Instead, what's required is that 
from among all possible strings~$\omega$ 
there is none
such that the verifier accepts $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.\footnote{%
  In light of the necessity of verification,
  perhaps a better term for $\omega$ is
  ``potential witness'' or ``proposed witness.'' 
  But those are not standard.}
%</disc:Vefieriii>

%<*disc:Vefieriv>
This is an asymmetry between establishing membership in a language
and establishing non-membership.
Consider \probname{Satisfiability} again.
If a propositional logic expression~$\sigma$ is
satisfiable then a witness to that points to 
a line of the truth table.
But for non-satisfiability 
there is no natural witness;~instead, the natural thing is to check all 
lines.
As far as we know today,
verifying that a Boolean formula is not satisfiable takes more than polytime. 
Consonant with this, it does not appear that 
$\prob\in\NP$ implies that its
complement~$\setcomp{\prob}$ is a member of~$\NP$\Dash 
where the class 
\compclass{co$\NP$}\index{coNP@\compclass{co$\NP$}} 
contains the complements of languages from $\NP$,
we suspect that $\NP\neq\compclass{coNP}$.
%</disc:Vefieriv>

%<*disc:Vefiervi>
Finally, note that
the lemma requires that the verifier runs in time polynomial in the length
of $\sigma$,
not polynomial in the length of its input, $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
If it said the latter then we could check 
the demon's chess strategy just by using a witness that is exponentially long, 
which would make $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ exponentially long.
In addition, observe that assuming that $\TM[V]$ runs in time polynomial in 
$\lh{\sigma}$ implies that any successful  witness's length
is at most polynomial in $\lh{\sigma}$, because
with $\omega$'s that are too long 
\citetext{the verifier cannot even input them before its runtime bound expires}{%
  Some authors instead define that the 
  verifier runs in time polynomial in its input, $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, 
  and add the explicit restriction that $\sizeof{\omega}$ 
  must be polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.}.
%</disc:Vefiervi>

% In summary, the definition says that if $\sigma\in\lang$ 
% then $\omega$ certifies 
% that and the verifier deterministically validates the certificate in polytime,
% while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no certificate that the 
% verifier can validate.

%<*ex:HamPathIsNPi>
\begin{example}\label{ex:HamPathIsNP}
The 
\definend{\probname{Hamiltonian Path}} problem\index{Hamiltonian Path problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Path} problem} 
is like the
\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem\index{Hamiltonian Circuit problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem} 
except that 
instead of requiring that the starting vertex equals the ending one,
it inputs two vertices. 
It is the problem of determining membership in this set. 
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}\suchthat 
             \text{some path in~$\mathcal{G}$ between $v$ and~$\hat{v}$ visits every vertex exactly once}}
\end{equation*}
To show that $\lang$ is in~$\NP$
the lemma asks for a verifier~$\TM[V]$\!.
It is sketched below.
The summary with technical details omitted
is:~for $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}$, take the witness
to be a path between those vertices that contains every vertex.

As this is the first example we will give the details a run-through.
Strictly speaking, the language is not a set of triples, it is a set of strings
which represent those triples with reasonable efficiency.
The $\sigma$ is a string that is a  
candidate  for membership in~$\lang$, and so
represents some $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}$.
The~$\omega$ is a string representing a path, 
$\sequence{v,v_1,\ldots{}\, \hat{v}}$.
(In later examples and exercises we will
downplay the representation aspect, for instance just saying something like that
$\sigma$ is the triple.)
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts001.pdf}}
\end{center}
%</ex:HamPathIsNPi>

%<*ex:HamPathIsNPii>
There are two possibilities for an input pair that begins with~$\sigma$.
If $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}$ has a Hamiltonian path then
the point of the choice of witness is that we use
that path as~$\omega$, so that $\TM[V]$ can accept its input.
Verification in the if-then box takes $\TM[V]$ time that is polynomial
in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.
(The crux of the argument for that is:~if $\mathcal{G}$ has
$n$ vertices then the path has $n-1$ edges.
So verifying that each vertex appears in the path once and only once requires at
most $n\cdot(n-1)\in\bigOh(n^2)$ checks.
Because that is polynomial in the number of vertices, it is polynomial
in the size of the representation, $\sizeof{\sigma}$.)
On the other hand, if $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}$ has
no Hamiltonian path then there is no witness~$\omega$ 
that will verify, and~$\TM[V]$ will not accept any input pair
starting with~$\sigma$.
\end{example}
%</ex:HamPathIsNPii>

%<*ex:CompositeIsNP>
\begin{example}
The \probname{Primality} problem\index{Primality problem@\probname{Primality} problem}
asks whether a given number has nontrivial factors,
$\lang=\set{n\in\N^+\suchthat \text{$n$ has a divisor~$d$ with $1<d<n$}}$.
To show that $\lang\in\NP$
we construct a verifier~$\TM[V]$.
The idea is that its first input  
is a number and
its second, the witness, is a divisor of that number.
If~$\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a suitable~$\omega$,
while if $\sigma\notin\lang$
then no $\omega$ will make $\TM[V]$ accept its input. % (see below).
The verification can run in polytime.
(Briefly, that's because integer division is polytime in the
number of digits in the integers, and so in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.)
% \begin{center}
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts016.pdf}}
% \end{center}
% \footnote{%
%   We saw on \zcpageref{prob:Composite} that determining membership in
%   $\lang$ is in~$\P$.
%   Since $\P\subseteq\NP$ this fact is another proof that $\lang\in\NP$.}
\end{example}
%</ex:CopositeIsNP>


We are ready for the proof
of \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}.

\begin{proof} \label{proof:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
%<*pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivi>
  For one direction suppose
  that the language~$\lang$ is decided by a nondeterministic
Turing machine~$\TM$ in polytime.
We will construct a deterministic verifier~$\TM[V]$ that runs in polytime.
Since $\lang$ is decided by $\TM$ there is a
polynomial $\map{p}{\N}{\N}$  
such that for any input~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, all maximal paths of 
$\TM$'s computation tree terminate in at most
$p(|\sigma|)$ steps.
If $\sigma\in\lang$ then we can use
an accepting maximal path to create a witness to $\sigma$'s acceptance,
for instance as in the sequence $\omega=\sequence{3,2\ldots}$ meaning, 
``in the computation tree, at the first node take the third child, 
then take the second child of that, 
etc.''
More precisely, $\TM$ has
a finite number of states~$k$
so we can represent the accepting branch with 
a sequence~$\omega$ of at most $p(\sizeof{\sigma})$ many numbers,
each less than~$k$.
In total, $\omega$'s length is polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$. 
With this $\omega$, 
a deterministic machine~$\TM[V]$ can verify $\TM$'s
acceptance of~$\sigma$, in polynomial time.
%</pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivi>

%<*pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivii>
For the converse suppose that the language $\lang$
is accepted by a verifier~$\hat{\TM[V]}$ that runs in time
bounded by a polynomial~$q$.
We will construct a nondeterministic Turing machine~$\hat{\TM}$ 
that in polytime  accepts an input bitstring~$\hat{\sigma}$
if and only if $\hat{\sigma}\in\lang$.

The key is that $\hat{\TM}$ is nondeterministic.
Given a candidate~$\hat{\sigma}$ for membership in the 
language, 
(1)~have $\hat{\TM}$ nondeterministically produce a witness~$\hat{\omega}$
(of length less than $q(\sizeof{\hat{\sigma}})$),
(2)~have $\hat{\TM}$ then run  $\sequence{\hat{\sigma},\hat{\omega}}$ 
through $\hat{\TM[V]}$,
and
(3)~if the verifier accepts its input then $\hat{\TM}$ accepts~$\hat{\sigma}$, 
while if $\TM[V]$ does not accept then $\hat{\TM}$ rejects~$\hat{\sigma}$.

We must check that  $\hat{\TM}$ accepts $\hat{\sigma}$
if and only if $\hat{\sigma}\in\lang$. 
A nondeterministic machine accepts a string 
if and only if there exists some maximal path that accepts the string. 
Suppose first that $\hat{\sigma}\in\lang$.
Because $\hat{\TM[V]}$ is a verifier, in this case 
there exists a witness~$\hat{\omega}$ 
(of length less than $q(\sizeof{\hat{\sigma}})$)
that will 
result in $\hat{\TM[V]}$ accepting $\sequence{\hat{\sigma},\hat{\omega}}$,
so there is a way for the prior paragraph to result in
acceptance of~$\hat{\sigma}$, and so $\hat{\TM}$ accepts~$\hat{\sigma}$.
Conversely, suppose that $\hat{\sigma}\notin\lang$.
By the definition of a verifier, no witness~$\hat{\omega}$ will result in 
$\hat{\TM[V]}$ accepting $\sequence{\hat{\sigma},\hat{\omega}}$,
and thus $\hat{\TM}$ rejects~$\hat{\sigma}$.
%</pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivii>
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
A common reaction to the second half of that proof is,
``In (1) the machine $\hat{\TM}$ plucks $\hat{\omega}$ out of 
the air?
\citetext{How is that legal?}{%
  This is reminiscent of 
  Quantum Bogosort,\protect\index{Bogosort}\protect\index{Quantum Bogosort} 
  a facetious sorting algorithm.
  Given an unordered list of length~$n$, 
  it uses a quantum source of randomness to 
  generate a permutation of $n$.
  It reorders the input according to that permutation.
  If the list is now sorted then good.
  If not then the algorithm destroys the entire universe.
  Assuming the Many World's Hypothesis, the result is that in any
  surviving universe the list has been sorted in linear time.}''
This reaction\Dash an objection that nondeterminism is not realistic\Dash is 
common and reasonable so we will address it.

The first response is formal.
We've given a definition,
\zcref{def:Verifier}, that the candidate $\hat{\sigma}$ is accepted
if and only if there is an~$\hat{\omega}$.
The proof covers the two
possibilities:~if $\hat{\sigma}\in\lang$ 
then there is such an $\hat{\omega}$ and otherwise
there is not, so the definition is satisfied.
True, ``nondeterministically produce[s] a witness''
is provocative, but this language is common in the literature 
(we can take 
`nondeterministically produce[s]' to 
mean either that it uses unbounded parallelism to run through all 
possible $\hat{\omega}$'s, or that 
it guesses $\hat{\omega}$ or gets it from a demon.)

The second response is broader, putting
nondeterministic machines in a setting.
We today do not have physical devices 
bearing the same relationship to nondeterministic machine models 
that everyday computers bear to deterministic ones.
(We can write a program to simulate nondeterministic behavior
but no device does it natively.)
When Turing formulated his definition there were no
physical computers but they were coming;~will
we someday have nondeterministic devices?
We don't know of any candidates.\footnote{%
  A caution about quantum computers: \index{quantum computing} 
  subatomic particles can be in a superposition of many states at once.
  \protect\citetext{Some popularizations wrongly suggest}{%
    A good first take is in the \protect\href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-UrdExQW0cs}{Veritasium video \textit{Exposing Why Quantum Computers Are Already A Threat}}}
  that quantum computers can therefore try all potential
  solutions in parallel,
  which makes them sound like nondeterministic machines.
  But, that we know of, these popularizations are mistaken.
  We believe that we cannot get information on individual parts
  out of a quantum system and instead must use interference.} %
(There have been proposals  
involving such things as time travel through wormholes
but we put them aside as speculative.)
However that doesn't make thinking about nondeterministic Turing machines 
a purely academic exercise.\footnote{%
  Not that there is anything wrong with academic exercises.}
% The model of nondeterministic Turing machines
% has proven to be very fruitful.
Nondeterministic machines are practical in that 
the problems that are associated with
these machines, the members of $\NP$, are eminently practical. 
Computer scientists have been trying to 
find fast solutions to many of them since computers have existed.
More evidence of their practicality
is that \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} gives an equivalence between
questions about nondeterministic machines and
questions about deterministic ones, the verifiers.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary} \label{co:NPProbsTakeEXPtime}
  Every problem in $\NP$ can be solved on a deterministic machine
  in exponential time.  
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\lang\in\NP$.
There is a polynomial~$p$
and a deterministic Turing machine~$\TM[V]$ that halts on all 
inputs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
in $p(\sizeof{\sigma})$ time, and is such that
$\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $\TM[V]$ accepts 
$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ for at least one 
witness~$\omega\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
Also, the length of~$\omega$ is bounded by
$p(\sizeof{\sigma})$.

So, solve the $\lang$ problem by 
trying the computation of $\TM[V]$ on
input $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ for all $\omega$'s of length 
at most $p(\sizeof{\sigma})$.
Then $\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if at least one of these inputs 
results in acceptance.
There are at most $b^{p(\sizeof{\sigma})}$ many such $\omega$'s,
where $b=\sizeof{\Sigma}$.
\end{proof}

We close by making a connection with the computably enumerable sets.
The computable sets are those for which there is a Turing machine decider,
while the computably enumerable sets are those for which there is
a recognizer.
The problems in~$\P$ are the ones
where there is a good way to find the solution.
while the problems in $\NP$ as those for which
there is a good way to verify a solution.
So, just as we can think of the computably enumerable sets
as the limit of what can be done in theory,
we cn think of the sets in $\NP$ as the limit of
what can be done feasibly.




% ........................
\begin{exercises}[Some of these require noting that the verifier
  runs in time polynomial in the size of the input problem instance.
  For that, some handy facts are that addition, subtraction, multiplication,
  and division run in time polynomial in the number of digits of the two numbers,
  that in a simple graph the maximum number of edges is polynomial
  in the number of vertices, and that evaluating a Boolean expression
  can be done in time polynomial in the length of that expression.]
  % See https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_complexity_of_mathematical_operations
  
\recommended
\begin{exercise} 
\begin{credit}
  SE user Simone, https://math.stackexchange.com/q/49509
\end{credit}
Someone asks, 
``In \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}, since the witness~$\omega$ is not
required to be effectively computable, 
why can't I just take it to be exactly the problem solution, the bit~\str{1} if
$\sigma\in\lang$, and \str{0} if not?
Then the verifier can just ignore $\sigma$ and follow
the bit.''
They are confused.
Straighten them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
That lemma requires that the verifier have the property 
that if $\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a witness leading to acceptance, 
while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there does not exists a witness that would
cause the verifier to accept.
The proposed verifier doesn't verify, it relies on whatever $\omega$ is.
\begin{display}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts008.pdf}}
\end{display}
Because it does not go through the check,
it will accept an input $\sigma$ if the bit tells it to,
even if $\sigma$ is not in the language.

For a concrete instance, 
consider the problem $\lang=\set{n\in\N\suchthat \text{$n$ is even}}$.
The proposed verifier inputs a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$,
where $\sigma$ is a number and $\omega$ is a bit, and then
accepts the pair if $\omega=\str{1}$.
Absolutely, given the pair $\sequence{5,\str{0}}$,
the verifier will not accept, which is desirable here because $5\notin \lang$.
However, 
given $\sequence{5,\str{1}}$, the verifier will accept.
So, for $\sigma=5$ there exists an~$\omega$ leading to acceptance,
which violates the definition of a verifier.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In the context of all branches being finite,
which is the negation of
`at least one branch accepts'?
\begin{exparts*}
\item Every branch accepts.
\item At least one branch rejects.
\item Every branch rejects.
\item At least one branch fails to reject.
\item None of these.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
It is `Every branch rejects'.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Is it satisfiable?
\begin{exparts*}
\item $(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$
\item $(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
The easiest way to check satisfiability is to make a truth table.
\begin{exparts}
\item The expression $(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$ is satisfiable. 
\begin{center} 
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg Q$ &$\neg Q\wedge R$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item The expression $(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$
is not satisfiable. 
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{cc|ccccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$P\to Q$ &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg P$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P$ &$\neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  &$(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% True or false?  
% If a language is in~$\P$ then it is in~$\NP$.
% \begin{answer}\verified
% True, by \zcref{lem:PSubsetNP}.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Uh-oh.
You find yourself with a nondeterministic Turing machine where
on input~$\sigma$, one maximal path of the computation tree accepts
and one rejects.
Some maximal paths don't end at all.
What does this machine decide about the language?
\begin{answer}\verified
This machine does not decide any language.
To be a decider, a nondeterministic Turing machine's computation tree
must have that on all inputs all maximal paths are finite.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  Your study partner is working through a point,
  ``The proof of \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
  says that the machine `nondeterministically produce[s]' the
  witness~$\hat{\omega}$.
  I don't get this machine-guesses model.
  Suppose that for the input~$\sigma$ the computation tree has ten maximal paths,
  all are finite, and one ends in an accepting configuration.
  So the machine has a one out of ten chance of accepting~$\sigma$?''
  Help them.
  \begin{answer}\verified
    No.
    The definition is:~the machine accepts the input
    if there exists a maximal path that ends in an
    accepting configuration.
    Being a little bit informal or possibly evocative, we say that
    the machine accepts
    its input if there is a way for it to guess a path that triggers acceptance.
    
    That definition means that the two models agree on their conclusions.
    The unboundedly parallel model is about all paths while the machine
    guesses model is about there exists a path, but we need them to
    agree on when a machine accepts its input and when it rejects.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
You get an exercise, \textit{Write a nondeterministic algorithm
that inputs a maze and outputs~\str{1} if there is a path from
the start to the end.}
\begin{exparts*}
\item You hand in an algorithm that does backtracking to find
any possible solution.
Your professor sends it back, and says to try again.
What was wrong?
\item You hand in an algorithm that when it comes to 
a fork in the maze, chooses at random which way to go.
Again you get it back.
What is wrong with this one?
\item Give a correct answer. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Backtracking will solve the problem.
But backtracking is a deterministic algorithm.
(True, a deterministic algorithm is a special case of a nondeterministic
one, but when you ask about it, your professor says, ``The point of this
exercise is to demonstrate understanding of nondeterminism, 
not to quibble.'')
\item Your algorithm chooses, but it does so at random.
If there are paths through the maze, but
the random choices happens not to lead to such a path then 
your algorithm will not detect them.
A nondeterministic algorithm is required to accept the input maze if there
is a maximal path that ends in an accepting configuration
and to not accept the input if there is not.
\item We will give two answers, one using the mental model of
  unbounded parallelism and one using choosing.

For the unbounded parallelism model,
whenever the algorithm finds itself at a place in the maze where it 
can do more than one thing, it forks child processes to handle each 
possibility.
So it may have lots of branches, and lots of them may go to dead ends,
but if there is a way through the maze then this algorithm will find it.
It accepts the input maze if there is evem a single path through
and never accepts when there is not.

The algorithm for choosing will choose.
That is, the machine guesses the path.
(Instead of choosing, we can say that it receives the path~$\omega$
from a demon.)
By definition of the mental model, if there is a way for the machine to guess
right then the machine accepts the input.
(Note again that this algorithm is not guessing at random.
It is guessing correctly.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false?
For the
language~$\set{\sequence{a,b,c}\in\N^3\suchthat a+b=c}$,
the problem of deciding membership 
is in $\compclass{NP}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
True.
By \zcref{lem:PSubsetNP} it suffices to show that the language 
decision problem is in~$\P$.
But that's clear:~given a triple $\sequence{a,b,c}$, checking whether
the first two sum to the third is clearly a polytime task.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Paul Black, \url{https://xlinux.nist.gov/dads/HTML/nondetermAlgo.html}
\end{credit}
Sketch a nondeterministic algorithm to search an array of numbers
for the entry~$k\in\N$, returning $1$ if some array entry equals~$k$
and $0$ if not.
Describe the algorithm both in terms of unbounded parallelism and in terms
of guessing, as discussed on \zcpageref{disc:NondeterminismTMsMentalModels}.
What is its runtime in Big~$\bigOh$?
\begin{answer}\verified
In both models the input is an array $\sequence{a_0,a_1,\ldots{}\, a_{n-1}}$.

In the unbounded parallelism model, the machine forks a child process for
each index $0\leq i< n$.
Child~$i$ checks whether $a_i=k$, and if so signals the machine to 
accept.

The guessing model has the machine guess at an index~$i$,
and if it finds that $a_i=k$ there then
it signals for acceptance. 
Under this model the machine accepts the same arrays as under the other because,
by definition, the machine accepts an input if
there is a maximal path that ends in an accepting configuration, that is, if
at least one guess would be correct.
If the array has a~$k$ entry then there is a way to guess it
and the nondeterministic machine accepts that array.
If the array has no such entry then there is a no way to guess an index
leading to~$k$
and the nondeterministic machine rejects the input array.

On a deterministic machine the runtime is linear,~$\bigOh(n)$.
In both nondeterministic models the runtime is constant,~$\bigOh(1)$.
For instance in the unboundedly parallel model the machine looks in
every entry but not in succession, instead all at the same time.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% A \definend{simple substitution cipher} encrypts text by substituting one
% letter for another.
% Fix a permutation of the capital ASCII letters, for example
% $\sequence{\str{W},\str{P},\ldots}$\sentencespace 
% The cipher is that any
% \str{A} is replaced by the first letter in the permutation, here \str{W}, 
% any \str{B} is replaced by the second letter, etc.
% Assuming that you can recognize a correctly decoded string
% (such as that perhaps it begins with a correctly 
% formatted date),
% a natural algorithm is to try every permutation,
% until the program recognizes the result. 
% Describe implementing this
% \begin{exparts*}
% \item expressed in terms of
% unbounded parallelism,
% and 
% \item in terms of guessing.
% \end{exparts*}

% That is,
% the computation tree has a single root node with one child process for each
% of the $26!$-many permutations. 
% That child takes its single permutation,
% substitutes it, and then checks it.

% child processes that fail the check.
% But one of the substitutions is right, so overall
% we get the correctly decoded string.
% \item
% The guessing model guesses.
% It guesses a permutation (or is given it in a hint from the demon), and then
% it works like any of the prior item's processes:~it makes the substitution
% and then applies the program to check it.
% Under this model of nondeterminism
% it just needs to be possible to have a guess that checks out.
% \end{exparts}
% \end{answer}

\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Linear Programming} problem
is described on page~\pageref{prob:LinearProgramming}.\index{Linear Programming language decision problem@\probname{Linear Programming} language decision problem}
The related problem \definend{\probname{Integer Linear Programming}}\index{Integer Linear Programming problem@\probname{Integer Linear Programming} problem}
also seeks to maximize a linear objective function
$F(x_0,\ldots\, x_n)=d_0x_0+\cdots+d_nx_n$, subject to linear constraints
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_n\leq b_i$ (or $\geq b_i$), 
but with the restriction that all of~$x_j$, $d_j$, $b_j$, and $a_{i,j}$
are integers. 
Recast it as a parametrized family of language decision problems.
Sketch a nondeterministic algorithm,
giving both an unbounded parallelism formulation and a guessing formulation.
\begin{answer}\verified
  To recast,
  we can denote each language decision problem as a tuple
  \begin{equation*}
    \sequence{d_0,\ldots\,d_n,a_{0,0},\ldots\,a_{0,n},b_0,\ldots\, a_{m,0},\ldots\,a_{m,n},b_m}
    \qquad\text{each number is an integer}
  \end{equation*}
  representing that $F(x_0,\ldots\, x_n)=d_0x_0+\cdots+d_nx_n\geq B$
  and that
  $a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_n\leq b_i$ for $0\leq i\leq m$.
  (Observe that we can represent a `$\geq$' constraint with negative numbers.)

  An unboundedly parallel algorithm tries all feasible assignments
  of integers to the variable $x_j$'s.
  (An assignment is `feasible' if it satisfies all of the constraints.)
  If there is a satisfying assignment then this algorithm will find it
  and accept the input tuple.
  If there is no satisfying assignment then there is no way for
  the input to get accepted.

  A guessing algorithm guesses an assignment $\sequence{x_0,\ldots\,x_n}$.
  If there is a satisfying assignment then there is a way to guess it,
  and so by the definition of machine acceptance under this model, the machine
  accepts the input tuple as a member of the language~$\lang_B$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The 
\definend{\probname{Semiprime}} problem\index{Semiprime problem@\probname{Semiprime} problem} 
inputs a number $n\in\N$ and decides if its prime
factorization has exactly two primes, $n=p_0^{e_0}p_1^{e_1}$ where
$e_0,e_1>0$.
State it as a language decision problem.
Sketch a nondeterministic solution algorithm that runs in polytime.
Give an unbounded parallelism formulation and a guessing formulation.
\begin{answer}\verified
The language is 
$\lang=\set{n\in\N\suchthat \text{$n$'s prime factorization is $n=p_0^{e_0}p_1^{e_1}$ for $e_0,e_1>0$ and $p_0\neq p_1$ primes}}$.

An unbounded parallelism algorithm tries each
conceivable prime factorization, simultaneously
(by `conceivable' we mean that it doesn't include any primes greater
than the input number).
For instance, given the input~$8$, it could check the 
products $2^03^05^07^0$\!, $2^13^05^07^0$\!, \ldots{}
$2^73^75^77^7$\!, to see whether any of them equals~$8$ and 
exactly two primes have nonzero exponents.
Multiplying is fast and checking whether a number is composite or
prime can also be done in polytime.
Thus each of these checks happens in polytime.
Since the machine runs them all at the same time, overall this runs in polytime.

A guessing algorithm is that the machine inputs the number~$n$
and guesses 
a two-prime factorization, or is given one by a demon.
For instance, by the definition of this model the machine accepts
the input $45$, because it could guess $3^{2}5^{1}\!$.
If the input is not a semiprime then there is no way to guess a
suitable factorization.
Clearly this runs in polytime.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\exerciseinstructions{
These instruction apply through \zcref{exer:PartitionisNP}:
\begin{exparts*}
\item State it as a language decision problem,
\item Describe the key idea:~given the problem instance~$\sigma$, what is
  the witness~$\omega$?
\item Sketch a flowchart for the verifier.
\item Use \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
  to show that the language is in~$\NP$.
\item In a sentence or two describe why the witness verification runs in
  time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.
\end{exparts*}
(As \zcref{ex:HamPathIsNP} notes, answers should suppress the 
detail that $\sigma$ and $\omega$ are string representations.)}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}\label{exer:GraphColoringisNP}
  Consider the
  \definend{\probname{Graph Coloring}}\index{Graph Coloring problem@\probname{Graph Coloring} problem}
  problem that inputs a planar graph and
  a bound $B\in\N$, and decides whether the graph is $B$-colorable.
  Show that it is in $\NP$.
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
    The language is a straightforward translation of the problem,
    $\lang=
    \set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
      \text{$\mathcal{G}$ is planar and is $B$ colorable}}$.
  \item Take the witness to be a coloring of the given graph with $B$-many
    colors.
  \item\
    \begin{display}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts011.pdf}}%   
    \end{display}
  \item
    \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} needs a deterministic verifier.
    Its first input $\sigma$ is a
    pair~$\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$ of a graph and a nonzero natural number.
    Its second input $\omega$ is the $B$-coloring of $\mathcal{G}$;~we
    can think of this as the machine's guess, or the demon's hint.

    If the graph has a $B$-coloring then there exists a suitable witness and
    the verifier~$\TM[V]$ can check that
    using time polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.
    If the graph does not have a $B$-coloring then then
    no $\omega$ will cause the verifier to accept its input.
  \item
    Where $\mathcal{V}$ is the set of vertices of
    $\mathcal{G}$, there are at most $\sizeof{\mathcal{V}}$ choose~$2$
    many edges to check.
    That's $\bigOh(\sizeof{\mathcal{V}}^2)$, which is
    $\bigOh(\sizeof{\mathcal{G}}^2)$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Recall that we recast the \probname{Traveling Salesman}\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem} optimization problem
as a language decision problem for a parametrized family of languages.
Show that each such $\lang_B$ is in $\NP$ by following the instructions
before \zcref{exer:GraphColoringisNP}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}%
    \item $\lang_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat
        \text{$\mathcal{G}$ is a weighted graph with
          a circuit of length less than~$B$}}$
    \item The witness is a circuit of weight no more than than the bound~$B$.
    \item\ 
      \begin{display}
        \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts013.pdf}}%   
      \end{display}
    \item \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} needs a 
      deterministic Turing machine verifier $\TM[V]$.
      It must input pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
      There, $\sigma$ is a weighted graph~$\mathcal{G}$
      that is a candidate for membership in the language, 
      and $\omega$ is a witness circuit of total cost no more than~$B$.
      If $\sigma\in\lang_B$ then there a circuit witness~$\omega$
      so that~$\TM[V]$ accepts the input pair,
      while if $\sigma\notin\lang_B$
      then there is no~$\omega$ that will verify and
      so $\TM[V]$ does not accept any input pair.
    \item If the graph has $n$ vertices then a circuit has $n-1$ edges.
      Checking that the vertices inside the circuit are different takes
      $n$ steps, and adding the edge weights is linear in the number
      of edges and the number of digits in the weights.
      So verification is polytime in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Independent Sets}\index{Independent Sets problem@\probname{Independent Sets} problem}
problem starts with a graph and a natural number~$n$ and
decides whether in the graph there are $n$-many independent vertices,
that is, vertices that are not connected by an edge.
Show that it is in $\NP$.
Follow the instructions before \zcref{exer:GraphColoringisNP}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item $\lang=
    \set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},n}\suchthat
      \text{the graph~$\mathcal{G}$ has at least $n$ independent vertices}}$
  \item Given a graph and a natural number the witness is an independent set
    of graph vertices.
  \item\
    \begin{display}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts014.pdf}}%   
    \end{display}
  \item   \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} requires that we produce a 
    deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
    Its first input $\sigma$ is a graph and natural number pair,
    $\sequence{\mathcal{G},n}$. 
    Its second input~$\omega$ is an $n$~element independent set of
    $\mathcal{G}$'s vertices.
    
    The verifier checks 
    that the vertices are in the graph and that no two of them are connected.
    Those checks take time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.
    
    If the pair $\sequence{\mathcal{G},n}$ is indeed an element of~$\lang$
    then there is such a witness~$\omega$
    and so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
    that the  verifier accepts.
    If $\sequence{\mathcal{G},n}$ is not an element of~$\lang$
    then there is no such $\omega$ and so there does not exist a pair that the 
    verifier will accept.
  \item The verifier must check $\omega$'s 
    vertices pairwise against the edges in the graph.
    The number of graph edges is polynomial in the number of vertices,
    which makes it polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The \definend{\probname{Longest Path}} problem\index{Longest Path problem@\probname{Longest Path} problem}
inputs a graph and a bound, $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$, 
and determines whether the graph contains a simple path
of length at least~$B\in\N$.
(A path is simple if no two of its vertices are equal).
Show that this problem is in $\NP$
by following the instructions before \zcref{exer:GraphColoringisNP}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item $\lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
      \text{$\mathcal{G}$ has a simple path of length at least $B$}}$
  \item For a specified graph and bound, the witness is a path of length
    no less than the bound.
  \item\ 
    \begin{display}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts017.pdf}}%   
    \end{display}
  \item \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} wants a 
    deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
    It inputs pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
    $\sigma=\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$ and
    $\omega=\sequence{v_0,\ldots{}\,v_{n-1}}$ is a simple path
    with no fewer than $B$ edges.
    
    The verifier checks 
    that the path is in~$\mathcal{G}$, that no two vertices are equal, 
    and the the length at least~$B$.
    These checks can be done in time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.
    If $\sigma$ is indeed an element of~$\lang$ then there is such a
    path~$\omega$, so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
    that the  verifier accepts.
    If $\sigma$ is not an element of~$\lang$ then there is no such 
    $\omega$ and so there does not exist a pair that the 
    verifier accepts.
  \item Where the path $\omega$ has length~$n-1$, checking that the vertices
    are unequal can be done by traversing the path, so that has a
    runtime of $\bigOh(n)$, which is clearly polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:ThreeDMatchingisNP}
  The \probname{Three Dimensional Matching} problem is:~given\index{Three Dimensional Matching problem@\probname{Three Dimensional Matching} problem}
  three sets $X,Y,Z$ that each
  have~$n$ elements, and also given as input a set of those triples 
  $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$,
  decide if there is an $n$-element subset $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ so that
  no two of its elements agree on their first coordinates, or second, or third.
  Show it is in $\NP$.
  Follow the instructions before \zcref{exer:GraphColoringisNP}.
  \begin{answer}
    \begin{exparts}
    \item The language is the collection of sets $M$ that contain triples
      $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$ where
      $\sizeof{X}=\sizeof{Y}=\sizeof{Z}=n$ for some $n\in\N^+$\!,
      and $M$ has a subset $\hat{M}$ of size~$n$ such that no
      two of its triples agree on any if their three coordinates.
    \item Given $\sigma=M$, take $\omega=\hat{M}$ if one exists.
    \item\
      \begin{display}
            \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts009.pdf}}%
      \end{display}
    \item The verifier
      input~$\sigma$ is a set of triples~$M$
      whose first coordinates are limited to some size~$n$ set~$X$,
      whose second coordinates are limited to a size~$n$ set~$Y$,
      and whose third coordinates are limited to a size~$n$ set~$Z$.
      It also inputs the witness~$\omega$ that
      is $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ satisfying the condition
      (we can think of this
      as the nondeterministic machine's guess or as the demon's hint).
      
      If $\sigma=M$ has such a sub-multiset~$\hat{M}$ then  
      verifying that $\omega$ works
      can be done in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.
      So in this case there is an input pair beginning with~$\sigma$
      that the verifier accepts.
      If there is no such~$\hat{M}$ for the input $\sigma=M$
      then for no~$\omega$ will the verifier find that it checks out, so
      it will not accept any input pairs.
    \item Checking that no two triples have the same first
      coordinate requires $\sizeof{\hat{M}}$ choose~$2$ comparisons,
      which is $\bigOh(\sizeof{\hat{M}}^2)$.
      To check all three coordinates takes three times as many steps, again
      $\bigOh(\sizeof{\hat{M}}^2)$.
      Because $\hat{M}\subseteq M$, that's
      $\bigOh(\sizeof{\sigma}^2)$.
    \end{exparts}    
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\label{exer:PartitionisNP}
  The \probname{Partition} problem\index{Partition problem@\probname{Partition} problem} 
  starts with a finite multiset~$A$ of natural numbers,
  decide if~$A$ splits into sub-multisets
  $\hat{A},A-\hat{A}$ whose elements total to the same number,
  $\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}a=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}a$.
  Prove that it is in $\NP$
  by following the instructions before \zcref{exer:GraphColoringisNP}.
  \begin{answer}
    \begin{exparts}
    \item $\lang=\set{A\suchthat
        \text{$A$ is a multiset of natural numbers 
          with a sub-multiset $\hat{A}\subseteq A$ such that 
          $\textstyle \sum_{a\in\hat{A}}a=\sum_{a\in A-\hat{A}}a$}}$
    \item The witness $\omega$ is the sub-multiset~$\hat{A}$.
    \item\
      \begin{display}
        \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts010.pdf}}%  
      \end{display}
      It is on the right of
      \figureref{fig:ThreeDMatchingAndPartitionAreNP}.
    \item \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} needs a deterministic verifier.
      It inputs $\sigma$, the problem instance~$M$,
      and~$\omega$, the witness~$\hat{A}$
      (we can think of this
      as the nondeterministic machine's guess or as the demon's hint).
      If $\sigma\in\lang$ then by definition such an $\hat{A}$ exists
      and so the verifier accepts at least one input pair beginning
      with~$\sigma$ (verification takes time polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$).
      If $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no $\omega$ that will verify so
      $\TM[V]$ accepts no input pair beginning with~$\sigma$.
    \item Adding the two multisets of numbers takes time polynomial in
      their digits, which is polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.
    \end{exparts}    
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  \newenvironment{problemquote}%
  {\list{}{\leftmargin=0.75em\rightmargin=0em\listparindent=1em}\item[]\it}%
  {\endlist}
For each problem, cast it as a language decision problem
and then prove that it is in~$\NP$
by filling in the blanks in this argument.
\begin{problemquote}
    \hspace{1em}\zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
    requires a deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
    It must input pairs of the form $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
    the $\sigma$ is \fillinblankLabelled{(1)}.
    The witness is \fillinblankLabelled{(2)}.

    If $\sigma\in\prob$ then there exists a suitable witness~$\omega$
    that~$\TM[V]$ can verify and consequently accept its input.
    That verification takes time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$ 
    (briefly, that's because \fillinblankLabelled{(3)}).
    If $\sigma\notin\prob$
    then by definition there is no $\omega$ that will
    cause~$\TM[V]$ to accept its input.
\end{problemquote}
\begin{exparts}
\item 
The \definend{\probname{Double-$\SAT$}}\index{Double-SAT problem@\probname{Double-$\SAT$} problem} 
problem inputs a propositional logic statement and
decides whether it has at least two different substitutions of Boolean values
that make it true.
\item
The
\probname{Subset Sum} problem\index{Subset Sum problem@\probname{Subset Sum} problem}
inputs a multiset of numbers $S\subset\N$ and a target sum~$T\in\N$,
and decides whether least one sub-multiset of~$S$ adds to~$T$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This is the language~$\lang$.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\suchthat
             \text{$\sigma$ represents a Propositional Logic statement
                   where at least two lines of the truth tables
                   end in \true}}
\end{equation*}
These are suitable fill-ins:
\begin{parenum}
\item a Propositional Logic statement~$E$,
\item a pair, pointing to two lines of the truth table whose final entry is
  \true,
and
\item evaluation of a fixed Propositional Logic statement takes time polynomial
  in the length of the statement, and thus polytime in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.
\end{parenum}
\item
Here is the problem cast as the decision problem for a language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{S,T}\suchthat
     \text{a sub-multiset of $A\subseteq S$ has $\textstyle \sum_{a\in A}a=T$}}
\end{equation*}
These are fill-ins:
\begin{parenum}
\item a pair $\sequence{S,T}$ of a multiset and a number,
\item a sub-multiset~$\hat{S}$ of $S$ whose elements add to $T$,
and
\item addition takes time polynomial in the number of inputs
  and $\hat{S}\subseteq$ so that is time polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.
\end{parenum}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
In the British game show 
\citetext{\textit{Countdown}}{%
  See \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countdown_(game_show)}.
  A version that you may find fun\Dash
  and it is well worth finding the videos\Dash is 
  \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8_Out_of_10_Cats_Does_Countdown}.}, 
players are given a multiset of
six numbers from
$S=\set{1,2,\ldots{} 10, 25, 50, 75, 100}$,
along with a target integer~$T$ from the set
$I=\set{100.\ldots\,999}$.
% (Recall that a multiset\index{multiset} 
% is like a set but repeats don't collapse.) 
They must make an arithmetic expression that evaluates to the target.
They can use addition, subtraction, 
multiplication, and division with a remainder of zero.
Their expression can fail to use some of the given numbers, but cannot
use one more than the number of times that it 
appears in the multiset.
Show that the \definend{\probname{Countdown}}\index{Countdown problem@\probname{Countdown} problem}
problem
$  \lang=
  \set{\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_5,T}\in S^6\times I
    \suchthat \text{a combination of the $s_i$ gives $T$}}$
is a member of $\NP$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
  requires that we produce a deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]\!$.
  It inputs pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
  $\sigma$ is a problem instance
  $\sequence{s_0,\ldots{}\, s_5,T}\in S^6\times\set{100,\ldots\,999}!$.
  % \begin{display}
  %   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts012.pdf}}%   
  % \end{display}  
  The witness~$\omega$ is an arithmetic expression that evaluates
  to the target number~$T$ and that involves a sub-multiset of 
  the numbers $s_0, \ldots\, s_5$.
  
  The verifier checks that the expression does indeed evaluate to the target,
  and does indeed use each of the six numbers at most once.
  Those checks can be done in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.
  (Briefly:~addition of two $n$-digit numbers is
  $\bigOh(n)$ and so addition of six $n$-digit numbers is also linear.)
  If 
  $\sigma\in\lang$ then by definition there exists such an
  arithmetic expression and so $\TM[V]$ can 
  accept an input pair.
  If $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no such arithmetic expression,
  and therefore no witness~$\omega$ will cause~$\TM[V]$ to accept.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Use \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} to show that the
\probname{Knapsack} problem\index{Knapsack problem@\probname{Knapsack} problem} 
is in~$\NP$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The \probname{Knapsack} problem 
  is the decision problem for this language $\lang$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \set{\sequence{(w_0,v_0),\ldots{} (w_{n-1},v_{n-1}),B,T}\suchthat
      \text{there is $I=\set{i_0,\ldots{} i_k}\subseteq\set{0,\ldots{} n-1}$
        so $\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}w_i\leq B$ and $\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}v_i\geq T$}}
  \end{equation*}
  The summary is that we take the witness to be a set of indices
  $\set{i_0,\ldots\,i_k}\subset\set{0,\ldots\,n-1}$
  naming items that together meet the constraints.

  For more detail:~\zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} asks for a 
  deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
  Its first input $\sigma$ is a problem instance
  $\sequence{(w_0,v_0),\ldots{} (w_{n-1},v_{n-1}),B,T}$.
  As described in the prior paragraph, its second input
  names items that meet the constraints $B$ and~$T$.
  % \begin{display}
  %   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts015.pdf}}%   
  % \end{display}
  
  If $\sigma\in\lang$ then there
  is a witness to that,~$\omega$, and~$\TM[V]$ can verify it in time
  polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.
  (For that argument:~addition of two $m$~digit integers is linear
  in the number of digits, $\bigOh(m)$.
  When there are more potential items to add to the knapsack,
  the representation~$\sigma$ gets
  bigger, and verification remains linear in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.)
  Consequently, in this case the verifier accepts its input.
  If $\sigma\notin\lang$
  then there is no such~$\omega$ and the verifier will not accept any
  pair that begins with~$\sigma$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Recast each as a language decision problem 
and then show it is in~$\NP$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
The \definend{\probname{Linear Divisibility}} problem\index{Linear Divisibility problem@\probname{Linear Divisibility} problem} 
inputs a pair of 
natural numbers $\sigma=\sequence{a,b}$ and asks
if there is an $x\in\N$ with $ax+1=b$.
\item
Given $n$ points scattered on a line, how far they are from each other
defines a multiset. 
The reverse of this problem, starting with a multiset~$M$ of numbers and 
deciding whether there exist a set of points on a line whose 
pairwise distances defines $M$, is the 
\definend{\probname{Turnpike}} problem.\index{Turnpike problem@\probname{Turnpike} problem}
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
For both we will use \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}.
\begin{exparts}
\item
  Here is an appropriate language.
  \begin{equation*}
    \lang=\set{\sequence{a,b}\in\N^2\suchthat
      \text{there exists $x\in\N$ with $ax+1=b$}}
  \end{equation*}
  The short version of the argument is that
  we can take the witness to be a suitable~$x$.
  
  For more, 
  \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} asks for a  
  deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
  Its first input~$\sigma$ is the number pair $\sequence{a,b}$.
  It second input~$\omega$ is a number $x$ so that $ax+1=b$ if there is one.
  
  In the case that $a$ and~$b$ are such that there is such an~$x$ then
  The verifier can check that $\omega$ works, that is, that  
  that $ax+1=b$.
  Because arithmetic can be done in polytime, the verifier
  can do this in time polynomial in $\sizeof{\sigma}$.
  In the other case that for the give $a$ and~$b$ there is no such number
  then there is no input pair beginning with $\sigma$ that causes the
  verifier to accept its input.
\item
  This is the associated language.
  \begin{equation*}
    \lang=\set{M\suchthat 
      \text{there is a set~$P\subset\Z$ of numbers such that 
        $M$ is the multiset of pairwise distances}}
  \end{equation*}
  The summary:~given a multiset problem instance~$\sigma=M$,
  take the witness $\omega=P$.

  More detail is that we want
  a deterministic Turing machine verifier $\TM[V]$
  that inputs pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
  $\sigma$ is a multiset~$M$. 
  As a witness we take a set of positions, $\omega=P$.

  If $\sigma\in\lang$ then there is such a
  set of positions~$P$, so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
  that the verifier accepts.
  (The check 
  that the pairwise distances among elements of~$P$ equals~$M$.
  can be done in time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$ because arithmetic
  can be done in polytime.)
  On the other hand,  if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no such 
  $\omega$ and so there does not exist an inut pair that the 
  verifier accepts.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
A company has two delivery trucks.
They use a road map that is a weighted graph,
where some vertex is distinguished as the start/finish. 
Each morning the company gets a set of vertices $V\!$, 
which is a subset of the map's vertices.
They must decide if there are two cycles such that every member of~$V$
is on at least one of the two cycles, 
and each cycle has total weight at
most~$B\in\N$.
Show that their problem is in $\NP$.
\begin{answer}\verified
We use \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}.
This is an appropriate language $\lang_B$,
where the road map is the weighted 
graph~$\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}$.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{V\subseteq\mathcal{V}\suchthat
    \text{there are two cycles, whose weights are each at most $B$ and whose vertices together make $V$}}
\end{equation*}
A summary of the proof is:~given~$V$, a suitable witness is a pair
$\sequence{C_0,C_1}$ of two cycles.

To use \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} 
we must produce a verifier.
It inputs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where $\sigma=V$.
If $\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a suitable pair of cycles, so there exists a 
witness~$\omega$ such that the verifier accepts the input pair.
(Verification of the conditions clearly can be done in time polynomial in
$\sizeof{\sigma}$, because arithmetic can be done in polytime.)
If $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there are not two such cycles,
so there is no suitable~$\omega$, and so the
verifier will not accept any input pair starting with~$\sigma$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The definition of when a nondeterministic machine decides a language,
\zcref{def:NondeterministicDecides}, requires that every branch in the 
computation tree is finite.
For recognition of languages we drop that condition.
A nondeterministic Turing machine
\definend{recognizes} a language~$\lang$\index{recognized language!of a nondeterministic Turing machine}\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!recognized language} 
when if $\sigma\in \lang$ then
there is at least one maximal path 
in the computation tree that accepts~$\sigma$,
while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then no maximal path 
in the computation tree accepts
(some may fail to accept because they are infinite).  
Show that the nondeterministic Turing machines recognize the same set of
languages as deterministic Turing machines.
\begin{answer}\verified
The proof is much like \zcref{lem:NonDeterAndDeterTMsHaveSameLangs}'s.
Here also one direction is easy because
a deterministic Turing machine is a special case of a
nondeterministic one,
so if a deterministic machine recognizes a language
then a nondeterministic one does also.

For the converse 
suppose that a nondeterministic Turing
machine~$\TM$ recognizes a language~$\lang$.
We will describe a deterministic machine~$\TM[Q]$ that does the same.

Fix an input~$\sigma$.
Have the deterministic machine~$\TM[Q]$  
do a breadth-first search of $\TM$'s computation tree.
If $\TM$ accepts~$\sigma$ then there is a maximal path whose
terminal configuration has an accepting state, and~$\TM[Q]$ 
will eventually find that configuration because the search is breadth-first.
If the tree has no accepting maximal path then~$\TM[Q]$ will never find one 
and so will not accept~$\sigma$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Two graphs $\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1$ are 
isomorphic\index{graph!isomorphism} if there is a
one-to-one and onto function $\map{f}{\mathcal{N}_0}{\mathcal{N}_1}$ such
that $\set{v,\hat{v}}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_0$ if and only if
$\set{f(v),f(\hat{v})}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_1$.
Consider the problem of computing whether two graphs are isomorphic.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Define the appropriate language.
\item Show that the language membership problem
  is in $\NP$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The most natural language is
$\lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1}\suchthat
      \text{they are isomorphic}}$.
\item
  We will show that the problem is in $\NP$ using
  \zcref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}.
  The summary is:~given the problem instance
  $\sigma=\sequence{\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1}$,
  choose as witness the function~$\omega=f$ that is the isomorphism.
  
  In more detail,
  we must produce a deterministic Turing machine verifier.
  It inputs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ where 
  $\sigma$ is a pair of graphs,
  $\sequence{\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1}$.
  If $\sigma\in\lang$ then there is an isomorphism map and so
  there is a witness~$\omega$ so that the verifier can accept its
  input pair.
  (Checking that the function is one-to-one and onto, and that 
  $\set{v,\hat{v}}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_0$ if and only if
  $\set{f(v),f(\hat{v})}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_1$
  can clearly b done in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.)
  If $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no such function, so
  no witness~$\omega$ will check out and the verifier never accepts an
  input pair that begins with~$\sigma$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is $\NP$ countable or uncountable?
\begin{answer}\verified
Countable.
A verifier is a deterministic Turing machine,
and there are countably many such machines.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Show that the \HP{} in not in $\NP$.
\item
What is wrong with this reasoning?
The \HP{} is in $\NP$ because given $\sequence{\TM,x}$,
we can take as the witness~$\omega$ a number of steps for
$\TM$ to halt on input~$x$.
If it halts in that number of steps then the verifier accepts, 
and if not then the verifier rejects.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
The class~$\NP$ is the set of languages decidable by a nondeterministic
machine in polytime.
Every problem decidable by a nondeterministic Turing machine is
decidable by a deterministic Turing machine.
The \HP{} is not decidable by a deterministic Turing machine, 
so it is not in $\NP$.
\item
There is no polynomial bound on the number of steps, 
and consequently on the length of~$\omega$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:DefnYieldsNondeterministicTM}
Following the definition of Turing machine,
on page~\pageref{text:DescriptionOfTMAction},
we gave a formal description of how those machines act.
We did the same for Finite State machines on
page~\pageref{page:DefnYieldsFiniteStateMachine},
and for nondeterministic Finite State machines on 
page~\pageref{page:DefnYieldsNondeterministicFSM}.
