Give a formal description of the action of a nondeterministic 
Turing machine.
\begin{answer}\verified
% \textit{Comment:~there are a number of possible different approaches 
% but rather than give no answer here we give one of those.}
A 
configuration
of a nondeterministic Turing machine~$\TM$
is a four-tuple, 
$\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}\in Q\times\Sigma\times\kleenestar{\Sigma}\times\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
The items signify the current state~$q$, 
the character under the read/write head~$s$, and
the tape contents to the left and right of the head~$\tau_L$ and~$\tau_R$.
Where the machine's input is the string $\sigma$,
if that string's first character is $s$, so that
$\sigma=\sequence{s}\concat\alpha$, 
then the computation's initial configuration is
$\configuration_0=\sequence{q_0,s,\emptystring,\alpha}$.
(If the input is empty then we take $s=\blank$.)

We next describe under what circumstances a configuration~$\hat{\configuration}$
succeeds~$\configuration$, 
denoted $\configuration\yields\hat{\configuration}$.
Suppose that $\configuration=\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}$. 
Compute the set~$\Delta(q,s)$.
If that set is empty then there is no next configuration~$\hat{\configuration}$.

Otherwise that set is not empty. 
For every instruction $\sequence{q_pT_pT_nq_n}\in\Delta(q,s)$ 
there are three possibilities.
Exactly one of those three holds and it defines a 
succeeding configuration~$\hat{\configuration}\!$.
\begin{parenum}
\item
If the action symbol~$T_n$ is a member of the tape alphabet, $T_n\in\Sigma$, 
then write it to the tape,
$\hat{\configuration}=\sequence{q_n,T_n,\tau_L,\tau_R}$.
\item
If $T_n$ is the character $\str{L}$ 
then move the tape head to the left.
That is,
$\hat{C}=\sequence{q_n,\hat{s},\hat{\tau}_L,\hat{\tau}_R}$
where $\hat{s}$ is the rightmost character of the string~$\tau_L$ (if
$\tau_L=\emptystring$ then $\hat{s}$ is the blank character, \blank),
where $\hat{\tau}_L$ is $\tau_L$ with its rightmost character omitted
(if $\tau_L=\emptystring$ then $\hat{\tau}_L=\emptystring$ also),
and where $\hat{\tau}_R$ is the 
string formed by pushing the tape character~$s$ 
onto the front of~$\tau_R$, giving
$\hat{\tau}_R=\sequence{s}\concat\tau_R$.
\item
If $T_n=\str{R}$ then move the tape head to the right.
This is so much like the prior item that we omit the details.
\end{parenum}

A computation path
is a sequence of configurations
$
  \configuration_0 \yields \configuration_1 \yields \cdots\,  
$,
starting with the initial configuration.
The union of all such paths forms a directed graph called the 
computation tree, or simply the computation.
If for a given input~$\sigma$ at least one maximal path in that 
tree ends in a configuration
whose state is accepting then we say that the machine accepts~$\sigma$.
If the machine does not accept $\sigma$ then it rejects~$\sigma$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{NP@$\NP$|)}





% ==============================================
\section{Reductions between problems}\index{reductions between problems|(}
%<*disc:PolyTimeReductioni>
When we studied uncomputability we considered a sense in which
some problems are harder than others.
Recall that the
$\probname{Halts on Three}$ problem\index{Halts On Three problem@\probname{Halts On Three} problem}  
is to
decide membership in the set
$S=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$.
We showed that if we could solve it 
then we could solve the \HP.
We denoted this relationship between problems with
$K\leq_TS$.
In general, a set $B$ Turing reduces to~$A$,\index{Turing reducible}
written $B\leq_T A$, 
if there is a Turing machine that computes~$B$ from an $A$ oracle, so that 
$\TMfcn_e^A=\charfcn{B}$.\footnote{%
  As discussed on \protect\zcpageref{pageref:reducesto},
  people often get `reduces to' 
  the wrong way around.
  Think of `$B$ reduces to $A$' informally as meaning that $A$
  knows at least as much as~$B$.
  For instance, where $A$ is  
  the \protect\textit{Entscheidungsproblem} of answering all
  questions in Mathematics
  and $B$ is Goldbach's conjecture 
  then $B$ reduces to~$A$ 
  because if we could solve~$A$ then we would get 
  a solution to $B$ as a side effect.}
  % Perhaps they are misled by `$B\leq_T A$' into
  % thinking that $B$ is smaller and so somehow is the reduced thing, 
  % but that is mistaken.
  % One example is that the problem of solving a linear system of equations
  % reduces to the problem of inverting the matrix of coefficients, because
  % if we can solve the second then we can solve the first. 
  % Another example is where $A$ is  
  % the \protect\textit{Entscheidungsproblem} of answering all
  % questions in Mathematics
  % and $B$ is Goldbach's conjecture. 
  % Then the correct terminology is that $B$ reduces to~$A$ 
  % because if we can solve~$A$ then we get a solution to $B$ as a side effect.
  % }
In words, we can answer questions about membership in~$B$ by
being given access to
questions about membership in~$A$.
%</disc:PolyTimeReductioni>

% %<*disc:PolyTimeReductionii>
% More generally, in Mathematics we say that a problem $B$ `reduces to' $A$
% when in order to solve $B$, it suffices to solve~$A$.
% An example is that the problem of finding the maximum of a list of numbers 
% reduces to the problem of sorting that list.
% The intuition is that $A$ is at least as hard as $B$.
% %</disc:PolyTimeReductionii>

There are reductions other than 
Turing reductions.
Consider these two problems, where the second is a translation or 
alternate presentation of the other: 
$S=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$ 
and $T=\set{e^2+1\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$.
We can compute
answers to questions about~$T$
from answers about~$S$ since
$x$ is in~$T$ iff $\,\sqrt{x-1}$ is a natural number and is in~$S$.
We say that $B$ is 
\definend{mapping reducible}\index{mapping reducible}\index{reducible!mapping} 
or  
\citetext{\definend{many-one reducible}}{%
  The name reflects the fact that still 
  another reducibility is one-one reducibility, 
  where the function must be one-to-one.
  }\index{many-one reducible}\index{reducible!many-one} 
to~$A$,
denoted \definend{$B\leq_m A$},
if there is a total computable 
\definend{translation function}~$f$\index{translation function}\index{function!translation}
such that $x\in B$ if and only if
$f(x)\in A$.
Thus, $T\leq_m S$.

The difference between the two reducibilities is that with
$B\leq_T A$, we answer questions about membership in $B$
by asking a number of questions of an $A$ oracle
and possibly doing more processing with that information.  
But with $B\leq_m A$ we make only one oracle call, asking whether $f(x)\in A$,
and then return that call's result.
% Many-one reductions are a special case of 
% Turing reductions, meaning that if $B\leq_m A$ then $B\leq_T A$,
% but the converse does not hold;~see \zcref{exer:TReducableDiffersFromMReducible}.

This chapter focuses on efficient use of computing resources
so one natural thing to do is to define
\definend{Cook reduction}\index{Cook reducibility}\index{reducibility!Cook} 
or  \definend{polytime Turing reduction}\index{Turing reducibility}\index{reducibility!polytime Turing},
denoted \definend{$B\leq_T^pA$}, 
if there is an oracle Turing machine giving
$\TMfcn_e^{A}=\charfcn{B}$ that runs in polytime.
However, more common is to 
adapt many-one reduction by restricting the translation function to 
be computable and to run in polytime.

%<*def:PolyTimeReduction>
\begin{definition}\label{def:PolytimeReducibility}
Let $\lang_0,\lang_1$ be languages, subsets of $\kleenestar{\B}\!$.
Then 
\definend{$\lang_1$ is polynomial time reducible to $\lang_0$},\index{polynomial time reducibility}\index{reducibility!polynomial time}\index{reducibility!polytime}
or \definend{Karp reducible},\index{Karp reducible}\index{reducibility!Karp} 
or \definend{polynomial time mapping reducible},\index{reducibility!polytime mapping}
or
\definend{polynomial time many-one reducible},\index{reducibility!polytime many-one}
written 
\definend{$\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$} or \definend{$\lang_1\leq_m^p\lang_0$},
if there is a 
\definend{reduction function}\index{reduction function}\index{function!reduction}
or \definend{transformation function}\index{transformation function|see{reduction function}}
$\map{f}{\kleenestar{\B}}{\kleenestar{\B}}$ that 
is polynomial time computable
and such that
$\sigma\in\lang_1$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$.\footnotemark
\end{definition}
\footnotetext{%
  If between two problems there is a Karp reduction then there
  is a Cook reduction also.
  (The converse does not hold since
  % the chapter's fundamental question is
  % whether a problem has an algorithm returning `yes' in polytime. 
  % But this does not imply that there is an algorithm that returns 
  % `no' in polytime.
  % \protect\citetext{The opposite appears to be common}{Derived from \protect\autocite{SE:vzn}.}, as with \probname{Satisfiability}.
  under Cook reduction a problem and its complement are equivalent but 
  \citetext{that's not true under Karp reduction}{%
    The \HP{} set $K$ and its complement
    are not Karp reducible to each other.   
    For, we already know that $K$ is computably enumerable.
    If $\setcomp{K}\leq_p K$ then 
    $x\in\setcomp{K}$ implies that $f(x)\in K$, and
    we can enumerate $f(0), f(1), \ldots$ and check those against the
    values enumerated into~$K$, so we would have that $\setcomp{K}$ is 
    also computably enumerable.
    That would imply that $K$ is computable, which it is not.}.)
  % The two apppear to often differ:~an example 
  % is that with Cook reducibility we get 
  % $\text{\protect\probname{Chromatic Number}}\leq_T^p\text{\protect\probname{Graph Colorability}}$\index{Chromatic Number@\probname{Chromatic Number} problem}\index{Graph Colorability problem@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem} 
  % by first 
  % seeing if the graph is $2$-colorable, then seeing if it is 
  % $3$-colorable, etc.,
  % but there is no evident way to do this with one oracle call so 
  % it does not seem that there is a Karp reduction.
  % Another example is that 
  % An important case is that the complexity classes $\NP$ and 
  % \compclass{co$\NP$} % won't be correctly processed by makeindex: \protect\index{coNP@\protect\compclass{co$\protect\NP$}} 
  % (the problems whose complement is in $\NP$)
  % are equal under Cook reduction but appear 
  % to be separate under Karp reduction.
  Since any Karp reduction result is automatically a Cook reduction result,
  Karp reduction gives a more 
  fine-grained partition of the problems and so while it may 
  seem less natural, it
  is the right one for us to study.
  In any event, Karp reduction is the standard in computational complexity.
  }
%</def:PolyTimeReduction>
 
The point of a polytime reduction $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ is that 
a fast way to determine membership in $\lang_0$ gives
a fast way to determine membership in $\lang_1$.
% For, to settle an
% $x\in\lang_1$ question, translate it to an
% $f(x)\in\lang_0$ question.
% Both the translation step and the $\lang_0$ answering step are fast,
% and so their combination is also fast.

\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeanWithReductions]{This 
  is the collection of all problems, \mbox{$\lang\in\powerset(\kleenestar{\B})$},
  with a few shown as dots.
  Ones with fast algorithms are at the bottom.
  Problems are connected if there is a polytime reduction from one 
  to the other.
  Highlighted are connections within~$\P$.
  }
  % (Because problems outside of $\probname{Rec}$ don't have a decider,
  % they don't form part of the web of connections.)
  \label{fig:NPComplete}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2008.pdf}}
\end{fig}

The intuition is that there is a reduction of this kind when 
one problem is a translation of the other, or a special case.
% The first example illustrates.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:PathLeShortestPath}
The \probname{Shortest Path} problem\index{Shortest Path problem@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} 
inputs a weighted graph, two vertices, and a bound,
and
decides if there is path between the vertices of length less than the bound.
The \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}\index{Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem@\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem} 
problem inputs an unweighted graph and two vertices, 
and then decides if there is a
path between the two.
We will show that the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem reduces to
\probname{Shortest Path}, 
that $\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}\leq_p\probname{Shortest Path}$,
by viewing the first 
as a special case of the second.

First, express them as language decision problems.
\begin{align*}
  \lang_0&=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,B}\suchthat
               \text{there is path from $v_0$ to $v_1$ of total weight at most~$B$}} \\
  \lang_1&=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{H},w_0,w_1}\suchthat
               \text{there is path between $w_0$ and $w_1$}}
\end{align*}
We will show that $\lang_1\leq_p \lang_0$ by describing a reduction 
function~$f\!$.
Given $\sequence{\mathcal{H},w_0,w_1}$ as input, make
a weighted graph~$\mathcal{G}$ with the same vertices as~$\mathcal{H}$ 
by giving each edge a weight of~$1$. 
Also take the bound to be the number of vertices, 
$B=\sizeof{\mathcal{G}}$.
Then $\sequence{\mathcal{H},w_0,w_1}\in\lang_1$ if and only if
$f(\mathcal{H})=\sequence{\mathcal{G},w_0,w_1,\sizeof{\mathcal{G}}}\in\lang_0$. 
Clearly $f$ runs in polytime.
\end{example}

In the next example, 
at first glance it doesn't seem that one problem is a translation
of the others\Dash  
for one thing,
one is a graph problem and the other is not\Dash so we include 
some development suggesting 
how to see a relationship.\footnote{%
  In looking at materials, for example on the web, be aware that
  it is common to expect readers to 
  work out for themselves 
  the motivation for the reduction function's definition.}

\begin{example} \label{ex:SatReducesToClique}
%<*ex:SATLeqCliquei>
The \probname{Clique} 
problem is the decision problem for the language
$\lang_B=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat\text{$\mathcal{G}$ has a clique with $B$ vertices}}$.
We will sketch that 
$\text{\probname{Satisfiability}}\leq_p\text{\probname{Clique}}$,\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem}
that is, \probname{Clique}
is at least as hard as \probname{Satisfiability}.

Consider how to satisfy this Boolean expression.
\begin{equation*}
  E=(x_0\vee \neg x_1\vee x_2)
  \wedge (\neg x_0\vee x_2\vee \neg x_3)
  \wedge (\neg x_1\vee \neg x_2)
  % \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
The $\wedge$'s make the statement as a whole \true{} 
if and only if all of its clauses are $T\!$.
Inside each clause, the $\vee$'s mean that the clause is \true{} 
if and only if at least one of its literals is $T\!$.
So to satisfy the expression, select a literal from
each clause and assign it the value $T\!$.
For example, we can make $E$ be \true{} by
selecting $x_0$ from the first clause, $x_2$ from the second, 
and $\neg x_1$ from the third, and making them $T\!$.
Similarly, if $\neg x_1$ from the first and third clauses and 
$x_3$ from the second are $\true$ then $E$ is~$T\!$.
What we cannot do is 
pick $x_2$ from the first and second and then $\neg x_2$
from the third, because we cannot set both of these literals to be $T\!$.
%</ex:SATLeqCliquei>

%<*ex:SATLeqCliqueii>
Thus, we can think of
\probname{Satisfiability} as a combinatorial problem.
The clauses are like buckets and we select one thing from each bucket,
subject to the constraint that the things we select must be
pairwise compatible.
%</ex:SATLeqCliqueii>

%<*ex:SATLeqCliqueiii>
This view of \probname{Satisfiability} has a binary
relation `can be compatibly picked' between the literals.
So, as below, let $\mathcal{G}_E$ be a graph whose vertices are pairs
$\sequence{c,\ell}$ where $c$ is the number of a clause and 
$\ell$ is a literal in that clause.
Two vertices $v_0=\sequence{c_0,\ell_0}$ and 
$v_1=\sequence{c_1,\ell_1}$ are connected by an edge
if they are compatible:~if they come from different clauses, $c_0\neq c_1$, 
and the literals are not negations of each other,
$\ell_0\neq \neg\ell_1$.
%</ex:SATLeqCliqueiii>
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{Compatibility graph associated with $E$.}\centering 
    \includegraphics{\asydir complexity/complexity39}
  \end{fig} 
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{Compatibility graph associated with $E$.}\centering 
    \animategraphics[poster=last]{1}{\asydir complexity/complexity}{38}{39}
  \end{animatedfigure} 
}
%<*ex:SATLeqCliqueiv>
\noindent A choice of three mutually compatible vertices makes $E$ 
evaluate to \true.
That is, the $3$ clause expression $E$ is satisfiable 
if and only if $\mathcal{G}_E$ has a $3$-clique.
%</ex:SATLeqCliqueiv>

%<*ex:SATLeqCliquev>
More formally,
the reduction function $f$ inputs a propositional logic 
expression~$E$ and outputs a pair $f(E)=\sequence{\mathcal{G}_E,B}$
where $\mathcal{G}_E$ is the compatibility graph associated with $E$ as in
the prior paragraph and where $B$ is the number of clauses in~$E$.
Then
$E\in\SAT$ if and only if $f(E)\in\lang_B$.
Clearly this function can be computed in polytime.
%</ex:SATLeqCliquev>
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:GraphColoringReducesToSatisfiability}
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATi>
Recall that a graph is $k$-colorable if we can partition the vertices
into $k$~many classes, called `colors', 
so that two vertices can have the same color only when there is no edge 
between them.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATi>
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{A $3$-coloring of \protect\citetext{the Petersen graph}{The Petersen graph is a rich source of counterexamples for conjectures in Graph Theory}.}\centering 
    \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity01}
  \end{fig} 
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{A $3$-coloring of \protect\citetext{the Petersen graph}{The Petersen graph is a rich source of counterexamples for conjectures in Graph Theory}.}\centering 
    \animategraphics[poster=last]{2}{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity}{00}{01}
  \end{animatedfigure} 
}
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATii>
\noindent 
% Recall also that a propositional logic expression
% is satisfiable if there is an
% assignment of the values \true{} and \false{} to the variables 
% that makes the statement
% as a whole evaluate to~\true.
We will illustrate that the 
\probname{Graph Colorability}\index{Graph Colorability problem@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem}
problem reduces to the 
\probname{Satisfiability}\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem} 
problem,
$\probname{Graph Colorability}\leq_p\probname{Satisfiability}$,
by focusing on the $k=3$ construction.
(Larger $k$'s work much the same way, although the $k=2$ case is different.)
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATii>

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiii>
Denote the set of satisfiable propositional logic statements as~$\lang_0$
and the set of $3$-colorable graphs as $\lang_1$.
To show that $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ we must produce a 
reduction function~$f\!$.
It inputs a graph~$\mathcal{G}$
and a outputs a propositional logic expression $E=f(\mathcal{G})$
such that the graph is $3$-colorable 
if and only if the expression is satisfiable.

Suppose that the input graph~$\mathcal{G}$ has $n$-many vertices 
$\set{v_0,\ldots\, v_{n-1}}$.
Then~$E$ has $3n$-many Boolean variables,
$
  {a_0,\ldots\, a_{n-1}}
$, and
$  
  {b_0,\ldots\, b_{n-1}}
$, and
$  
  {c_0,\ldots\, c_{n-1}}
$.
The idea is that if the $i$-th vertex~$v_i$ gets the first color then 
$E$ will be satisfied when 
the first variable is true but the others are false, 
$a_i=\true, b_i=\false, c_i=\false\!$,
while if $v_i$ gets the second color then $E$ will be satisfied when
$a_i=\false,b_i=\true,c_i=\false\!$, 
and if $v_i$ gets the
third color then $E$ will be satisfied when
$a_i=\false,b_i=\false,c_i=\true\!$.
 
Specifically, the expression $E$ 
includes two kinds of clauses.
For every vertex~$v_i$, there is a clause saying 
that the vertex gets at least one color.
\begin{equation*}
  (a_i\vee b_i \vee c_i)
\end{equation*}
And for each edge~$\set{v_i,v_j}$, there are 
three clauses which together ensure that 
the edge does not connect two same-color vertices.
\begin{equation*}
  (\neg a_i\vee \neg a_j)
  \qquad
  (\neg b_i\vee \neg b_j)
  \qquad
  (\neg c_i\vee \neg c_j)
\end{equation*}
Then ~$E$ is the conjunction of all of these clauses.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiii>

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiv>
This illustrates.
The expression's top line has the clauses of the first kind while
the remaining lines have the other kind.
\begin{center}\vspace{1.75ex plus 1ex}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir problems/problems23.pdf}}% 
  \hspace{0em}% 
  \begin{minipage}{3.5in}\noindent\small
    \vspace*{-2\abovedisplayskip}% minipages really should not start with eqs
    \vspace*{-\topskip}%
    \vspace*{-\lineskip}%
    \begin{multline*}
      (a_0\vee b_0 \vee c_0)\wedge  
      (a_1\vee b_1 \vee c_1)\wedge  
      (a_2\vee b_2 \vee c_2)\wedge  
      (a_3\vee b_3 \vee c_3)             \\
         \begin{aligned}
             &\wedge (\neg a_0\vee\neg a_1)
               \wedge (\neg b_0\vee\neg b_1)
               \wedge (\neg c_0\vee\neg c_1)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_0\vee\neg a_3)
               \wedge (\neg b_0\vee\neg b_3)
               \wedge (\neg c_0\vee\neg c_3)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_1\vee\neg a_2)
               \wedge (\neg b_1\vee\neg b_2)
               \wedge (\neg c_1\vee\neg c_2)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_1\vee\neg a_3)
               \wedge (\neg b_1\vee\neg b_3)
               \wedge (\neg c_1\vee\neg c_3) 
       \end{aligned} 
    \end{multline*}
  \end{minipage}
\end{center}
By construction, we can satisfy the expression with some 
assignments of truth values for the variables   
if and only if the graph has a $3$-coloring.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiv>
  
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATv>
Completing the argument requires
checking that the reduction function, which inputs a bitstring
representation of the graph and outputs a bitstring representation
of the expression, is polynomial.
That's clear so we omit the details.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATv>
\end{example}

% A reduction function is a kind of compiler.
% A programming language compiler inputs descriptions from one domain,
% such as a Racket program, and outputs 
% a corresponding statement from another domain,
% such an executable in the machine's native format.
% Similarly, the function~$f$ above translates problem instances in the domain
% of graphs to those in the domain of propositional logic.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:SubsetSumLeqKnapsack}
We will show % that $\probname{Subset Sum}$ reduces to $\probname{Knapsack}$,
that $\probname{Subset Sum}\leq_p\probname{Knapsack}$.
The 
\probname{Subset Sum} problem\index{Subset Sum problem@\probname{Subset Sum} problem} 
inputs a multiset 
$S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}\subset\N$ and a target~$T\in\N$.
It asks for a sub-multiset whose elements add to the target.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{S,T}\suchthat
                \text{some $R\subseteq S$ has $T=\sum_{r\in R}r$}}
\end{equation*}
The \probname{Knapsack} problem\index{Knapsack problem@\probname{Knapsack} problem} 
starts with a multiset $U=\set{u_0, \ldots\, u_{n-1}}$
whose elements have a weight~$w(u_i)$ and a value~$v(u_i)$,
along with an upper bound on the weights $W\in\N$ and a lower bound 
for the values $V\in\N$.
It asks for sub-multiset $A\subseteq U$
whose weight total does not exceed~$W$
and whose value total is at least~$V$.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{U,w,v,W,V}\suchthat
                \text{some $A\subseteq U$ has 
                     $\sum_{a\in A}w(a)\leq W$
                     and $\sum_{a\in A}v(a)\geq V$}}
\end{equation*}

The reduction function~$f$ must input pairs $\sequence{S,T}$ 
and output five-tuples $\sequence{U,w,v,W,V}$,
and must be such that $\sequence{S,T}\in\lang_1$ if and
only if $f(\sequence{S,T})\in\lang_0$.
And it must run in polytime.

A numerical example gives the idea of how $f$ proceeds. 
Imagine that we 
want to know if there is a subset of
$S=\set{18, 23, 31, 33, 72,  86, 94}$ that adds to $T=126$.
If we had access to an oracle for \probname{Knapsack}
then we could set $U=S$, let $w$ and~$v$ be the identity functions so that
$w(18)=v(18)=18$ and $w(23)=v(23)=23$, etc.,
and then fix the weight and value targets as $W=V=126$.
Then $\sequence{S,T}\in\lang_1$ iff $\sequence{S,w,v,W,V}\in\lang_0$.
% as in the table below.
% \begin{center}
% \begin{tabular}{r|*{7}{c}}
%   \tabulartext{object} &$s_0$ &$s_1$ &$s_2$ &$s_3$ &$s_4$ &$s_5$ &$s_6$  \\
%   \cline{2-8}
%   \rule{0pt}{11pt}
%   \tabulartext{weight} &$18$  &$23$  &$31$  &$33$  &$72$  &$86$ &$94$  \\
%   \tabulartext{value}  &$18$  &$23$  &$31$  &$33$  &$72$  &$86$ &$94$  \\
% \end{tabular}
% \hspace{2em}
% $W=V=126$
% \end{center}

More generally, let $f$ take the input $\sequence{S,T}$ to the output
$\sequence{S,w,v,T,T}$, 
where the functions $w$ and $v$ are given by 
$w(s_i)=v(s_i)=s_i$.
Then $\sequence{S,T}\in\lang_0$ if and only if 
$f(\sequence{S,T})\in\lang_1$. 
In this way, 
we think of \probname{Subset Sum} as the specialization of, or translation of,
\probname{Knapsack} that is obtained by setting $w(s_i)=v(s_i)=s_i$
and $W=V=T$.
Clearly $f$ can be done in polytime.
\end{example}

We close with some basic facts about polytime reduction.

%<*lem:PolytimeReductionTrans>
\begin{lemma} \label{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}
\begin{parenum}
\item
Polytime reduction is reflexive: $\lang\leq_p\lang$ for all languages.
\item
It is also transitive: $\lang_2\leq_p\lang_1$ and $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$
imply that $\lang_2\leq_p\lang_0$.
% There are pairs of languages that are incomparable, that are
% without a polytime reduction in either direction.
\item  \label{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRedItemPMinimal}
If $\lang_1\in\P$ then
every language $\lang_0$ that is nontrivial, where 
$\lang_0\neq\emptyset$ and $\lang_0\neq\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
satisfies that $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
\item \label{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRedItemPClosedDown}
The class $\P$ is closed downward:~if
$\prob_0\in\P$ and $\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ then $\prob_1\in\P$. 
\item \label{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRedItemNPClosedDown}
So also is the class~$\NP$.
\end{parenum}
\end{lemma}
%</lem:PolytimeReductionTrans>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:PolytimeReductionTransi>
% The third sentence holds because there are uncountably many languages
% but only countably many reduction functions, as
% each such function is computed by a Turing machine.
The first two items and 
the final one are \zcref{exer:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}.

For the third, fix a $\lang_1\in\P$.
Let $\lang_0$ be nontrivial 
so that it has a member $\sigma\in\lang_0$ and a nonmember $\tau\notin\lang_0$.
We will give a polytime reduction function~$f$ 
showing that $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
Where $\alpha\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
computing whether $\alpha\in\lang_1$ can be done in polytime.
If it is a member of $\lang_1$ then let $f(\alpha)=\sigma$
while if not then let $f(\alpha)=\tau$.
%</pf:PolytimeReductionTransi>

%<*pf:PolytimeReductionTransii>
As to the fourth, suppose that
there is a polytime algorithm to decide membership in~$\lang_0$
and that $\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ via a polytime reduction function~$g$.
Then decide membership in $\lang_1$ by starting with an
input $\sigma$, finding $g(\sigma)$, and applying the $\lang_0$~algorithm
to settle whether $g(\sigma)\in\lang_0$.
Where the $\lang_0$ algorithm runs in time that is $\bigOh(n^i)$
and where $g$ runs in time that is~$\bigOh(n^j)$,
determining $\lang_1$ membership in this way 
runs in time that is~$\bigOh(n^{\max(i,j)})$, which is polynomial.
%</pf:PolytimeReductionTransii>
\end{proof}

By the lemma, because
\zcref{ex:GraphColoringReducesToSatisfiability}
shows $\probname{Graph Colorability}\leq_p\probname{Satisfiability}$
and \zcref{ex:SatReducesToClique} gives
$\probname{Satisfiability}\leq_p\probname{Clique}$,
we know that 
$\probname{Graph Colorability}\leq_p\probname{Clique}$.

\medskip
We close by reiterating this section's main point:~the
reducibility 
$\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$
means that if we could solve the $\lang_0$ problem 
in polynomial time then we could also solve the
$\lang_1$ problem in polynomial time.
(Finally, more examples of problem reductions are in
\zcref{sec:NPCompleteness}.)

% Finally, note that the examples above give some sense of why 
% the \probname{Satisfiability} problem
% is convenient for reducibility.
% Often it is natural to describe the conditions in a problem
% with propositional logical statements.
% The next section gives a theorem saying that 
% \probname{Satisfiability} is a benchmark in that it
% is at least as hard as every problem in $\NP$.


% .......................................
\begin{exercises}

\begin{exercise}
Which is the right way to use the phrase `reduces to' 
when reading $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ 
aloud, ``$\lang_1$ reduces to $\lang_0$'' or
``$\lang_0$ reduces to~$\lang_1$?''
\begin{answer}\verified
The first way is right:~when $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ then
$\lang_1$ reduces to $\lang_0$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your found this online as 
the intuition behind polytime reduction,
$B\leq_p A$:~\textit{If you had a black box that can solve instances of
problem~$A$, then you could solve any instance of $B$ using
polynomial number of steps plus a polynomial number of
calls to the black box that solves $A$.}
Is it right?
\begin{answer}\verified
No.
That intuition fits Cook reduction.
Karp reduction calls the black box for~$A$ at most one time.
This is
the `$f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$' at the end of \zcref{def:PolytimeReducibility}.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The relation $\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ implies which?
\begin{exparts}
\item A fast algorithm for $\prob_0$ would give a fast algorithm
for $\prob_1$.
\item A fast algorithm for $\prob_1$ would give a fast one
for $\prob_0$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
It is the first.
A fast algorithm for $\prob_0$ would give a fast one for $\prob_1$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that if $\lang_1\notin\P$ and $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$
  then $\lang_0\notin\P$ also.
What about $\NP$? 
\begin{answer}\verified
The assertion about $\P$ is immediate from 
\zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}'s
\zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRedItemPClosedDown}.
It also holds for $\NP$ by  
\zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRedItemNPClosedDown}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your friend is confused.
``\zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}
says that every language in $\P$ is $\leq_p$ every other nontrivial language.
But there are only countably many
$f$'s because they each come from some Turing machine, while there are
uncountably many languages.
So I'm not seeing how there are enough reduction functions for a given 
language to be related to all others.'' 
Un-confuse them.
\begin{answer}\verified
If $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ via the reduction function~$f$
then there can be another language $\lang$
where $\lang_1\leq_p\lang$, also via the reduction function~$f\!$.
There can be uncountably many such other languages.

Expanding a bit:~in the proof of \zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}'s
argument for \zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRedItemPMinimal},
the reduction function is as below for the input string~$\alpha$,
using one member $\sigma\in\lang_0$ and one nonmember $\tau\notin\lang_0$.
\begin{equation*}
  f(\alpha)=
  \begin{cases}
    \sigma  &\case{if $\alpha\in\lang_1$}  \\
    \tau     &\case{otherwise}  
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
This definition
leaves untouched the question of elementhood for almost all
of the codomain.
Strings other than $\sigma$ and $\tau$ can be elements or non-elements 
of~$\lang$. 
As a result, this one reduction function relates
$\lang_1$ to uncountably many other sets.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\zcref{ex:SubsetSumLeqKnapsack} includes as 
illustration a \probname{Subset Sum} problem,
where $S=\set{18, 23, 31, 33, 72,  86, 94}$ 
and $T=126$.
Solve it.
\begin{answer}\verified
There is indeed a subset that adds to~$T$,
namely $A=\set{23, 31, 72}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
% sage: 23+31+72
% 126


\begin{exercise}
Fix a language decision problem~$\prob_0$ in $\bigOh(n^3)$,
and fix $\prob_1\in\bigOh(n^2)$,
and $\prob_2\in\bigOh(2^n)$ but not in $\P$,
and $\prob_3\in\bigOh(\lg n)$.
In the array entry $i,j$ below, put `N' if $\prob_i\leq_p\prob_j$ is not
possible and `Y' if it is.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
             &$\prob_0$  &$\prob_1$  &$\prob_2$  &$\prob_3$  \\
    \cline{2-5}
  $\prob_0$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,3)} \\  
  $\prob_1$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,3)} \\  
  $\prob_2$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,3)} \\  
  $\prob_3$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,3)} \\  
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
\zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed} says that $\leq_p$ is a reflexive relation
so the diagonal below contains Y's.
By the same lemma, any problem in $\P$ is polytime 
reducible to any other problem at all.
So the first, second, and fourth row are all Y's.
\begin{display}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{c}} 
\multicolumn{1}{r}{\ } &$\prob_0$ &$\prob_1$ &$\prob_2$ &$\prob_3$ \\ \cline{2-5}             $\prob_0$ &Y         &Y         &Y        &Y \\ 
             $\prob_1$ &Y         &Y         &Y        &Y  \\ 
             $\prob_2$ &N         &N         &Y        &N  \\ 
             $\prob_3$ &Y         &Y         &Y        &Y  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
Also by the lemma, 
if a problem is polytime reducible to one in~$\P$ 
then it is itself in $\P$.
But $\prob_2$ is given as not in~$\P$,
which accounts for the N's in the third row. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % 
Suppose that the language $A$ is polynomial time reducible to the language~$B$,
$A\leq_p B$. 
Which of these are true?
\begin{exparts}
\item If $A$ is tractably decidable then~$B$ is tractably decidable also.
\item If $A$ is not tractably decidable then $B$ is also not tractably 
decidable.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This is wrong. 
If $A$ is the empty set, which is decidable, 
and $B=K$, then $A\leq_pB$ but $B$ is not decidable in polytime.
The reason that $A\leq_pB$ is that $A$ can be decided in polytime
without any reference to~$B$ at all; in fact, deciding $A$ is $\bigOh(1)$.
\item This is true.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce the compatibility graph as in \zcref{ex:SatReducesToClique} for 
$(x_0\vee x_1)\wedge(\neg x_0\vee\neg x_1)\wedge(x_0\vee \neg x_1)$.
\begin{answer}\verified
\zcref{ex:SatReducesToClique} says that we want the graph
$\mathcal{G}$ whose vertices are pairs
$\sequence{c,\ell}$ where $c$ is the number of a clause and 
$\ell$ is a literal in that clause.
Two vertices $v_0=\sequence{c_0,\ell_0}$ and 
$v_1=\sequence{c_1,\ell_1}$ are connected by an edge
if they come from different clauses, $c_0\neq c_1$, 
and the literals are not negations of each other,
$\ell_0\neq \neg\ell_1$.

The given propositional logic 
expression has three clauses, $x_0\vee x_1$, $\neg x_0\vee\neg x_1$, and
$x_0\vee \neg x_1$, each with two literals.
This is the graph.
\begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{l}
    $v_{0,0}=\sequence{0,x_0}$  \\
    $v_{0,1}=\sequence{0,x_1}$  \\
    $v_{1,0}=\sequence{1,\neg x_0}$  \\
    $v_{1,1}=\sequence{1,\neg x_1}$  \\
    $v_{2,0}=\sequence{2,x_0}$  \\
    $v_{2,1}=\sequence{2,\neg x_1}$  
  \end{tabular}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity03.pdf}}
\end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Following the method of \zcref{ex:GraphColoringReducesToSatisfiability}
give the expression associated with the question of whether this graph
is $3$-colorable. 
Is that expression satisfiable?
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{\asydir complexity/complexity03.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
For each vertex there is a clause.
\begin{equation*}
  a_{0,0}\vee b_{0,0}\vee c_{0,0}
  \quad
  a_{0,1}\vee b_{0,1}\vee c_{0,1}
  \quad
  a_{1,0}\vee b_{1,0}\vee c_{1,0}
  \quad
  a_{1,1}\vee b_{1,1}\vee c_{1,1}
  \quad
  a_{2,0}\vee b_{2,0}\vee c_{2,0}
  \quad
  a_{2,1}\vee b_{2,1}\vee c_{2,1}
\end{equation*}
For each edge there are three clauses.
For instance, for the edge between $v_{0,0}$ and $v_{1,1}$
the three clauses are here.
\begin{equation*}
  (\neg a_{0,0}\vee \neg a_{1,1})
  \qquad
  (\neg b_{0,0}\vee \neg b_{1,1})
  \qquad
  (\neg c_{0,0}\vee \neg c_{1,1})
\end{equation*}
Construct the entire expression,
\begin{align*}
  E=
  &(a_{0,0}\vee b_{0,0}\vee c_{0,0})
  \wedge
  (a_{0,1}\vee b_{0,1}\vee c_{0,1})
  \wedge
  (a_{1,0}\vee b_{1,0}\vee c_{1,0})
  \\ &\quad\wedge
  (a_{1,1}\vee b_{1,1}\vee c_{1,1})
  \wedge
  (a_{2,0}\vee b_{2,0}\vee c_{2,0})
  \wedge
  (a_{2,1}\vee b_{2,1}\vee c_{2,1})  
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{0,0}\vee \neg a_{1,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{0,0}\vee \neg b_{1,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{0,0}\vee \neg c_{1,1})
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{0,0}\vee \neg a_{2,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{0,0}\vee \neg b_{2,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{0,0}\vee \neg c_{2,0})
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{0,0}\vee \neg a_{2,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{0,0}\vee \neg b_{2,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{0,0}\vee \neg c_{2,1})
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{0,1}\vee \neg a_{1,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{0,1}\vee \neg b_{1,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{0,1}\vee \neg c_{1,0})
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{0,1}\vee \neg a_{2,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{0,1}\vee \neg b_{2,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{0,1}\vee \neg c_{2,0})
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{1,0}\vee \neg a_{2,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{1,0}\vee \neg b_{2,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{1,0}\vee \neg c_{2,1})
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{1,1}\vee \neg a_{2,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{1,1}\vee \neg b_{2,0})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{1,1}\vee \neg c_{2,0})
  \\ &\qquad \wedge   (\neg a_{1,1}\vee \neg a_{2,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg b_{1,1}\vee \neg b_{2,1})
                     \wedge
                     (\neg c_{1,1}\vee \neg c_{2,1})
\end{align*}
by taking the conjunction of the clauses.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Substring} problem\index{Substring problem@\probname{Substring} problem} 
inputs two strings and decides if the second is a substring of the first.
The \definend{\probname{Cyclic Shift}} problem\index{Cyclic Shift problem@\probname{Cyclic Shift} problem}
inputs two strings and decides if the second is a cyclic shift of the first.
(If $\alpha=a_0a_1\ldots{}a_{n-1}$ and
$\beta=b_0b_1\ldots{}b_{n-1}$ are length~$n$ strings,
then $\beta$ is a cyclic shift of $\alpha$ when there is an 
index $k\in\set{0,\ldots n-1}$ such that $a_i=b_{(k+i)\bmod n}$ for 
all~$i<n$.
Thus, where $\alpha=\str{abcde}$, the cyclic shifts are $\str{bcdea}$,
$\str{cdeab}$, \ldots\,)
\begin{exparts}
\item Name three cyclic shifts of $\alpha=\str{0110010}$.
\item Decide whether $\beta=\str{101001101}$ is a cyclic shift of 
  $\alpha=\str{001101101}$.
\item State the \probname{Substring} problem as a language decision problem.
\item State \probname{Cyclic Shift} as a
  language decision problem. 
\item Show that $\probname{Cyclic Shift}\leq_p\probname{Substring}$.
  \hint~for same length strings, 
  $\beta$ is a cyclic shift of $\alpha$ if and only if 
  $\beta$ is a substring of $\alpha\concat\alpha$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Three are: 
$\beta_0=\alpha=\str{0110010}$,
$\beta_1=\str{1100100}$,
and $\beta_2=\str{1001001}$.
\item It is a cyclic shift starting from index $k=6$.
\item It is the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\sigma,\tau}\in\Sigma^2\suchthat
                        \text{$\tau$ is a substring of $\sigma$}}
\end{equation*}
\item It is the decision problem for this.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\alpha,\beta}\in\Sigma^2\suchthat
               \text{$\beta$ is a cyclic shift of $\alpha$}}
\end{equation*}
\item Following the hint,
  the reduction function~$f$ inputs $\sequence{\alpha,\beta}$
  and if the two are the same length then 
  it outputs $\sequence{\alpha\concat\alpha,\beta}$ (else it outputs
  some pair sure to fail, such as $\sequence{\emptystring,\str{0}}$).
  Clearly~$f$ 
  and $\sequence{\alpha,\beta}\in\lang_1$ if and only if 
  $f(\sequence{\alpha,\beta})\in\lang_0$.
  Also, $f$ is clearly computable in polytime.
  Therefore $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Shortest Path} problem, \zcref{prob:ShortestPath}, inputs a 
weighted graph and two vertices and finds a least-weight path between them
(or that no such path exists).
\begin{exparts}
\item The \definend{\probname{Unweighted Shortest Path}} problem\index{Unweighted Shortest Path problem@\probname{Unweighted Shortest Path} problem} 
takes 
as input an unweighted graph and two of its vertices, and finds a
path between the two with the fewest number of edges
(or that no such path exists).
Express it as language decision problem.
Prove that it reduces to the \probname{Shortest Path} problem.
(How does it differ from the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem?)
\item
The \definend{\probname{Longest Path}} problem\index{Longest Path problem@\probname{Longest Path} problem} 
takes as input a weighted graph and two vertices 
and finds a greatest-weight path containing no repeated edges
(or that no such path exists).
Express it as language decision problem.
Prove that this problem reduces to the one in the first item.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
In \zcref{ex:PathLeShortestPath} this is the language given for
the \probname{Shortest Path} problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,B}\suchthat
               \text{there is path from $v_0$ to $v_1$ of total weight at most~$B$}} 
\end{equation*}
\begin{exparts}
\item The \probname{Unweighted Shortest Path} problem is an optimization
problem, calling for the shortest path if one exists.
As with many such problems, to express it as a language decision problem
we use a parameter bound~$B_1$
(the graph $\mathcal{G}$ is unweighted.)
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,B_1}\suchthat
               \text{there is path from $v_0$ to $v_1$ having fewer than~$B_1$ edges}} 
\end{equation*}
(In contrast, \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}
is a decision problem:~it just wants a `yes there is a path' or 
`no there is not' answer.)

For the reduction, the function takes  
input $\sequence{\mathcal{G}_1,v_0,v_1,B_1}$
for the \probname{Unweighted Shortest Path} problem, that is,
$\mathcal{G}_1$ is unweighted.
It makes a weighted graph $\mathcal{H}_1$ by using $\mathcal{G}_1$'s vertices
and giving each of its edges a weight of one.
Then the output of the reduction function is 
$\sequence{\mathcal{H}_1,v_0,v_1,B_1}$.
If there is a path from $\mathcal{H}_1$'s vertex $v_0$ to its~$v_1$ of total
weight~$B_1$ then there is an unweighted path with at most $B_1$-many edges.  
\item
It is an optimization problem so this is the canonical language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_2=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,B_2}\suchthat
               \text{there is path from $v_0$ to $v_1$ having total weight at least as large as~$B_2$}} 
\end{equation*}

The reduction function inputs $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,B_2}$, where
$\mathcal{G}$ is a weighted graph.
It converts to a new graph $\mathcal{H}$ with the same vertices but where 
the weights of all edges have been made the negative of the weight of 
$\mathcal{G}$'s edges.
Then the output of the reduction function is   
$\sequence{\mathcal{H},v_0,v_1,-B_2}$.
The idea is that a path that is short for the negatives of the weights 
is long for the original weights.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


% \recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that $\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}\leq_p\probname{Traveling Salesman}$.\index{Hamiltonian Circuit problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem}
\begin{exparts*}
\item State each as a language decision problem.
\item Produce the reduction function.
\item Argue that it runs in polytime.  
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
The \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem is the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\mathcal{H}\suchthat
                \text{the unweighted graph contains a circuit that includes each vertex exactly once}}
\end{equation*}
The \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem is this decision problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
          \text{some circuit of the weighted graph $\mathcal{G}$ that includes each vertex has total cost at most $B$}}
\end{equation*}
\item
The reduction function 
inputs an instance of \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}, a graph
$\mathcal{H}=\sequence{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}}$
whose edges are unweighted.
It returns
the instance of \probname{Traveling Salesman} that uses the vertex set
$\mathcal{N}$ as cities and that takes the distances between the cities
to be: $d(v_i,v_j)=1$ if $v_iv_j$ is an edge of $\mathcal{G}$ and
$d(v_i,v_j)=2$ if $v_iv_j\notin\mathcal{E}$,
and such that the bound~$B$ is the number of vertices~$|\mathcal{N}|$.

Because of this bound, there will be a \probname{Traveling Salesman} solution
if and only if there is a \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} solution, namely
the \probname{Traveling Salesman} solution uses the edges of 
the Hamiltonian circuit.
That is, $\mathcal{H}\in\lang_0$ if and only if
$f(\mathcal{H})\in\lang_1$.
\item 
The number of
edges is less than twice the number of vertices, so that polytime in the
input graph size is the same as polytime in the number of vertices.
To output the \probname{Traveling Salesman} instance, the algorithm examines
all the pairs of vertices, which is a nested loop and so takes time that
is quadratic in the number of vertices.
So $f$ runs in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise} \label{exer:3SATmixedBelow3SATstrict}
The \ThreeSAT{} problem\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem!Strict variant}
is to decide the satisfiability of 
Conjunctive Normal Form\index{CNF}\index{Propositional logic!Conjunctive Normal form}
propositional logic expressions
where every clause has at most three literals 
(see Appendix~\ref{sec:PropLogic}). 
The \definend{\probname{Strict 3-Satisfiability} problem}\index{Strict 3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{Strict 3-Satisfiability}}
requires that each clause has exactly three unequal literals.
We will show that the two problems are inter-reducible.
\begin{exparts}
\item Express each as a language decision problem.
\item Show the easy half, that 
$\probname{Strict 3-Satisfiability}\leq_p \ThreeSAT$.
\item
  Also show that we can go from clauses with two literals to
  clauses with three by introducing an irrelevant variable:
  $P\vee Q$ is equivalent to
  $(P\vee Q\vee R)\wedge(P\vee Q\vee\neg R)$.
  Along the same lines, show that $P$
  is equivalent to 
  $(P\vee Q\vee R)
  \wedge (P\vee \neg Q\vee R)
  \wedge (P\vee Q\vee \neg R)
  \wedge (P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R)$.
\item     
Show
$\ThreeSAT\leq_p \probname{Strict 3-Satisfiability}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This is the language for the \ThreeSAT{} problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{E\suchthat \text{$E$ is a satisfiable Boolean expression in CNF where each clause has at most three literals}}    
\end{equation*}
This is the language for \probname{Strict 3-Satisfiability}.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{E\suchthat \text{$E$ is a satisfiable Boolean expression in CNF where each clause has exactly three literals}}    
\end{equation*}
\item
Any instance of \probname{Strict 3-Satisfiability} is an instance
of \ThreeSAT.
So the reduction function is the identity function.
\item
We are asked to show that the two-literal Propositional Logic expression
$E_0=P\vee Q$ is equivalent to the 
the expression
$E_1=(P\vee Q\vee R)\wedge(P\vee Q\vee\neg R)$
where each clause has three literals.

The most straightforward thing is to use a truth table
to compare the two expressions.
\begin{display}
\begin{tabular}[t]{*{3}{c}|*{4}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{$P$} 
     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$Q$} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R$} 
     &$E_0$ &$P\vee Q\vee R$ &$P\vee Q\vee\neg R$ 
       &$E_1$   \\
  \hline
  $F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$   \\
  $F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$   \\
  $F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
  $F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\[1ex]
  $T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
  $T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
  $T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
  $T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   
\end{tabular}
\end{display}

The same technique works for the one-literal expression $E_2=P$.
It is equivalent to the expression
  $E_3=(P\vee Q\vee R)
  \wedge (P\vee \neg Q\vee R)
  \wedge (P\vee Q\vee \neg R)
  \wedge (P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R)$
where each clause has three literals.
\begin{display}
\begin{tabular}[t]{*{3}{c}|*{6}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{$P$} 
     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$Q$} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R$} 
     &$E_2$
       &$(P\vee Q\vee R)$
       &$(P\vee \neg Q\vee R)$   
       &$(P\vee Q\vee \neg R)$   
       &$(P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R)$
       &$E_3$   
  \\
  \hline
  $F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$   \\
  $F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$   \\
  $F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$   \\
  $F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$   \\[1ex]
  $T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
  $T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
  $T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
  $T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$   \\
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
\item The reduction function inputs expressions
in Conjunctive Normal form 
$E=\sigma_0\wedge \sigma_1\wedge \ldots\,\sigma_k$,
where the $\sigma_i$ clauses are disjunctions of three or fewer literals.
It must output equivalent propositional logic expressions that also are in 
Conjunctive Normal form but where the clauses are disjunctions of 
exactly three literals.
The prior item shows how the function converts the one to the other.
Obviously the function can run in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}\label{exer:IndSetLeqVertexCover}\label{exer:DefVertexCover}
Consider graphs without loops.
The \probname{Independent Set} problem\index{Independent Set problem@\probname{Independent Set} problem} 
inputs a graph and a bound, and
decides if there is a set of vertices of size at least equal to the bound,
that are not connected by any edge.
The \probname{Vertex Cover} problem\index{Vertex Cover problem@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem}
inputs a graph and a bound and decides if there is a vertex set 
of size less than or equal to the bound, 
such that every edge contains at least one vertex in the set.
\begin{exparts}
\item State each as a language decision problem.
\item
Consider this graph.
Find a vertex cover with four elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity22.pdf}
\end{center}
\item In that graph
find an independent set with six elements.
\item Show that in a graph, $S$ is an independent set if and only if
  $\mathcal{N}-S$ is a vertex cover, where $\mathcal{N}$ is the
  set of vertices.
\item Conclude that $\probname{Independent Set}\leq_p \probname{Vertex Cover}$
and that $\probname{Vertex Cover}\leq_p\probname{Independent Set}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
The \probname{Independent Set} problem is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G,B}}\suchthat
               \text{there is $I\subseteq\mathcal{N}(_{\mathcal{G}}$ with 
                    $B\leq \sizeof{I}$
                    such that no edge contains two vertices from~$I$}}
\end{equation*}
\probname{Vertex Cover} is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{H},D}\suchthat
               \text{there is $C\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{H}}$ 
                     with $\sizeof{C}\leq D$
                     such that every edge contains at least one vertex from~$C$}}
\end{equation*}
\item A vertex cover with $k=4$ elements is $S=\set{q_2,q_5,q_8,q_9}$.
\item An independent set with $\hat{k}=6$ elements is 
  $\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4,q_6,q_7}$.
\item By definition, a set~$I$ of vertices is independent if every edge contains
either zero elements or one element of~$I$.
Also by definition, a set $C$ of vertices is a vertex cover if every edge
contains either one element or two elements of~$C$.
Thus a set~$S$ of vertices is independent if and only if its complement
$\mathcal{N}-S$ is a vertex cover.
\item We will do $\probname{Independent Set}\leq_p\probname{Vertex Cover}$; the 
other reduction is similar.
By the prior item, there is a relationship between independent sets and
vertex covers.
In particular, if there is an independent set of size~$\hat{k}$ then there is a 
vertex cover of size $\sizeof{\mathcal{N}}-\hat{k}$.

The definition requires that we produce a polytime computable
function~$f\!$.
Given an instance of \probname{Independent Set} 
$\sigma=\sequence{\mathcal{G},\hat{k}}$,
the function converts it to an instance of  \probname{Vertex Cover}
by adjusting the bound, 
$f(\sigma)=\sequence{\mathcal{G},\lh{\mathcal{G}}-\hat{k}}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Vertex Cover} problem\index{Vertex Cover problem@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem}
inputs a graph and a bound and decides if there is a vertex set 
of size at most equal to the bound
such that every edge contains at least one vertex in the set.
The \definend{\probname{Set Cover}} problem\index{Set Cover problem@\probname{Set Cover} problem} 
inputs a set~$S$, a collection of subsets $S_0\subseteq S$, \ldots{}  
$S_{n}\subseteq S$, and a bound, and
decides if there is a subcollection of the $S_j$, with a number of sets at most
equal to the bound,
whose union is~$S$.
\begin{exparts}
\item State each as a language decision problem.
\item Find a vertex cover of size six for this graph.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity27.pdf}
\end{center}
\item Make a set~$S$ consisting of all of that graph's edges, 
  and for each~$v$ make a subset $S_v$ of the edges incident on
  that vertex.
  Find a set cover.
\item Show that $\probname{Vertex Cover}\leq_p\probname{Set Cover}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item 
\probname{Vertex Cover} is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
               \text{there is $C\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{G})$ 
                     with $\sizeof{C}\leq B$
                     such that every edge contains a vertex from~$C$}}
\end{equation*}
\probname{Set Cover} is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{S,S_0,\ldots{}S_{k-1},D}\suchthat
               \text{each $S_j$ is a subset of~$S$, and $k\leq D$, and 
                     $S_0\cup\cdots{} \cup S_{k-1}=S$}}
\end{equation*}
\item One vertex cover is $C=\set{q_0,q_3,q_4,q_6,q_7,q_9}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity28.pdf}
\end{center}
\item The set of edges is $S=\set{a,b,\ldots{}\, n}$.
The subsets are these.
\begin{align*}
  &S_0=\set{a,b},\;
  S_1=\set{a,c},\;
  S_2=\set{b,d,e},\;
  S_3=\set{c,d,f,g},\;
  S_4=\set{e,h,i},       
  \\  
  &\qquad
  S_5=\set{g,h,j,k},\;
  S_6=\set{f,j,l},\;
  S_7=\set{i,k,m},\;
  S_8=\set{l,m,n},\;
  S_9=\set{n}
\end{align*}
To find a set cover we can take 
the subsets associated with vertices in the
vertex cover.
\begin{equation*}
  S_{q_0}\cup S_{q_3}\cup S_{q_4}\cup S_{q_6}\cup S_{q_7}\cup S_{q_9}
  =\set{a,b}\cup\set{c,d,f,g}\cup\set{e,h,i}\cup\set{f,j,l}
     \cup\set{i,k,m}\cup\set{n}
  =S
\end{equation*}
\item We must produce a reduction function~$f\!$.
It inputs an instance $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$ of the
\probname{Vertex Cover} problem.
The computation is:~let~$S=\mathcal{E}$, the set of of all edges.  
For each vertex~$v$, construct $S_v\subseteq S$ consisting of the
edges incident on~$v$.
Then the output of the function is an instance of the \probname{Set Cover}
problem, 
$f(\sequence{\mathcal{G},B})=\sequence{S,S_{v_0},\ldots{} B}$.
Clearly we can compute this function in polytime, and clearly
also $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\in\lang_0$ if and only if
$\sequence{S,S_{v_0},\ldots{} B}\in\lang_1$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise} \label{exer:DefMaxFlow}
In this network, each edge is labeled
with a capacity.
% (Imagine railroad lines going from $q_0$ to~$q_6$.)
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity25.pdf}
\end{center}
The 
\definend{\probname{Max-Flow}} problem\index{Max-Flow problem@\probname{Max-Flow} problem} 
is to find the maximum total amount that can flow across the 
network,
typically by using many paths at once.
That is, we will find a \definend{flow}~$F_{q_i,q_j}$\index{flow} 
for each edge, 
subject to the constraints that the flow through an edge must not exceed
its capacity and that the flow into a vertex must equal the flow out
(except for the source~$q_0$ and the sink~$q_6$).
% The problem is to find the edge flows so that the source and sink get maximal
% total flow. 
The \probname{Linear Programming} problem,
described on page~\pageref{prob:LinearProgramming},\index{Linear Programming language decision problem@\probname{Linear Programming} language decision problem}
is to optimize a linear function 
$F(x_0,\ldots x_n)=c_0x_0+\cdots+c_{n}x_n$ % (either maximize or minimize it),
subject to linear constraints, ones of the form 
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_{n}\leq b_i$ 
or
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_{n}\geq b_i$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Express each as a language decision problem.
\item For the above network, find the maximum flow by eye.
\item For each edge $v_iv_j$ in that network, define a variable $x_{i,j}$.
Describe the constraints on that variable imposed by the edge's capacity.
Also describe the constraints on the set of variables imposed 
by the limitation that for many vertices the flow in must equal
the flow out.
Finally, use the variables to give an expression to optimize 
in order to get maximum flow.  
\item Show that 
  $\probname{Max-Flow}\leq_p\probname{Linear Programming}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item \probname{Max-Flow} has this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
               \text{the flow~$F$ meets the constraints of $\mathcal{G}$ and the flow into the sink is at least~$B$}}
\end{equation*}
This is the language for the \probname{Linear Programming} optimization problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\vec{x},C,Z,D}\suchthat
                \text{the point~$\vec{x}$ meets the constraints~$C$ and
                     makes~$Z(\vec{x})\geq D$}}
\end{equation*}
\item There are algorithms to solve these problems, but for small ones we
can do it by eye.
The maximum flow is~$5$, with each edge's numbers
given in parentheses.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity26.pdf}  
\end{center}
\item The variables are $x_{0,1}, \ldots{}\,x_{5,6}$.
These are the capacity constraints.
\begin{align*}
  &0\leq x_{0,1}\leq 3,\quad 
  0\leq x_{0,2}\leq 2,\quad 
  0\leq x_{0,3}\leq 1,\quad
  0\leq x_{1,3}\leq 2,\quad
  0\leq x_{1,4}\leq 2,\quad
  \\  &\qquad  
  0\leq x_{2,3}\leq 2,\quad
  0\leq x_{2,5}\leq 1,\quad  
  0\leq x_{3,4}\leq 4,\quad
  0\leq x_{3,5}\leq 2,\quad
  0\leq x_{3,6}\leq 2,\quad
  \\  &\qquad  
  0\leq x_{4,6}\leq 1,\quad\text{and}\quad
  0\leq x_{5,6}\leq 2
\end{align*}
(These fit the definition of the problem because each is 
a combination of two linear constraints.
For instance, $0\leq x_{0,1}\leq 3$ is a combination of 
$0\leq x_{0,1}$ and $x_{0,1}\leq 3$.)  
These are the flow constraints.
\begin{align*}
  &x_{0,1}=x_{1,3}+x_{1,4},\quad
  x_{0,2}=x_{2,3}+x_{2,5},\quad
  x_{0,3}+x_{1,3}+x_{2,3}=x_{3,4}+x_{3,5}+x_{3,6},
  \\  &\qquad  
  x_{1,4}+x_{3,4}=x_{4,6},\quad\text{and}\quad
  x_{2,5}+x_{3,5}=x_{5,6}
\end{align*}
(Here also, these fit the definition of the problem because each is 
a combination of two linear constraints.
For instance, $x_{0,1}=x_{1,3}+x_{1,4}$ is a combination of 
$0\leq x_{0,1}-x_{1,3}-x_{1,4}$ and $x_{0,1}-x_{1,3}-x_{1,4}\leq 0$.)  
We want to maximize $Z=x_{3,6}+x_{4,6}+x_{5,6}$.
\item We must construct a reduction function~$f\!$.
The input is a triple $\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},B}$.
Use that information to construct variables,  capacity constraints and
flow constraints~$C$, and an optimization expression~$Z$.
In particular, the flow~$F$ (shown in parentheses in the network above) 
gives an associated point~$\vec{x}$ 
in the \probname{Linear Programming} instance.

Clearly we can do that construction in polytime.
Clearly also $\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},B}\in\lang_0$
if and only if $\sequence{\vec{x},C,Z,B}\in\lang_1$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The 
\probname{Max-Flow} problem\index{Max-Flow problem@\probname{Max-Flow} problem} 
inputs a directed graph where each edge is labeled with a capacity,
and the task is to find a 
the maximum amount that can flow from the source node to the sink node
(a diagram is in \zcref{exer:DefMaxFlow}).
The \citetext{\definend{\probname{Matching}} problem}{%
  This is traditionally called the \probname{Marriage} problem,
  where the women list men that they would accept and men accept any match. 
  But perhaps 
  a recasting suits the times.}\index{Matching problem@\probname{Matching} problem}\index{Marriage problem@\probname{Marriage} problem|see{\probname{Matching} problem}}
starts with two same-sized sets, the rock bands,~$B$, and 
potential drummers,~$D$.
Each band $b\in B$ has a set $S_b\subseteq D$ of drummers
that they would agree to take on. 
Drummers will go anywhere that there is employment.
The goal is to make the most matches.
\begin{exparts}
\item Consider four bands $B=\set{b_0,b_1,b_2,b_3}$ and
  drummers $D=\set{d_0,d_1,d_2,d_3}$.
  Band $b_0$ likes drummers $d_0$ and~$d_2$.
  Band $b_1$ likes only drummer~$d_1$, and 
  $b_2$ also likes only~$d_1$.
  Band~$b_3$ like the sound of both $d_2$ and~$d_3$.
  What is the largest number of matches?
\item Draw a graph with the bands on the left and the drummers on the right.
  Make an arrow from a band to a drummer if there is a potential match.
  Now add a source and a sink node to make a flow diagram.
\item Express each as a language decision problem.
\item Show that 
  $\probname{Matching}\leq_p\probname{Max-Flow}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The maximum number of matches is three, as in 
  $(b_0,d_0)$, $(b_1,d_1)$, and $(b_3,d_2)$.
\item Here is a network diagram.
Each edge has weight~$1$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity29.pdf}
\end{center}
\item The \probname{Max-Flow} problem has this language, where $W$ is a bound.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},W}\suchthat
               \text{the flow~$F$ meets the constraints of $\mathcal{G}$ and the flow into the sink is at least~$W$}}
\end{equation*}
The \probname{Matching} problem has this, where $X$ is a bound.
The pairs $(b_j,d_{i_j})$ are band-drummer matches.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X}\suchthat
            \text{each $d_{i_j}\in B_j$ and $m\geq X$}}
\end{equation*}
\item A reduction function~$f$
is given a sequence 
$\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X}$.
It constructs a network that, from left to right, has a source node,
a bank of band nodes, a bank of drummer nodes, and a sink node.
Every edge is directed, and every edge has weight~$1$.
The pairs $(b,d)$ in the input give a flow~$F\!$.
So the output is the triple
$f(\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X})=\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},X}$.
Clearly we can compute this~$f$ in polytime.
Also clearly, 
$\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X}\in\lang_1$ if and only if $\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},X}\in\lang_0$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:SATBelow3SAT}
\begin{credit}
  SE user JesusIsLord at \url{https://cstheory.stackexchange.com/a/47031/4731}
\end{credit}
We will show that 
the \ThreeSAT{} problem\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
is inter-reducible with 
\SAT.\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}
These problems assume that the propositional logic expressions
are in Conjunctive Normal 
form,\index{CNF}\index{Propositional logic!Conjunctive Normal form}
made of clauses joined with $\wedge$'s, so they have the form
$C_0\wedge C_1\ldots$,
where each clause consists of literals joined by $\vee$'s.
% More on CNF is in Appendix~\ref{sec:PropLogic}.)
A propositional logic
statement $S$ is satisfiable if there is an assignment of 
the truth values \true{} or \false{} to the Boolean variables in that statement
making it evaluate to true.
\begin{exparts}
\item Show the easy half, that 
$\ThreeSAT\leq_p \SAT$.
\item For the other direction start with a clause
$C=p_0\vee p_1\vee p_2\vee p_3\vee p_4$
with more than three literals (each $p_i$ is either a Boolean variable
$P_i$ or its negation $\neg P_i$).
Introduce a new Boolean variable $A$ and consider
the expression
$E=(A\vee p_0\vee p_1)\wedge(\neg A\vee p_2\vee p_3\vee p_4)$.
Show that an assignment of 
the truth values \true{} or \false{} to the variables
$P_0,\ldots\, P_4$ makes $C=\true$
if and only if $E=\true$ under an extension of that assignment
that uses the same values for the $P_i$'s and adds a truth value for $A$.  
\item     
Show that
$\SAT\leq_p \ThreeSAT$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
This is the language for the \ThreeSAT{} problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{E\suchthat \text{$E$ is a satisfiable Boolean expression in CNF where each clause has at most three literals}}    
\end{equation*}
This is the language for \SAT.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{E\suchthat \text{$E$ is a satisfiable Boolean expression in CNF}}    
\end{equation*}
\begin{exparts}
\item
The reduction function $f$ must input an instance of $\ThreeSAT$, 
a satisfiable propositional logic
expression in Conjunctive Normal form where the 
clauses have at most three literals.
It must output an instance of $\SAT$, 
a satisfiable propositional logic
expression in Conjunctive Normal form.
So take $f$ to be the identity $f(\sigma)=\sigma$.
Obviously it runs in polytime.
\item 
First suppose that there is an assignment of 
the truth values \true{} or \false{} to the variables
$P_0,\ldots\, P_4$ such that $C$ is true.
Then at least one of the literals 
$p_0,\ldots\,p_4$ evaluates to \true\!.
If $p_o$ or $p_1$ is \true{} then setting $A$ to \false{} makes
$E=(A\vee p_0\vee p_1)\wedge(\neg A\vee p_2\vee p_3\vee p_4)$
evaluate to \true\!.
Otherwise setting $A$ to \true{} makes $E=\true$\!.

Now suppose that no assignment of 
the truth values to the variables
$P_0,\ldots\, P_4$ makes $C$ evaluate to \true\!.
Then no matter what truth value we assign to~$A$,
one of $E$'s two clauses
$(A\vee p_0\vee p_1)$ and $(\neg A\vee p_2\vee p_3\vee p_4)$ is \false\!.
Hence for every assignment of truth values to 
$P_0,\ldots\,P_4$ and~$A$ the expression~$E$ gives~\false.
\item
The reduction function inputs  statements~$S$ in Conjunctive Normal form.
It must output Conjunctive Normal form statements where the clauses
have three or fewer literals.

The prior item suggests how the reduction function does that.
It replaces each clause~$C$
having more than three literals 
with a number of clauses having three or fewer.
For instance starting with input $p_0\vee p_1\vee p_2\vee p_3\vee p_4$,
the reduction function can go to 
$(A\vee p_0\vee p_1)\wedge(\neg A\vee p_2\vee p_3\vee p_4)$,
and then to the output 
$(A\vee p_0\vee p_1)\wedge(B\vee \neg A\vee p_2)\wedge(\neg B\vee p_3\vee p_4)$.
Clearly that transformation
can be done in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\recommended
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:Def3SATa}
% See page~\pageref{prob:SAT} for a definition of the 
% \ThreeSAT{} problem.\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
The \probname{Independent Set} problem\index{Independent Set problem@\probname{Independent Set} problem} 
inputs a graph and a bound, and
decides if there is a set of vertices, of size at least equal to the bound,
that are not connected by any edge.
\begin{exparts}
\item In this graph,
find an independent set with at least $B=3$ members.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity30.pdf}
\end{center}
\item State \probname{Independent Set} as a language decision problem.
\item State \ThreeSAT{} as a language decision problem.
\item Decide if $E=(P_0\vee\neg P_1\vee\neg P_2)\wedge(P_1\vee P_2\vee\neg P_3)$
  is satisfiable.
\item With the expression~$E$, 
  make a triangle for each of the two clauses, where the vertices of the
  first are labeled $c_{0,0}$, $\bar{c}_{0,1}$, 
  and~$\bar{c}_{0,2}$, 
  while the vertices of the second are 
  $c_{1,1}$, $c_{1,2}$, and $\bar{c}_{1,3}$.
  In addition to the edges forming the triangles, 
  also put one connecting $\bar{c}_{0,1}$ with~$c_{1,1}$, 
  and one connecting $\bar{c}_{0,2}$ with~$c_{1,2}$.
\item Sketch an argument that
  $\ThreeSAT\leq_p\probname{Independent Set}$.\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
  \hint~follow the development in \zcref{ex:SatReducesToClique}.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The natural independent set is this.
\begin{display}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity31.pdf}
\end{display}
\item This is the language for it as a language decision problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
        \text{$\mathcal{G}$ has at least $B$ vertices that do not share an edge}}
\end{equation*}
\item This is the language for the \ThreeSAT{} problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{E\suchthat \text{$E$ is a satisfiable Boolean expression in CNF where each clause has at most three literals}}    
\end{equation*}
\item Here is the truth table.
\begin{display} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}[t]{cccc|ccc}
  $P_0$  &$P_1$  &$P_2$  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$P_3$} 
   &$P_0\vee \neg P_1\vee \neg P_2$ 
   &$P_1\vee P_2\vee \neg P_3$  
   &$E$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$   \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\[1ex]     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$  \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$  \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$  \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$  \\[1ex]     
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
Any line ending in \true{} shows that it is satisfiable.
\item
Here is the graph.
\begin{display}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity42.pdf}
\end{display}
\item 
The development in \zcref{ex:SatReducesToClique} points out that a 
CNF formula is satisfiable if and only if at least one literal in each
clause evaluates to \true\!. 
Literals from different clauses are compatible if they are not based on the
same Boolean variable $P_i$, 
or if they are based on the same $P_i$ and either both are `$P_i$'
or both are `$\neg P_i$'.

So we make a graph whose
vertices are constructed from the literals. 
The vertices associated with clause~$i$ are:~$c_{i,j}$ 
if clause~$i$ has a literal $P_j$, and    
$\bar{c}_{i,j}$ if clause~$i$ has~$\neg P_j$.

As to edges, for any clause $C_i$ 
connect together all of the vertices $c_{i,j}$ from that clause.
In addition, 
where $i\neq \hat{i}$ connect 
all vertices of the forms $c_{i,j}$ and $\bar{c}_{\hat{i},j}$, since
they arise from literals that are incompatible
($\P_j$ and $\neg P_j$).

Recall that an independent set~$I\subseteq \mathcal{N}$ 
is one where for each edge at most one vertex is in~$I$.
Because the vertices associated with each clause are all connected, the fact
that a set is independent implies that at most one vertex in that grouping
is in that set.

So assume that the graph has an independent set~$I$ 
with as many members as there are clauses. 
Then $I$ contains exactly one vertex for each clause. 
Also, because our construction connects vertices associated with incompatible
literals, the set $I$ contains only vertices associate with compatible
literals.

Therefore the graph has an independent set containing as many vertices 
as there are clauses if and only if the underlying expression is satisfiable.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% Additional?
% \begin{exercise}
% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/133213/50343
% \end{exercise}


% 3-SAT < Integer LP
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user user326210, \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/2564255}
\end{credit}
% See page~\pageref{prob:SAT} for a definition of the 
% \ThreeSAT{} problem.\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
The decision problem 
\definend{\probname{Integer Linear Programming}}\index{Integer Linear Programming problem@\probname{Integer Linear Programming} problem!decision problem}
starts with a list of linear inequalities with variables
$x_0,\ldots x_n$, such as that 
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_{n}\leq b_i$ or
that $b_i\leq a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_{n}$,
where the numbers $a_{i,j}$ and $b_i$ are integers.
It decides if there is a sequence of integers $\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_{n}}$
that satisfies all of the constraints.
\begin{exparts}
\item Consider the propositional logic clause $P_0\vee \neg P_1\vee\neg P_2$.
Create variables $x_0$, $x_1$, and~$x_2$ and list linear constraints such
that each must be either~$0$ or~$1$.
Give a linear inequality that holds if and only
if the clause is true.
\item
Show that $\ThreeSAT\leq_p\probname{Integer Linear Programming}$.\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
These constraints
ensure in this integer programming context that each variable is 
either~$0$ or~$1$.  
\begin{equation*}
  0\leq x_0, \quad x_0\leq 1,\quad
  0\leq x_1, \quad x_1\leq 1, \quad
  0\leq x_2, \quad x_2\leq 1,
\end{equation*}
As to the expression, use $1\leq x_0+(1-x_1)+(1-x_2)$.
\item
We must produce a reduction function~$f\!$.
It inputs a propositional logic expression.
For each variable $P_i$ in that expression, create an integer variable $x_i$.
To force each~$x_i$ to be either $0$ or~$1$, include the constraints
$0\leq x_i$ and $x_i\leq 1$.
For every clause in the propositional logic expression, add a 
constraint to the \probname{Integer Linear Programming} 
instance as in the prior item.
\begin{equation*}
  P_i\vee \neg P_j\vee\neg P_k
  \qquad \Longleftrightarrow \qquad
  1\leq x_i+(1-x_j)+(1-x_k)
\end{equation*}
Clearly we can write $f$ to run in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A multivariable polynomial\index{multivariable polynomial} 
is one with more than one variable,
such as $p(x,y)=x^2-2y^2-1$.
Consider the problem~\probname{D} of deciding whether such a
polynomial has any integer roots.
We will show that $\ThreeSAT\leq_p \probname{D}$.\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
\begin{exparts}
\item State \probname{D} as a language decision problem.
\item 
Recall that \ThreeSAT{} inputs CNF propositional logic expressions.
In CNF a clause
is \true{} if and only if any of its
literals is~\true\!.
For instance, $E_0=P_0\vee \neg P_1$  
is true if and only if $P_0=T$ or $\neg P_1=T$.
Associate $E_0$ with the set 
$S_{E_0}=\set{x_0(1-x_0),x_1(1-x_1),x_0(1-x_1)}$  of three polynomials.
Argue that all three equal~$0$ if and only if
both $x_0$ and~$x_1$ has a value of either $0$ or~$1$ and 
either $x_0=0$ or $x_1=1$.
\item
For the expression  
$E_1=(P_0\vee\neg P_1\vee\neg P_2)\wedge(P_1\vee P_2\vee\neg P_3)$,
produce a set of polynomials $S_{E_1}$ with the analogous properties.
\item 
Combine the polynomials from $S_{E_1}$ into a single
polynomial so that it has an overall value of~$0$
if and only if all the members of~$S$ have a value of~$0$.
\item
Show that $\ThreeSAT\leq_p \probname{D}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
It is the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
D=\set{p\suchthat \text{$p$ is a polynomial and there is $\vec{c}\in\Z^n$ so that $p(\vec{c})=0$}}
\end{equation*}
\item
Suppose first that all three polynomials in
$S_{E_0}=\set{x_0(1-x_0),x_1(1-x_1),x_0(1-x_1)}$
have a value of~$0$.
The first polynomial $x_0(1-x_0)$ evaluates to~$0$ 
if and only if $x_0$ is either~$0$ or~$1$.
The second polynomial gives the same for~$x_1$.
The third evaluates to~$0$ if and only if either $0=x_0$ or $1=x_1$.

The converse, that if both variables equal $0$ or~$1$
and either $0=x_0$ or $1=x_1$ then all three polynomials
evaluate to~$0$, is clear.
\item
For
$E_1=(P_0\vee\neg P_1\vee\neg P_2)\wedge(P_1\vee P_2\vee\neg P_3)$
the analogous set is this.
\begin{equation*}
  S_{E_1}=\set{x_0(1-x_0),\, x_1(1-x_1),\, x_2(1-x_2),x_3(1-x_3),\,
           x_0(1-x_1)(1-x_2),\, x_1x_2(1-x_3)}
\end{equation*}
\item
Take the sum of squares.
\begin{align*}
  p(x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3)
    &=
    [x_0(1-x_0)]^2 
    +[x_1(1-x_1)]^2 
    +[x_2(1-x_2)]^2
    +[x_3(1-x_3)]^2  \\
    &\qquad+[x_0(1-x_1)(1-x_2)]^2 
    +[x_1x_2(1-x_3)]^2
\end{align*}
\item We must produce the reduction function~$f\!$, 
that inputs an instance of \probname{3-Satisfiability},
a Boolean expression~$E$ in which all clauses have three or fewer literals, and
outputs an instance~$p$ of \probname{D}.
We must verify that
$E$ is satisfiable if and only of $p$ has zero for some 
integer $n$-tuple. 
(In addition, it will be clear that $f$ runs in polytime.)

This function constructs the polynomial~$p$ following the pattern outlined
in the prior items.
For each variable~$P_i$ used in the input expression~$E$, 
put $x_i(1-x_1)$ into the set~$S_E$.
In addition, for each clause $L_{i_0}\vee L_{i_1}\vee L_{i_2}$ where
