$L_i$ is a literal, add a polynomial:~ if $L_i=P_i$ then it
has the factor $x_i$, while for a negation
$L_i=\neg P_i$ it has the factor $(1-x_i)$. 
Finally, the polynomial~$p$ is the sum of the squares of the polynomials
from the set~$S_E$.

For one direction of the verification, suppose that $E$ is satisfiable.
For all atoms $P_i$ that are assigned~$T$, take $x_i=0$,
while if~$P_i$ is assigned~$F$ then take $x_i=1$. 
As a result, each polynomial member of $S_E$ has the value of~$0$.
Consequently the sum of squares polynomial~$p$ 
has a value of~$0$, and so the tuple of $x_i$'s is a zero of that polynomial.

Now for the other direction.
Suppose that we have a tuple of integers~$\vec{c}$ such that~$p(\vec{c})=0$.
Because~$p$ is a sum of squares, each term must have a value of~$0$.
The terms of the form $x_i(1-x_i)$ imply that
$x_i=0$ or $x_i=1$.
The terms that match the form of a clause imply that
there is a matching assignment to the
Boolean variables~$P_i$ that makes the clause true
(namely, $x_i=0$ is associated with $P_i=T$, and $x_i=1$ is associated with
$P_i=F$).
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Thanks to P~Rombach of the University of Vermont for help with this.    
\end{credit}
Our definition of the \probname{Traveling Salesman} 
problem\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem}
is based on an undirected graph.
The \citetext{\definend{\probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman}}}{%
  \autocite{Jonker}}
problem\index{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman} problem}\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem!Asymmetric@\probname{Asymmetric}}
takes place on a directed graph,
where the graph's matrix may be asymmetric
across the upper left to lower right diagonal, as below.
(When there is no path the matrix entry is blank.)
\begin{equation*}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir problems/problems52.pdf}}
  \hspace*{4em}
  \begin{blockarray}{*{4}{c}}
           &g_0 &g_1 &g_2    \\
    \begin{block}{c(*{3}{c})}
      g_0  &    &1   &2    \\
      g_1  &3   &    &4    \\
      g_2  &5   &6   &    \\
    \end{block}
  \end{blockarray}
\end{equation*}
An example of such a problem is when nodes are cities and edges are flights, 
with the weight of an edge being the flight's cost.
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that $\text{\probname{Traveling Salesman}}\leq_p\text{\probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman}}$. 
\item For the other direction,
  an asymmetric problem instance is a directed graph $\mathcal{G}$
  with vertices $g_0,\ldots{}\, g_n$.
  (Take all edge weights to be positive because otherwise 
  we can adjust them all up by $-v+1$ where 
  $v$ is the most negative edge weight.) 
  Make the associated symmetric graph~$\mathcal{H}$, having  
  vertices
  $h_0,\ldots{}\,h_{n}$ and $\hat{h}_0,\ldots{}\,\hat{h}_n$,
  in two stages.  
  First, if $g_i$ is connected to~$g_j$ with weight~$w$ 
  then connect $\hat{h}_i$ to~$h_j$, and also 
  $h_i$ to~$\hat{h}_j$, with weight~$w$. 
  Using $\mathcal{G}$ shown above, produce the matrix for this graph,
  verify that it is symmetric, and draw it.
\item For the second stage of defining $\mathcal{H}$,
  let~$w$ be the product of $\mathcal{G}$'s maximum edge weight 
  and twice the number of its vertices.
  For all indices~$i$, connect $h_i$ with~$\hat{h}_i$ 
  in both directions with an edge of weight~$-w-1$.
  Verify that the resulting graph matrix is symmetric
  and draw the graph.
\item Show that $\text{\probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman}}\leq_p\text{\probname{Traveling Salesman}}$.
  % Using the prior items to start with an asymmetric graph~$\mathcal{G}$ and 
  % convert to a symmetric graph~$\hat{\mathcal{H}}$.
  % whose optimum solution leads to the solution for~$\mathcal{G}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The reduction function must take in an instance of the symmetric
problem and return an instance of the asymmetric problem.
But a symmetric instance is a special case of an asymmetric instance.
So the reduction function can be the identity function.
\item This is the graph matrix for the first stage of 
constructing~$\mathcal{H}$. 
It is symmetric along the upper left to lower right
diagonal.   
\begin{equation*}\small
  \begin{blockarray}{*{7}{c}}
           &h_0 &h_1 &h_2 &\hat{h}_0 &\hat{h}_1 &\hat{h}_2    \\
    \begin{block}{c(*{6}{c})}
      h_0        &  &  &  &  &3  &5 \\
      h_1        &  &  &  &1 &   &6 \\
      h_2        &  &  &  &2 &4  &  \\
      \hat{h}_0  &  &1 &2 &  &   & \\
      \hat{h}_1  &3 &  &4 &  &   & \\
      \hat{h}_2  &5 &6 &  &  &   & \\
    \end{block}
  \end{blockarray}
\end{equation*}
This is the graph of the first stage of~$\mathcal{H}$ 
showing the symmetric edges with arrows on both ends.
\begin{display}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity43.pdf}    
\end{display}
We won't distinguish between that and an undirected graph.
\item The adjusted matrix, the graph matrix for $\mathcal{H}$, 
is also symmetric.    
\begin{equation*}\small
  \begin{blockarray}{*{7}{c}}
           &h_0 &h_1 &h_2 &\hat{h}_0 &\hat{h}_1 &\hat{h}_2    \\
    \begin{block}{c(*{6}{c})}
      h_0        &    &    &    &-37 &3   &5 \\
      h_1        &    &    &    &1   &-37 &6 \\
      h_2        &    &    &    &2   &4   &-37 \\
      \hat{h}_0  &-37 &1   &2   &    &    & \\
      \hat{h}_1  &3   &-37 &4   &    &    & \\
      \hat{h}_2  &5   &6   &-37 &    &    & \\
    \end{block}
  \end{blockarray}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{ \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity44.pdf} }
\end{equation*}
\item 
We first cover the case where the asymmetric instance is 
the pictured graph~$\mathcal{G}$
and the symmetric instance is the derived graph~$\mathcal{H}$.
To make a circuit in
$\mathcal{G}$, the only possibilities are going clockwise and going
counterclockwise.
Clockwise costs~$11$, so the least-expensive circuit
is $g_0\to g_1\to g_2\to g_0$, of total cost~$10$.

Observe that twice the number of vertices in $\mathcal{G}$ equals the number of
vertices in~$\mathcal{H}$, and so
$w$ is the number of edges in~$\mathcal{H}$ multiplied
by the maximum edge weight in~$\mathcal{H}$.
For $\mathcal{H}$, 
the large negative weights of $-w-1$ ensure that those edges must be part of a
circuit.

To prove that,
first observe that a to visit every vertex, a circuit must have a number of 
edges equal to the number of vertices, which here is six edges.
A circuit that does not use a edge with weight $-37$ has 
total weight at least $6\cdot 1$.
Replacing one positive weight with $-37$ means that any circuit
now has total weight at most $-37\cdot 1+6\cdot 5=-7$,
so that lowers the total weight.
From there, every circuit with one $-37$ edge 
has total weight at least $(-37)\cdot 1+1\cdot 5=-32$, while
replacing another positive weight edge means that the most a
circuit can weigh is $(-37)\cdot 2+6\cdot 4=-50$,
an improvement.
Finally, every circuit containing two $-37$ edges totals at 
least $(-37)\cdot 2+1\cdot 4=-70$,
while replacing a positive weight edge means that a circuit can weigh 
at most $(-37)\cdot 3+6\cdot 3=-93$.
So three $-37$'s is unbeatable, and by inspection there is such a circuit.
% sage: (-37)*1+6*5
% -7
% sage: (-37)*1+1*5
% -32
% sage: (-37)*2+6*4
% -50
% sage: (-37)*2+1*4
% -70
% sage: (-37)*3+6*3
% -93

Therefore $\mathcal{H}$'s optimal solution is 
$h_0\to \hat{h}_0\to h_1\to\hat{h}_1\to h_2\to\hat{h}_2\to\hat{h}_2\to h_0$.
It alternates between edges of the form $h_i\hat{h}_i$
and those of the form $\hat{h}_ih_j$, 
where edges with matching subscripts
$g_ig_j$ are in $\mathcal{G}$'s optimal circuit. 

The argument generalizes to any number of edges in~$\mathcal{G}$.
Let $v$ be the number of vertices in $\mathcal{G}$
and let $m$ be its maximum edge weight, so that $w=vm$.
Replacing a positive weight edge will change what a circuit
can weight by $-w-1=-vm-1$, and the remaining edges in any circuit using
this replaced edge have a total weight less than $vm$, 
so there is a net decrease. 
And there is a circuit using $v$-may edges of weight $-w-1$.

The reduction function can clearly do the change 
from $\mathcal{G}$ to $\mathcal{H}$ in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
The \definend{\probname{Assignment} problem}\index{Assignment problem@\probname{Assignment} problem} inputs a set of workers 
$W=\set{w_0,\ldots{}\,w_{n-1}}$ and a same-sized set of tasks
$T=\set{t_0,\ldots{}\, t_{n-1}}$. 
For each pair there is a cost $C(w_i,t_j)$, and the problem is 
to assign a task to each worker for minimal total cost.
The \probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman}
problem\index{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman} problem}\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem!Asymmetric@\probname{Asymmetric}}
asks for a minimal cost circuit in a directed weighted graph.\begin{exparts}
\item
By eye, solve this \probname{Assignment} problem instance.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{r}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Cost $C(w_i,t_j)$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_0$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_1$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_2$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_3$}}  \\
  \cline{2-5}
  \tabulartext{$t_0$} &$13$ &$4$  &$7$  &$6$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_1$} &$1$  &$11$ &$5$  &$4$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_2$} &$6$  &$7$  &$2$  &$8$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_3$} &$1$  &$3$  &$5$  &$9$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item 
Show that
$\probname{Assignment}\leq_p\probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman}$.
\hint~have the reduction function output a graph that is bipartite, 
i.e, $2$-colorable, where 
each worker is connected to each task by an edge in each direction
(this shows only the paired edges for $w_0$ because showing them 
all is less clear).
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity23.pdf}
\end{center}
Use the reduction function's input, the assignment cost table,
to make edge weights
that result in associating optimal assignments with optimal circuits.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item A little time playing with the table numbers
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{r}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$C(t_i,w_j)$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_0$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_1$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_2$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_3$}}  \\
  \cline{2-5}
  \tabulartext{$t_0$} &$13$ &$4$  &$7$  &$6$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_1$} &$1$  &$11$ &$5$  &$4$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_2$} &$6$  &$7$  &$2$  &$8$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_3$} &$1$  &$3$  &$5$  &$9$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
shows that the optimum solution is to pair
$t_0,w_1$, along with $t_1,w_3$, and $t_2,w_2$, and finally $t_3,w_0$.
We can check that with a routine.
\lstinputlisting[firstline=6]{scheme/complexity/assignment.rkt}
The total cost is~$11$.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (minimize-assignment cost-table)
11  
\end{lstlisting}
\item
The language for the \probname{Assignment} problem is this.
\begin{align*}
  \lang_0=
  \{\,\sequence{W,T,C,B} 
   &\suchthat 
   \text{$W$ and $T$ are same-sized sets and $\map{C}{W\times T}{\R}$,} \\
   &\qquad\text{and there is an assignment $\map{a}{W}{T}$ costing no more than~$B$}\,\}
\end{align*}
This is the language for the \probname{Asymmetric Traveling Salesman} problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=
  \set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},D} \suchthat 
       \text{$\mathcal{G}$ is a directed weighted graph having a circuit having total weight no more than~$D$}}
\end{equation*}

A reduction function
inputs a $\sequence{W,T,C,B}$  
and outputs a $\sequence{\mathcal{G},D}$. 
As in the hint, take the output graph
$\mathcal{G}$ to have vertices labeled with each $w_i$ and $t_j$,
but there are no inter-$w$ edges or inter-$t$ edges.
For each pair $w_i,t_j$ there is a directed edge in each direction.
Give the edge from $w_i$ to $t_j$ a weight of~$C(w_i,t_j)$.
Give the edge from $t_j$ to~$w_i$ a weight of~$0$.

If the input $\sequence{W,T,C,B}$ is an element of $\lang_0$
then the total cost of some assignment~$a$ does not exceed~$B$.
Let that assignment be
$w_{0}\mapsto t_{i_0}$,
$w_{1}\mapsto t_{i_1}$, \ldots\sentencespace
Then in $\mathcal{G}$ the circuit starting at the vertex labeled 
$w_0$ and traveling to the vertex~$t_{i_0}$, then
to $w_1$ and~$t_{i_1}$, etc.\, until returning back to $w_0$,
will be of total weight less than $D=B$.
That is, $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$ is an element of~$\lang_1$.

Clearly we can write reduction function associating this input and output
to run in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Must a set be polytime reducible to its complement?  
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that $\N$ is not polytime reducible to the empty set.
\item Show that if $A\leq_p B$ and $B$ is computably enumerable
then $A$ is computably enumerable.
Conclude that $\setcomp{K}\not\leq_p K$.  
\item Prove that $\lang\leq_p\setcomp{\lang}$
if and only if $\setcomp{\lang}\leq_p\lang$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This has nothing to do with the `polytime'.
If they were reducible then there would be a function~$f$ such that
$n\in\N$ if and only if $f(n)\in\emptyset$.
But the former is always true while the latter is always false.
\item Suppose that there is a computable function~$f$ such that
$x\in A$ if and only if $f(x)\in B$, and suppose that $B$ is computably
enumerable.
Then computably enumerate the set~$A$ by
dovetailing an enumeration of~$B$ with an enumeration
of the range of~$f$: $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots\,;
when an element appears on both lists,  $f(i)=b_j$, then
enumerate $i$ into~$A$. 

As to $\setcomp{K}\not\leq_p K$, the set $K$ is computably enumerable
but $\setcomp{K}$ is not.
\item
Assume that $\lang\leq_p\setcomp{\lang}\!$.
Then there is a polytime~$f$ so that 
$\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\in\setcomp{\lang}\!$.
That's equivalent to 
$\sigma\notin\lang$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\notin\setcomp{\lang}\!$,
which holds when
$\sigma\in\setcomp{\lang}$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\in\lang$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\recommended
\begin{exercise}\label{exer:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}
\zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed} leaves a couple of points undone.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Show that $\leq_p$ is reflexive and transitive.
\item
It says that if $\lang_1\in\P$ then
every language $\lang_0$ that is nontrivial, 
where $\lang_0\neq\emptyset$ and $\lang_0\neq\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, 
satisfies that $\lang_1 \leq_p \lang_0$.
What about trivial languages:~which languages reduce to the empty set?
to $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$?
\item Show that $\NP$ is downward closed, that  
if $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ and $\lang_0\in\NP$ then 
$\lang_1\in\NP$ also.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Reflexivity is easy;~we must show that $\lang\leq_p\lang$ for any 
$\lang\in\powerset(\kleenestar{\Sigma})$.
Take the reduction function to be the identity, $f(\sigma)=\sigma$.
Clearly it can be computed in polytime, and just as clearly
$\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang$.

For transitivity let $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_1$ via the function~$f$
and let $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_2$ via the function~$g$,
which are polytime computable.
Then for all $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ we have 
$\sigma\in\lang_0$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$,
and $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$ if and only if $\composed{g}{f}(\sigma)\in\lang_2$.
Because the two functions are polytime, so is their composition.
\item
Suppose that $\lang\leq_p\emptyset$.
Then there is a function~$f$ such that
$\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\emptyset$.
Since $f(\sigma)$ is never an element of the empty set, 
$\sigma$ is never an element of~$\lang$.
That is, any language that polytime reduces to the empty set is empty.

The argument that $\lang\leq_p\kleenestar{\B}$ implies that
$\lang=\kleenestar{\B}$ is just as easy.
\item 
  Assume that $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_1$, so that there is a polytime function 
  $\map{f}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}$ such that 
  $\sigma\in\lang_0$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$.
  We will use this reduction function to show that $\lang_1\in\NP$ implies that
  $\lang_0\in\NP$.

  Assume that $\lang_1\in\NP$. 
  Then there is a nondeterministic Turing machine~$\TM_1$
  that decides~$\lang_1$.
  For a machine~$\TM_0$ that decides~$\lang_0$, 
  start with an input candidate string~$\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
  Compute~$f(\tau)$.
  Run $\TM_1$ on input~$f(\tau)$.
  If it accepts then $\TM_0$ accepts, while if it rejects then $\TM_0$ rejects.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:TReducableDiffersFromMReducible}
Recall that $B$ is Turing reducible to~$A$, denoted $B\leq_T A$,
if there is a Turing machine using oracle~$A$ that computes~$B$, so that 
$\TMfcn_e^A=\charfcn{B}$ for some $e\in\N$.
And, $B$ is many-one reducible to~$A$, denoted $B\leq_mA$, 
if there is a total computable function~$f$ so that
$x\in B$ if and only if $f(x)\in A$.
We will show that 
\citetext{Turing reducibility is more general}{%
  Wording from \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/24590/50343}.
}, that
a many-one reduction is also a Turing reduction 
but some Turing reductions are not many-one reductions.
\begin{exparts*}
\item 
Show that  
if $B\leq_m A$ then $B\leq_T A$.
\item
Observe that $A\leq_T \setcomp{A}$ for all sets and so $K\leq_T \setcomp{K}\!$.
Show that if $B\leq_m A$ and $B$ is computably enumerable then 
so is $A$, and conclude that $K$ is not many-one reducible to its complement.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item 
As described in the section, $B\leq_m A$ if there is a total computable 
function~$f$ such that we make one oracle call, asking whether 
$f(x)\in A$, and then returning the result of that call.
\item 
Suppose that $B\leq_m A$ via the total computable function~$f$,
and suppose also that $B$ is computably enumerable.
As we enumerate $B=\set{b_0,b_1 \ldots}$, we can also enumerate
$A=\set{f(b_0),f(b_1)\ldots}$. 
Therefore $A$ is computably enumerable.

Now for the conclusion. 
If a set and its complement are both
computably enumerable then both are computable.
But of course $K$ is not computable, so the prior paragraph means that
$K\not\leq_m\setcomp{K}\!$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


% Good exercise but the language is a set of integers, so there is some
% awkwardness in matching with sets of strings.
% \begin{exercise}

% We will show that 
% $\probname{Fin}\leq_p\probname{Reg}$,\index{Fin problem@\probname{Fin} problem}\index{Reg problem@\probname{Reg} problem} 
% where they are 
% the decision problems for the language
% $
%   F=\set{i\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_i$ is the characteristic function of a finite set}}
% $
% and the language 
% $
%   R=\set{x\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x$ is the characteristic function of a regular set}}

% The flowchart makes clear that this behavior can be programmed on an
% everyday computer.
% Thus Church's Thesis says there is a Turing machine 
% with this behavior.

% \begin{center}
%   \includegraphics{complexity/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts003.pdf}
% \end{center}
% \item
% The input to the Turing machine $\TM_e$, 
% what is on the tape when the machine starts,
% is a string representing $\sigma,x$.
% So it decides a language.

% The reduction function~$f$ must have domain~$\N$ and codomain~$\N$.
% It must have the property that $i\in F$ if and only if
% $x\in R$.
% And it must run in polynomial time.

% Take~$f$ to be $i\mapsto s(e,i)$.
% Suppose that $i\in F$.
% Then the machine 
% $\TM_i$ is a language decider and so it halts
% on all inputs~$\tau$.
% Because the language of $\TM_i$ is finite, the language of $\TM_{s(e,i)}$
% is finite also.
% Any finite language is regular, so the language of $\TM_{s(e,i)}$ is regular.

% Now suppose that $i\notin F$.
% If the machine $\TM_{s(e,x)}$ has even one input~$\sigma$ where
% $\TM_x$ fails to halt on some~$\tau$ then this machine is
% not a language decider, and so $s(e,i)\notin R$.
% If, on the other hand, for all input~$\sigma$, 
% $\TM_x$ halts on all relevant $\tau$'s
% then because $i\notin F$, 
% the language decided by $\TM_{s(e,i)}$ is an infinite subset 
% of $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$, and so is not 
% regular.
% \end{exparts}
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there a connection between subset and polytime reducibility?
Find languages $\lang_0,\lang_1\in\powerset(\kleenestar{\B})$ for each:
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang_0\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$,
\item $\lang_0\not\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$,
\item $\lang_0\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\not\leq_p \lang_1$,
\item $\lang_0\not\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\not\leq_p \lang_1$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
We answer each with specific examples, but there are many other correct
answers.
For instance, 
for the first we can use any two members of~$\P$ where one is a proper subset
of the other. 
\begin{exparts}
\item
Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of strings whose length is a
multiple of four, and $\lang_1$ to be the language of strings whose length
is even.
Clearly $\lang_0\subset \lang_1$, and
both languages are in~$\P$ so $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$.
\item 
Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of even-length strings, 
and $\lang_1$ to be the language of odd-length strings.
Clearly both languages are in~$\P$, so $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$.
\item 
Recall that the only set that polytime reduces to~$\kleenestar{\B}$
is $\kleenestar{\B}$ itself.
Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of even-length strings and
$\lang_1=\kleenestar{\B}\!$.
\item Recall that the only set that polytime reduces to~$\emptyset$
is $\emptyset$ itself.
Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of even-length
strings and take $\lang_1=\emptyset$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise} % https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/92028
% If a problem~$\lang$ is in $\NP$ and $\lang\leq_p\hat{\lang}$
% then what can we say about the problem~$\hat{\lang}$?
% \begin{answer}
% Nothing.
% For instance, it could be that $\lang$ is in~$\P$ and so is $\hat{\lang}$.
% It could also be that $\hat{\lang}$ is in some higher-up class.
% There is not enough information to conclude anything. 
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{reductions between problems|)}




% ====================================================
% The \texorpdfstring avoids a hyperref error from math in the bookmarks
\section{\texorpdfstring{$\compclass{NP}$}{NP} completeness}\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete|(}\label{sec:NPCompleteness}
% \margingraphic{0.16\textwidth}{complexity/pix/cook.jpg}{\label{fig:StephenCook}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Cook}{Stephen Cook b~1939}}\index{Cook, S!picture} % http://web.cs.toronto.edu/Assets/Computer+Science+Digital+Assets/cooknsercmed.jpg http://web.cs.toronto.edu/news/current/herzbergcook.htm
Because $\P\subseteq\NP$, the class $\NP$ contains lots of easy problems,
ones with a fast algorithm.
% For instance, one member of $\NP$ is
% the problem of determining whether a number is odd.
But the interest in the class is that it also contains lots of
problems that seem to be hard, in that for decades experts have been 
unable to find fast algorithms for them.
Are they are indeed inherently hard?

\begin{wrapfigure}{r}[3em]{0.36\textwidth}  % "r" for right side so they look into page
  \centering
  \makebox{\includegraphics[height=2.5cm]{complexity/pix/cook.jpg}% 
  \quad %\vspace*{1ex}
  \includegraphics[height=2.5cm]{complexity/pix/levin.png}}% 
  \caption{\protect\citetext{\label{fig:cook}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Cook}{Stephen Cook\ b~1939} and \label{fig:levin}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Levin}{Leonid Levin b~1948}}{Photo credits University of Toronto, Boston University}}\index{Cook, S!picture}\index{Levin, L!picture}
\end{wrapfigure}
This question was raised by S~Cook in 1971.
He noted that the idea of polynomial time reducibility gives us a way to make 
precise that
a solution for one problem\Dash specifically, an efficient solution\Dash gives 
a solution for other problems.
He then showed 
that among the problems in $\NP$,
there are ones that are maximally hard.
This was also shown by L~Levin but he was behind the Iron Curtain and
  knowledge of his work did not spread to 
  the rest of the world for some time.

We call these $\NP$ problems `maximally hard' 
because  if we could solve one of these then 
we could solve all problems in~$\NP$.
So each of these is 
\citetext{at least as hard}{%
  They are ``at least as hard'' in the sense 
  that such problems can answer questions about
  any other problem in that class.
  However, note that it might be that one $\NP$~complete problem 
  runs in nondeterministic
  time that is~$\bigOh(n)$ while another runs in $\bigOh(n^{1\,000\,000})$ time.}.
as any other problem in $\NP$.

%<*th:CookLevin>
\begin{theorem}[Cook-Levin Theorem]\index{Cook-Levin theorem}\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}
The \probname{Satisfiability} problem, \SAT, is in $\NP$ and
has the property that any problem in $\NP$ reduces to it:
$\prob\leq_p\SAT$ for any $\prob\in\NP$.
\end{theorem}
%</th:CookLevin>

%<*pf:CookLevini>
We have already observed that $\SAT\!\in\NP$ because,
given a Boolean expression,
we can use as a witness~$\omega$ an assignment of truth values
that satisfies the expression.
%</pf:CookLevini>

% \margingraphic[l]{0.17\textwidth}{}{\label{fig:L Levin}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Levin}{Leonid Levin b~1948}} % https://www.bu.edu/today/2014/american-academy-of-arts-sciences-taps-cas-prof-for-membership/
%<*pf:CookLevinii>
% The rest of the proof is in an Extra section.
For the proof's other half,
given $\prob\in\NP$, we must show that
$\prob\leq_p\SAT\!$.
So we must produce a function~$f_{\prob}$ that translates
membership questions for $\prob$ into Boolean
expressions, such that the membership answer is `yes' if and
only if the expression is satisfiable.

In outline:~we know that~$\prob$ is decided
by a nondeterministic machine~$\TM$ in time given by
a polynomial~$q$.
From $\sequence{\TM,\sigma,q}$ the proof constructs
a Boolean expression that yields \true{} if and only if
$\TM$ accepts~$\sigma$.
The Boolean expression describes the constraints on a Turing machine
operates, such as that the only tape symbol that can be changed in
the current step is the symbol under the machine's head.
%</pf:CookLevinii>

%<*def:NPComplete>
\begin{definition}
A problem~$\prob$ is \citetext{\definend{$\NP$~complete}}{%
  The name is from a survey created by Knuth. 
  See
  \url{blog.computationalcomplexity.org/2010/11/by-any-other-name-would-be-just-as-hard.html}.}\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete}\index{complete!NP@$\NP$}
if it is a member of~$\NP$ and every problem in $\NP$ reduces to it, 
that is,
$\hat{\lang}\in \NP$ implies that $\hat{\lang}\leq_p\lang$.\footnotemark %
\end{definition}\footnotetext{%
  The relationship that the
  $\NP$~complete problems have with the class~$\NP$
  is like the relationship that the problems 
  Turing-equivalent to the \HP{} set~$K$
  have with the computably enumerable sets.
  In both cases elements of the subset sit at the top of the entire
  collection:~an $\NP$~complete set is $\geq_p$ every problem in $\NP$,
  and a set Turing-equivalent to~$K$ is $\geq_T$ every 
  computably enumerable set.}
%</def:NPComplete>

% This sketch illustrates.

\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeanWithNPComplete]{The blob contains all problems,
  ordered by $\leq_p$.
  In the bottom are $\P$ and~$\NP$.
  At the top of  $\NP-\P$ the highlighted area
  contains the $\NP$ complete 
  problems.} \label{fig:LangDecisionProblemsWithNPComplete}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2009.pdf}}
\end{fig}

\noindent 
The Cook-Levin Theorem says that there is at least one $\NP$~complete problem, 
namely $\SAT\!$.
We shall see that
there are many.

%<*lem:NPCompleteIsTransitive>
\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:InteractionNPCompleteAndReduction}
Suppose that $\prob_0,\prob_1\in\NP$.
If $\prob_0$ is $\NP$~complete and $\prob_0\leq_p\prob_1$
then $\prob_1$ is also $\NP$~complete.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:NPCompleteIsTransitive>

\begin{proof}
\zcref{exer:ProveInteractionNPCompleteAndReduction}.
\end{proof}

The prior section shows that 
$\SAT\leq_p\probname{Clique}$\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem}
so by the lemma that problem is also $\NP$ complete.

%<*discussion:KarpsProblems>
\margingraphic{0.20\textwidth}{complexity/pix/karp.jpg}{\label{fig:RichardKarp}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_M._Karp}{Richard M Karp b~1935}}\index{Karp, R!picture} % https://simons.berkeley.edu/people/richard-karp  https://simons.berkeley.edu/sites/default/files/styles/profile_main/public/rk1.jpg?itok=1kn5XodZ
Soon after Cook raised the question of $\NP$~completeness, 
R~Karp brought it to widespread attention.
Karp noted that there are clusters of 
problems:~there is a collection
of problems solvable in time $\bigOh(\lg(n))$, problems of
time $\bigOh(n)$, 
those of time~$\bigOh(n\lg n)$,
etc.

There is also a cluster of problems that
seem much tougher.
He gave a list of twenty~one of these,
drawn from Computer Science, Mathematics, and the natural sciences,
where lots of smart people had for years
been unable find efficient algorithms.
He showed that all of these problems are $\NP$~complete, so
that if we could efficiently solve any then we could
efficiently solve them all. 
% Based on 
% Karp's Turing award statement 
% http://amturing.acm.org/award_winners/karp_3256708.cfm
%</discussion:KarpsProblems>
% These are hard problems, ones that researchers have struggled to find a 
% fast algorithm to solve. 
Not every difficult problem is $\NP$~complete but many
thousands of problems have been shown to be so and thus 
whatever it is that makes these problems hard, all of them share it.
 
Typically we prove that a problem~$\lang$ is $\NP$~complete in two halves.
First we show that it is in $\NP$
by exhibiting a witness~$\omega$ 
that a deterministic verifier can check in polytime.
Second, we show that every problem in $\NP$ reduces to it,
usually by finding that
an $\NP$ complete problem that reduces to it. 
\citetext{The list below gives the $\NP$~complete problems most often used}{%
  These are from the classic standard reference \autocite{GareyJohnson}.}.
For instance, we might show
that $\ThreeSAT\leq_p\prob$.

%<*th:GJNPCompleteProblems>
\begin{theorem}[Basic $\compclass{NP}$ Complete Problems]\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete!basic problems}\label{th:BasicNPCompleteProbList}
Each of these problems is $\compclass{NP}$~complete.
\begin{thmdescription}
\item[\probname{3-Satisfiability}, $\ThreeSAT$]\index{3-Satisfiability problem@\probname{3-Satisfiability} problem}
Given a propositional logic formula in conjunctive normal form
in which each clause has at most $3$ variables,
decide if it is satisfiable.

\item[\probname{Three Dimensional Matching}]\index{Three Dimensional Matching problem@\probname{Three Dimensional Matching} problem}
Given as input a set $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$, where the sets $X,Y,Z$
all have the same number of elements, $n$,
decide if there is a matching,
a set $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ containing~$n$ elements such that no
two of the triples in $\hat{M}$ agree on any of their coordinates.

\item[\probname{Vertex cover}]\index{Vertex Cover problem@\probname{Vertex cover} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has
a size~$B$ set of vertices~$C$
such that for any edge, at least one of its ends
is a member of~$C$.

\item[\probname{Clique}]\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has
a set of $B$-many vertices where any two are connected.

\item[\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}]\index{Hamiltonian Circuit problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}
Given a graph, decide if it contains
a cyclic path that includes each vertex.

\item[\probname{Partition}]\index{Partition problem@\probname{Partition} problem}
Given a finite multiset~$S$ of natural numbers,
decide if there is a division of the set into the two parts
$\hat{S}$ and $S-\hat{S}$ so the total of their elements
is the same, $\sum_{s\in\hat{S}}s=\sum_{s\notin\hat{S}}s$.
\end{thmdescription}
\end{theorem}
%</th:GJNPCompleteProblems>

\begin{example}
%<*ex:TSIsNPComplete>
We will show that
the \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem}
is $\NP$ complete.
Recall that we have recast it 
as the decision problem for the language of pairs~$\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$,
where $B$ is a parameter bound, and that
this problem is a member of~$\NP$.
We will do the second half of showing that it is $\NP$~complete by proving that
the \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem\index{Hamiltonian Circuit problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}
reduces to it,
$\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}\leq_p\probname{Traveling Salesman}$.

We need a reduction function~$f\!$.
It must
input an instance of \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}, a graph
$\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}}$
whose edges are unweighted.
Define $f$ to return
the instance of \probname{Traveling Salesman} that uses
$\mathcal{N}$ as cities, that takes the distances between cities
to be $d(v_i,v_j)=1$ if $v_iv_j\in\mathcal{E}$ and
$d(v_i,v_j)=2$ if $v_iv_j\notin\mathcal{E}$,
and such that the bound is the number of vertices, $B=\sizeof{\mathcal{N}}$.

This bound means that there will be a \probname{Traveling Salesman} solution
if and only if there is a \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} solution; namely,
the salesman uses the edges that appear in 
the Hamiltonian circuit.
All that remains is to 
note that the reduction function can run in polytime 
(it examines all pairs of vertices, which takes time that
is quadratic in the number of vertices).
%</ex:TSIsNPComplete>
\end{example}

For a given problem~$\prob$, a common way
to show that every $\NP$~problem reduces to it is to show that 
every $\NP$ problem reduces to a special case of~$\prob$.

%<*ex:KnapsackIsNPComplete>
\begin{example}
The
\probname{Knapsack}\index{Knapsack problem@\probname{Knapsack} problem} 
problem starts with a 
multiset of objects~$S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$, each
with a natural number weight~$w(s_i)$ and value~$v(s_i)$,
along with a weight
bound $B$ and value target~$T\!$.
The goal is to find a knapsack
$K\subseteq S$ whose elements have total weight no larger than the
bound and total value of at least the target.

First we check that this problem is in $\NP$.
As the witness we can use the $k$-bit string~$\omega$ such that $\omega[i]=1$ 
if $s_i$ is in the knapsack~$K$ and $\omega[i]=0$ if it is not.
A deterministic machine can verify this witness in polynomial
time since it only has to total the weights and values
of the elements of~$K$.

To finish we must show that every $\NP$ problem
reduces to \probname{Knapsack}.
It is sufficient to show that every $\NP$ problem reduces to
a special case.
Consider the \probname{Knapsack} instance 
where $w(s_i)=v(s_i)$ for all~$s_i\in S$, and where
$B=T=0.5\cdot\sum_{0\leq i<k}w(i)$.
This shows that any instance of the 
\probname{Partition}\index{Partition problem@\probname{Partition} problem} 
problem, which is in the above basic list, can be expressed as a
\probname{Knapsack} instance, so 
$\probname{Partition}\leq_p\probname{Knapsack}$.
% Consequently, \probname{Knapsack} is $\NP$~hard.
\end{example}
%</ex:KnapsackIsNPComplete>

Another common strategy in these proofs is to build the
reduction function so that
the behavior of the input instance's fundamental
units is simulated by 
subpart of the output instance.
Such a construct is 
\citetext{a \definend{gadget}}{%
  See \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/1249/50343} from the 
  Computer Science Stack Exchange user Jeff.}.\index{gadget!in complexity arguments}
% The next example illustrates.
% ; it shows that
% $\ThreeSAT\leq_p\probname{3-Coloring}$
% by having the clauses in the input Boolean expression
% reflected by subgraphs in the output.

\begin{example}
Recall that a graph can be $3$-colored if we can partition its vertices
into three categories, the colors, 
in such a way that no two same-colored vertices are connected by an edge.
We will show that the 
\definend{\probname{3-Coloring}} problem,\index{3-Coloring problem@\probname{3-Coloring} problem}
the decision problem for $\lang=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat \text{the graph $\mathcal{G}$ has a $3$-coloring}}$,
is $\NP$~complete.

The easy half is $\lang\in\NP$.
As a witness we use a $3$-coloring
$\omega=\sequence{C_0,C_1,C_2}$
where the $C_i$ partition $\mathcal{G}$'s vertices.
Clearly we can produce a polytime verifier that inputs 
a graph~$\mathcal{G}$ along with an $\omega$,
and tries to verify that the members of
$\omega$ are disjoint and together make up all of the vertices, and 
that never do two same-color vertices lie on the same edge.
If the graph is $3$-colorable then there is an $\omega$ 
that it can verify, while if the graph is not then there is no such~$\omega$.

The other half of showing $\NP$ completeness is show that 
every $\NP$ problem reduces to~$\lang$.
We will sketch the proof
that $\ThreeSAT\leq_p\probname{3-Coloring}$.
The reduction function 
inputs a propositional logic expression 
in\index{Conjunctive Normal form}\index{CNF} 
Conjunctive Normal form where the clauses never have more than three literals. 
It outputs a graph that is
$3$-colorable if and only if the input expression is satisfiable.

The gadget is below.\index{gadget!example of}
It simulates one clause in the input propositional logic expression,
$a\vee b\vee c$ 
(these variables represent literals 
so each could be a Boolean variable $P_i$ or its negation $\neg P_i$).
At the top are nodes labeled $T\!$, $F\!$, and~$G$.
If the graph is to be $3$-colored then 
no two nodes in this triangle can have the same color.
At the gadget's bottom are nodes labeled $a$, $b$, and~$c$.
They are connected to~$G$ and consequently each of these three
must have either the color of node~$T$ or the color of node~$F\!$.
We will verify that this gadget is $3$-colorable if and only if 
nodes $a$, $b$, and~$c$ are not all the color of~$F\!$ 
(just as the clause $a\vee b\vee c$ is True if and only if
not all of its literals are False).
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity41.pdf}}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}

For `only if', let $a$, $b$, and $c$ be the color of~$F\!$. 
Then one of
$n_3$ and~$n_4$ is the color of $T$ while the other is the color of~$G$, 
and hence $n_0$ is the color of~$F$.
Node $n_2$ is the color of $T$ because it is connected to both $G$ and~$F$.
Those two sentences imply that $n_1$ is the color of $G$,
and this in turn gives that $n_5$ is the color of~$F$.
But we assumed that $c$ is the color of~$F$ so 
that violates $3$-colorability.

For `if', we need only exhibit that a $3$-coloring exists
for each remaining case.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{*{3}{c}|*{6}{c}} % verified 2025-Feb-21
  $a$ &$b$ &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$c$}  
    &$n_0$ &$n_1$ &$n_2$ &$n_3$ &$n_4$ &$n_5$  \\
  \hline
  $F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$G$ &$T$ &$T$ &$G$ &$F$ \\
  $F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$G$ &$F$ &$G$  \\
  $F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$G$ &$T$ &$T$ &$G$ &$F$  \\[1ex]
  $T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$G$ &$G$  \\
  $T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$G$ &$T$ &$G$ &$T$ &$F$  \\
  $T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$G$ &$G$  \\
  $T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$G$ &$G$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

We finish by illustrating what the reduction function outputs
when the input is an expression with multiple clauses.
Here is the graph for
$E=(P\vee Q\vee R)\wedge(\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee R)$.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity37.pdf}}
  \tag{$**$}
\end{equation*}
On the left is the gadget for the first clause and on the right is the 
gadget for the second.
At the bottom, instead of ($*$)'s single node~$a$, here there are two
nodes, $P$ and~$\neg P$.
Similarly there are $Q$ and~$\neg Q$ as well as $R$ and~$\neg R$.
All six share an edge with~$G$
so in a $3$-colored graph all six of these
nodes must have the color of~$T$ or the color of~$F$.
What's more, because $P$ is connected to~$\neg P$, in a $3$-colored
graph one of these has the color of~$T$ and the other has the color of~$F$.
The same holds for the other bottom pairs. 

The first clause is $P\vee Q\vee R$ so in the left gadget 
the reduction function outputs the graph with
the highlighted edges lead to the nodes marked $P$, $Q$, and~$R$.
The second clause is $\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee R$ so in the right
gadget the edges lead to nodes named accordingly.
As the analysis for ($*$) shows, 
this gives a $3$-colorable graph if and only if
the expression~$E$ is satisfiable.
\end{example}

One of Karp's points is the practical importance of
$\NP$~completeness.
Many problems from applications fall into this class.
The next example illustrates.

\begin{example} 
Scheduling is a rich source of difficult combinatorial problems.
One is the problem of scheduling college classes into
time slots. 
% such as Tuesday and Thursday from $8$ until $9$:$20$.
Usually colleges start the process by assigning classes to
slots and
then students try to find classes that they need
and that are offered at non-conflicting times.
However, imagine if instead the process began with each student submitting 
their list of desired classes and then the college tries to 
find a non-conflicting schedule of slots and rooms
to accommodate the requests.

For instance, consider a college with $t=12$ available time slots and that
must work $n=420$ classes into $r=60$ classrooms.\footnote{%
  We will ignore many issues, such as that lab classes must be in 
  laboratory-equipped rooms.} 
Since $12\cdot 60=720$ and we need only accommodate
$420$ classes, this might seem easy. 
But this college has $1724$ students and when each one submits their 
class requests, $S_i=\set{c_{i_0}, c_{i_1},\ldots c_{i_n}}$, each pair of classes 
$c_{i_p},c_{i_q}$ puts the restriction on the college-wide schedule that those two
cannot meet at the same time.
Can the college find a schedule despite all of these constraints?

The \definend{\probname{Class Scheduling}}\index{Class Scheduling problem@\probname{Class Scheduling} problem}
problem inputs the number of time slots and rooms,
along with the class requests 
from each student,
and decides if there is a way to allocate classes
so that there is no conflict.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{t,r,\set{S_0,S_1,\ldots }}\suchthat \text{a nonconflicting schedule exists}}
\end{equation*}
We will show that this problem is $\NP$~complete.

It is a member of $\NP$ because we can take as the witness~$\omega$ 
a schedule, a nonconflicting assignment of classes to rooms and times.
It is straightforward to write a verifier that inputs
$\sigma=\sequence{t,r,\set{S_0,S_1,\ldots }}$ and $\omega$,
and then checks in polytime 
that $\omega$ meets the restrictions.

What remains is show that every $\NP$ problem reduces to this one.
This problem is a good fit with the 
\probname{Graph Colorability}\index{Graph Colorability problem@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem} 
problem where the
nodes of the graph are the classes, where two nodes are 
connected when some student has requested them both,
and where nodes are colored the same if they are in the same time slot.
% (There is a further restriction about the number of available rooms
% that we will handle along the way.)
The prior example proves that \probname{Graph Colorability} 
is $\NP$~complete for $k=3$ colors and an extension of that argument proves 
the same for any larger~$k$.\footnote{%
  When $k=1$ or $k=2$, the \protect\probname{Graph $k$-Colorability} problem
  is solvable in polytime but 
  no doubt the college has more than one or two rooms.}
So we will show 
% that \probname{Class Scheduling} is $\NP$~hard by showing 
that $\probname{Graph Colorability}\leq_p\probname{Class Scheduling}$.

Assume that we are given an instance of 
\probname{Graph Colorability},
a graph $\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}$ and a
natural number~$k$.
Here is the instance of the \probname{Class Scheduling}
problem that the reduction function associates with~$\mathcal{G}$:~the 
number of classes is $\sizeof{\mathcal{V}}$, as is the number of rooms,
and the number of time slots is~$k$.
For each student there are two classes that they take, and a graph edge 
connects those two.
Clearly from the input instance we can in polytime produce the
output instance.
Also clearly, the \probname{Graph Colorability} instance has a solution
if and only if the associated \probname{Class Scheduling} instance has a 
nonconflicting schedule.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
A problem~$\lang$ is 
\definend{$\NP$~hard}\index{NP hard@$\NP$ hard}\index{hard!NP@$\NP$}
if every problem in $\NP$ reduces to it, 
that is, if
$\hat{\lang}\in \NP$ implies that $\hat{\lang}\leq_p\lang$.
\end{definition}

Thus, a problem is $\NP$~complete 
if, in addition to being a member of~$\NP$, it is also 
$\NP$~hard.\footnote{%
  In general, for a complexity class~$\compclass{C}$, a problem~$\prob$ is 
  \protect\definend{$\compclass{C}$~hard}\protect\index{hard!for a class} 
   when all problems in that class reduce to it:~if
  $\hat{\prob}\in\compclass{C}$ then $\hat{\prob}\leq_p\prob$.
  A problem is 
  \protect\definend{$\compclass{C}$~complete}\protect\index{complete!for a class}
  if is a member of that class and is hard for that
  class.}

\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeanWithNPComplete]{The blob contains all problems,
  ordered by $\leq_p$.
  % In the bottom is~$\NP$.
  % The top has the $\NP$-hard problems.
  The highlighted intersection
  is the set of $\NP$ complete problems.} \label{fig:LangDecisionProblemsWithNPHard}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2007.pdf}}
\end{fig}

As the figure illustrates, the collection of $\NP$~hard problems extends 
upward.

\begin{lemma}
The equivalent of the \HP, the decision problem for
$\hat{K}=\set{\hat{n}\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat 
   \text{$\hat{n}$ represents $n$ in binary and $\TM_n$ halts on~$n$}}$,
is $\NP$~hard but not $\NP$.  
(Here, $\TM_n$ is a deterministic Turing machine.)
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
It is not~$\NP$ because it is not decidable.
For $\NP$~hard fix $\lang\in\NP$.  
We will produce a reduction 
function~$f$ that inputs a string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ and
outputs a representation~$\hat{n}$ of an integer,
such that $\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $\hat{n}\in\hat{K}$.

The language $\lang$
is decided by a nondeterministic Turing machine $\TM_{e_0}$
in polytime.
This machine halts on every input, either accepting or rejecting.
The reduction function outputs the representation~$\hat{n}$ of the index~$n$
of the following computation.
It inputs a string~$\sigma$ 
and uses $e_0$ to deterministically simulate running $\TM_{e_0}$ on that input.
If the simulation accepts~$\sigma$ then this computation halts, 
while if the simulation rejects then this computation does not halt.

This reduction function runs in polytime.\footnotemark %
(A computation of $\TMfcn_n(n)$ may well take more than
polytime, because it simulates the nondeterministic machine  $\TM_{e_0}$,
or might not halt at all.
But going from the input~$\sigma$ to the output~$\hat{n}$ index of that 
computation takes at most polytime.)
Clearly $\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $\hat{n}\in\hat{K}$.
\end{proof}\footnotetext{%
  As discussed on \zcpageref{disc:BinaryInputUnaryOutputRunsExp},
  the numbers in $\hat{K}$ are represented in binary because
  If the input was in binary and the output was in unary then
  $f$ could have exponential runtime.}

Before leaving this discussion, we address a
natural question:~there are lots of problems that are $\NP$~complete but
which problems are not?

Trivially, the definition gives that 
the empty language and the language of all strings are 
not $\NP$~complete, since for each the only set that reduces to it is itself.
Also trivially, 
a problem cannot be $\NP$~complete if it is not in $\NP$.
For instance, a problem can be so
hard that we cannot even check its solution in polytime
(we discussed this earlier with the demon's proposed chess strategy).

The nontrivial aspects of the
answer are tied to whether $\P=\NP$ or $\P\neq\NP$.
We will address this issue in the next subsection
but just to not have brushed past it,
first note that if $\P=\NP$ then every nontrivial problem is $\NP$~hard by
\zcref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}.
So assume that $\P\neq\NP$.
%   (from SE user 
%    David Eppstein, \url{https://mathoverflow.net/a/9226}).}.
Any algorithms text describes many problems with polytime algorithms and
$\P\neq\NP$ implies that these are not 
$\NP$~complete.
Thus, if $\P\neq\NP$ then most problems from everyday programming 
are not $\NP$~complete.

In addition, if $\protect\P\neq\protect\NP$ 
then there is a problem in 
$\protect\NP-\protect\P$ that is not $\protect\NP$~complete;
this result is called Ladner's theorem.\index{Ladner's theorem}
Many interesting questions concern these
\definend{$\NP$~intermediate} problems.\index{NP intermediate problems@$\NP$ intermediate problems} 
The most important one is that
proving that a particular problem of interest is in this category
is astonishingly difficult\Dash 
at this moment the field just does not know how to do it.
Two 
problems of great interest that many experts believe are
$\NP$~intermediate, 
but no one can currently prove that, are
\probname{Prime Factorization}\footnote{%
  In 1994, P~Shor discovered an algorithm for a quantum computer that
  solves the \protect\probname{Prime Factorization} problem in polynomial time.
  This will have significant implications if we manage 
  to build quantum computers.
  Nonetheless, on classical computers most experts believe that this problem is
  is in $\NP-\P$ but not complete.}\index{Prime Factorization problem@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}
and
\probname{Graph Isomorphism}\index{Graph Isomorphism problem@\probname{Graph Isomorphism} problem} (to decide whether two graphs are isomorphic).
\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete|)}



% .................................
% The \texorpdfstring avoids a hyperref error from math in the bookmarks
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\mbox{$\P=\NP$?}}{P=NP?}}\index{P versus NP@$\P$ versus $\NP$|(}
We have seen that $\P\subseteq\NP$, and
thus every polytime problem is in $\NP$. 
But what about the other direction\Dash could it be that
$\P=\NP$ and every $\NP$ problem is,
in a sense, easy?

One of our mental modela of nondeterministic machines 
is that they are unboundedly parallel. 
So the $\P$~versus~$\NP$ question asks:~does adding parallelism add speed?
Can unbounded parallelism bring problems
from super-polynomial to polynomial?

In short, no one knows.
We do not know which of these two pictures is right.
\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeansPNP]{Which is it: $\P=\NP$
  or $\P\subsetneq\NP$?}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2005.pdf}}
  \hspace{5em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2004.pdf}}
\end{fig}

There are a number of ways
to potentially settle the question.
For example, by \zcref{lem:InteractionNPCompleteAndReduction} if there is even
one $\NP$ complete problem that we can prove is a member of~$\P$ then
we will know that $\P=\NP$.
Conversely, 
if we can show that there is an $\NP$ problem that is not a member
of~$\P$ then $\P\neq\NP$.
However, despite nearly a half century of effort by many brilliant
people, no one has accomplished either one.

To explain all the effort on the question, 
we will argue for its importance.
As formulated in Karp's original paper, the question of whether
$\P$ equals $\NP$ might seem of only technical interest.
\begin{quotation}
  \citetext{A large class}{%
  See \autocite{Karp}.}
  of computational problems involve the
  determination of properties of graphs, digraphs, integers,
  arrays of integers, finite families of finite sets, Boolean formulas and
  elements of other countable domains.
  Through simple encodings \ldots{} these problems can be converted into
  language recognition problems, and we can inquire into their
  computational complexity.
  It is reasonable to consider such a problem satisfactorily solved when
  an algorithm for its solution is found which terminates within a
  number of steps bounded by a polynomial in the length of the input.
  We show that a large number of classic unsolved problems of covering,
  matching, packing, routing, assignment and sequencing are equivalent,
  in the sense that either each of them possesses a
  polynomial-bounded algorithm or none of them does.
\end{quotation}
These careful words mask the excitement, which is in the phrase ``classic
unsolved problems.'' 
%<*discussion:PvsNPi>
Karp described that researchers who had been searching 
for years for an efficient solution to  
\probname{Vertex Cover} and those 
studying \probname{Clique}
are actually working on the same problem,
in that the two are inter-translatable, are polytime equivalent.
By now the list of $\NP$ complete problems includes
determining the best layout of transistors on a chip,
developing accurate financial-forecasting models,
% analyzing protein-folding behavior in a cell,
or finding the most energy-efficient airplane wing.
So the question of whether $\P$ equals $\NP$ is extremely practical, and
extremely important.\footnote{%
  One indication of its importance is its inclusion on the
  Clay Mathematics Institute's list
  of problems for which there is a one million dollar prize;
  see \protect\url{http://www.claymath.org/millennium-problems}.
  Part of the introduction there says, 
  ``[O]ne of the outstanding problems in computer science is determining 
  whether questions exist whose answer can be quickly checked, 
  but which require an impossibly long time to solve by any direct procedure. 
  Problems \ldots{} certainly seem to be of this kind, but 
  so far no one has managed to prove that any of them really are 
  so hard as they appear.''
  }
%</discussion:PvsNPi>

\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \relax
}{%
\margingraphic{0.46\textwidth}{complexity/pix/xkcdnpcomplete.png}{\label{fig:xkcdBigO}\href{https://xkcd.com/287/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
}
%<*discussion:PvsNPInPractice>
% Another importance of the question is that
Researchers often take 
proving that a problem is $\NP$~complete to be 
\citetext{an ending point}{%
  That is, as P~Pudl\`{a}k observes, we treat $\P\neq\NP$
  as an informal axiom. \autocite{Pudlak}}; 
they may feel that continuing to
look for an algorithm is a waste since many of the
world's best minds 
have failed to find one.
They may turn to finding
approximations (see \zcref{extra:Tractability})
or to probabilistic methods.
%</discussion:PvsNPInPractice>

We close this subsection by arguing 
that among many the important questions that we face,
$\P$ versus $\NP$ is especially significant, 
even beyond its practical importance,
because of how it fits with the larger ideas of the Theory of Computation. 

At the start of this book we studied problems that are unsolvable.
That is black and white\Dash either a problem is mechanically solvable
or it is not.
In this chapter we've seen many problems that 
are solvable in principle but where computing a solution seems to be infeasible.
The set $\P$ consists of the problems that we
can feasibly solve.
But if $\P\neq\NP$ then 
the problems in $\NP-\P$, including the 
$\NP$ complete ones, are ones for which 
we can verify a correct answer but we cannot reliably find it.
We can view these problems as  a transition between 
the possible and the impossible.\footnote{
  This brings to mind the famous lines by the poet R~Browning,
  ``A man's reach should exceed his grasp,
  Or what's a heaven for?''}

% \margingraphic{0.15\textwidth}{complexity/pix/browning.jpg}{\label{fig:browning}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Browning}{Robert Browning, 1812--1889}}
The sense that the $\P$ versus $\NP$ question fits into this larger 
intellectual setting
returns us to the book's opening.
%<*discussion:Entscheidungsproblemi>
Recall the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}} 
that was a motivation behind the definition of a Turing machine.
It asks for
an algorithm that inputs a mathematical statement
and outputs whether it is true.
Perhaps it is a \citetext{caricature}{%
  Paul Erdős joked that a mathematician is a machine for turning
  coffee into theorems.},
but imagine that the job of mathematicians is to prove theorems.
Then the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} asks whether we can replace 
mathematicians with mechanisms.

We have come to understand, through
the work of G\"odel and others,
that there is a difference between a statement's 
being true and its being provable.
Church and Turing expanded on this insight to show that the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem} is unsolvable.
Consequently, we modify this problem to asking for an algorithm
that inputs statements and decides whether they are provable.

In principle this is simple.
A  proof is a sequence of statements,
$\sigma_0$, $\sigma_1$, \ldots{} $\sigma_k$,
where the final statement is the conclusion
and where each statement either is an axiom or
else follows from the statements before it
by an application
of a rule of deduction. 
% (a typical rule allows the simultaneous replacement of all
% `$x$' with a `$y+4$').
A computer could brute-force the question of
whether a given statement is provable by
doing a dovetail, a breadth-first search of all derivations.
If a proof exists then it will appear, eventually.\footnote{%
  That is, in a particular subject such as elementary number theory,
  the set of theorems is computably enumerable.}
%</discussion:Entscheidungsproblemi>

%<*discussion:Entscheidungsproblemii>
The difficulty is the `eventually'.
This algorithm is very slow.
Is there a tractable way?
In the terminology that we now have, the modified 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}}
is a decision problem:~given
a statement~$\sigma$ and a bound, is there 
a sequence~$\omega$ of statements witnessing a proof 
that ends in~$\sigma$ and 
that is shorter than the bound?
A computer can quickly check whether a given proof~$\omega$ is valid so  
this problem is in~$\NP$.
With the current status of the
$\P$ versus~$\NP$ problem,
the answer to the question in the prior paragraph is that
no one knows of a fast algorithm, 
but no one can prove that there isn't one either.
%</discussion:Entscheidungsproblemii>

%<*discussion:Entscheidungsproblemiii>
As far back as 1956, G\"odel\index{Godel, K@G\"odel, K!letter to von Neumann} 
raised these issues in a letter to
von~Neumann (this letter did not become public until years later).\footnote{%
  At the 1930 meeting where G\"{o}del, as an unknown fresh PhD, 
  announced his Incompleteness Theorem,
  the only person who approached him with interest was
  von~Neumann, who was already well established.
  Later, when G\"{o}del was trying to escape the Nazis, 
  von~Neumann wrote to the director of the Institute for Advanced Study, 
  ``G\"{o}del is absolutely irreplaceable.
  He is the only mathematician \ldots{} about whom I would dare
  to make this statement.''
  So they were professionally quite close.
  At the time of the letter, von~Neumann had cancer, probably from his 
  work on the Manhattan Project. 
  G\"{o}del was misinformed and wrote, ``Since you now, as I hear, are 
  feeling stronger, I would like to allow myself to write you about a 
  mathematical problem, of which your opinion would very much interest me.''
  Within a year, von~Neumann passed away.
  We don't know if he replied or even read the letter.}
\begin{quotation}\small
One can obviously easily construct a Turing machine,
which for every formula $F$ in first order predicate logic and every
natural number $n$, allows one to decide if there is a proof of $F$ of
length $n$ ($\text{length} = \text{number of symbols}$).
Let $\Psi (F,n)$ be the number of steps the machine requires for this
and let $\phi (n) = \max_F \Psi(F,n)$.
The question is how fast $\phi (n)$ grows for an optimal machine.
One can show that $\phi (n) \geq k \cdot n$.
If there really were a machine with
$\phi(n) \sim k \cdot n$
(or even $\sim k \cdot n^2$),
this would have consequences of the greatest importance.
Namely, it would obviously mean that in spite of the undecidability of
the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem}, the mental work of a mathematician
concerning Yes-or-No questions could be completely replaced by a machine.
After all, one would simply have to choose the natural number $n$ so large
that when the machine does not deliver a result,
it makes no sense to think more about the problem.
Now it seems to me, however, to be
\citetext{completely within the realm of possibility
  that $\phi (n)$ grows that slowly}{%
  Hartmanis observes \autocite{Hartmanis} that it is interesting
  that G\"odel, the person who destroyed Hilbert's program of
  automating mathematics, seemed to think that these problems quite
  possibly are solvable in linear or quadratic time.}.
% Since it seems that $\phi (n) \geq k \cdot n$ is the only estimation which
% one can obtain by a generalization of the proof of the undecidability
% of the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} and after all
% $\phi(n) \sim k \cdot n$ (or $\sim k \cdot n^2$)
% only means that the number of steps as opposed to trial and error can be
% reduced from $N$ to $\log N$ (or $(\log N)^2$).
% However, such strong reductions appear in other finite problems,
% for example in the computation of the quadratic residue symbol using repeated
% application of the law of reciprocity.
\ldots{}\
It would be interesting to know, for instance,
the situation concerning the determination of primality of a number and
how strongly in general the number of steps in finite combinatorial problems
can be reduced with respect to simple exhaustive search.
\end{quotation}
%</discussion:Entscheidungsproblemiii>

Again, this brings up back to this book's beginning.
Again, we are asking, ``What can be done?''
But here we are asking about what can be done feasibly,
what can be done in a reasonable time (or reasonable space, etc).
Taking more time than the lifetime of the universe is not reasonable.

In summary, we can compare $\P$~versus~$\NP$ with the \HP.
The \HP{} and related results tell us, in the light of
Church's Thesis, what is knowable in principle. 
The $\P$~versus~$\NP$ question, in contrast,  
speaks to what we can know in practice.



% .........................................
\subsection{Discussion} % https://www.scottaaronson.com/papers/pnp.pdf and https://arxiv.org/pdf/1108.1791.pdf
%<*discussion:PvsNPii>
Whether $\P$ equals~$\NP$ 
is certainly the sexiest question in the Theory of Computing today.
It has attracted a great deal of gossip.
\citetext{In 2018, a poll}{%
  The poll was conducted by 
  W~Gasarch, a prominent researcher, and also blogger, 
  in Computational Complexity.
  There were 124 respondents.
  For the description see
  \url{https://www.cs.umd.edu/users/gasarch/BLOGPAPERS/pollpaper3.pdf}.
  Note the suggestions that both respondents and even the surveyor
  took the enterprise in a light-hearted way.}
of experts found that out of $152$ respondents, 
\citetext{$88\%$ thought that $\P\neq\NP$}{%
  Gasarch divided respondents into experts, 
  the people who are known to have seriously thought about the problem, and 
  the masses.
  The experts were $99\%$ for $\P\neq\NP$.} 
while only $12\%$ thought that $\P=\NP$.
%</discussion:PvsNPii>
This subsection outlines some of the intuition. 

\margingraphic[l]{0.25\textwidth}{complexity/pix/license_plate_alan_selman.jpg}{A~Selman's plate, courtesy S~Selman} % {\protect\citetext{A~Selman's plate}{Photo courtesy of S~Selman.}} % https://rjlipton.wordpress.com/2021/01/29/alan-selman-1941-2021/
%<*discussion:PvsNPiii>
% First we address the intuition around the conjecture that $\P\neq\NP$.
One way to think about the question is that
a problem is in $\P$ if finding a solution is fast
whereas a problem is in $\NP$ if verifying the correctness of a given
witness is fast.
Then $\P\subseteq\NP$
becomes the observation that if a problem is
fast to solve then it must be fast to verify.
But the $\P\supseteq\NP$ inclusion, the contention if a problem has an answer 
that is fast to verify then that problem must be fast to solve, is not
what we would naturally expect.
For example, speaking informally,
\citetext{S Aaronson has said}{%
  See \autocite{Roberts} for both the Aaronson and Williams estimates.
  See also Aaronson's \url{https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=1720}
  and \url{https://www.scottaaronson.com/papers/pnp.pdf}.
  },
``I'd give it a $2$ to~$3$ percent chance that $\P$ equals $\NP$.
Those are the betting odds that I'd take.''
Similarly, R~Williams puts the chance that $\P\neq\NP$ at $80$\%.
%</discussion:PvsNPiii>

% The collective failure by many talented people to find efficient algorithms for specific NP~complete problems strongly suggests that no efficient algorithm exists for any of them.

As early as Karp's original paper there was a sense that $\P\neq \NP$
was the natural supposition.
Here is the first paragraph of that paper.
\begin{quotation}\noindent
All the general methods presently known for computing the chromatic number of
a graph, 
deciding whether a graph has a Hamiltonian circuit,
\ldots{}
% or solving a system of linear inequalities in which the variables are
% constrained to be $0$ or~$1$,
require a combinatorial search for which the worst case time requirement
grows exponentially with the length of the input.
In this paper we give theorems which strongly suggest
\ldots{} that these problems,
as well as many others, will remain
intractable perpetually.
\end{quotation}

\margingraphic*{0.26\textwidth}{complexity/pix/jigsaw.png}
\begin{credit}
  By Psyon (Own work) CC BY-SA 3.0 \url{https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Jigsaw_Puzzle.svg}
\end{credit}
This intuition comes from a number of sources but an important one is the 
everyday experience that there is a genuine difference between the difficulty of
finding a solution and that of verifying that an existing solution is 
correct.

Imagine a jigsaw puzzle. 
We perceive that
if a demon gave us an assembled puzzle~$\omega$,
then checking that it is correct is very much easier than
it would have been to work out the solution from scratch.
Checking for correctness is mechanical, tedious.
But the finding of a solution, we perceive, 
is creative. 
% \Dash we feel that solving
% a jigsaw puzzle by brute-force trying every possible piece against every
% other is too much computation to be practical.
Similarly, mathematicians find that verifying the correctness of a 
formally-described proof is routine, while
finding that proof in the first place may be the work of a lifetime, or more.

V~Strassen has compared our confidence in $P\neq \NP$ with our confidence
in laws of natural science such as $F=ma$ or $PV=nRT$, 
``The evidence in favor of $\P\neq\NP$ \ldots{}\ 
is so overwhelming, and the consequences of their failure are so grotesque, 
that their status may perhaps be compared to that of physical laws 
rather than that of ordinary mathematical conjectures.''

Some commentators
have extended this way of thinking beyond the narrow bounds of
Theoretical Computer Science.
One is \citetext{A~Wigderson}{%
  See \autocite{Wigderson}.}, 
% ``[$\P=\NP$ would be] utopia for the quest for knowledge
% and technological development by humans.
% There would be a short program that, for every mathematical statement
% and given page limit, would quickly generate a proof of that length, if one
% exists!
% There would be a short program which, given detailed constraints on
% any engineering task, would quickly generate a design which meets the given
% criteria, if one exists. The design of new drugs, cheap energy, better strains
% of food, safer transportation, and robots that would release us from all
% unpleasant chores, would become a triviality.''
% He continues,
``[M]ost people revolt against the idea that such amazing discoveries 
like Wiles's proof of Fermat, Einstein's relativity, Darwin's evolution,
Edison's inventions, as well as all the ones we are awaiting, could be produced
in succession quickly by a mindless robot.
\ldots{} If $\P=\NP$ , any human (or
computer) would have the sort of reasoning power traditionally ascribed to
deities, and this seems hard to accept.'' 

\citetext{Cook is of much the same mind}{See \autocite{Cook}.},
``\ldots{} Similar remarks apply
to diverse creative human endeavors,
such as designing airplane wings, creating
physical theories, or even composing music.
The question in each case is to what
extent an efficient algorithm for recognizing a good result can be found.''
Perhaps it seems to be hyperbole to say that if $\P=\NP$ then writing
great symphonies would
be a job for mechanisms,
but it is correct to say that if $\P=\NP$ and if we can write
fast algorithms to recognize excellent music\Dash and our everyday
experience with Artificial Intelligence makes this seem more and
more a possibility\Dash 
then we could have fast mechanical writers of excellent music.

We close this section with a taste of the intuition behind the contrarian view, 
the sense that perhaps $\P=\NP$ could be right.

%<*discussion:PvsNPiv>
\citetext{Many observers}{% 
  For example,  \autocite{Viola}.} 
have noted that  
there are cases where everyone ``knew'' that some algorithm was the 
fastest but in the end it proved not to be so.
This chapter began with a story about A~Kolmogorov trying to show that
multiplication requires $n^2$ steps only to have a student,
A~Karatsuba, find a faster way.
Another is the problem of solving systems
of linear equations.
The Gauss's Method algorithm, which runs in time $\bigOh(n^3)$, is 
perfectly natural and had been known for centuries without anyone
making improvements.
However, while  trying to prove that Gauss's Method is optimal,
V~Strassen found a \citetext{$\bigOh(n^{\lg 7})$ method 
($\lg 7\approx 2.81)$}{%
  Strassen's algorithm is used in practice.
  The current record is $\bigOh(n^{2.37})$ but it is not practical.
  It is a \definend{galactic algorithm} because while runs faster than any 
  other known algorithm when the problem is sufficiently large, 
  but the first such problem is so big that we never use the algorithm.
  For other examples of this happening see \autocite{wiki:Galactic}. 
}.\footnote{%
  Here is an analogy:
  consider the problem of evaluating $2p^3+3p^2+4p+5$.
  Someone might claim that writing it as 
  $2\cdot p\cdot p\cdot p+3\cdot p\cdot p+4\cdot p+5$
  makes obvious that it requires
  six multiplications.
  But rewriting it as $p\cdot(p\cdot (2\cdot p+3)+4)+5$ 
  shows that it can be done with just three.
  That is, naturalness and obviousness
  do not guarantee that something is correct. 
  Without a proof, it is a genuine possibility that someone will
  produce a clever way to do the job with less.}

A more dramatic speedup happens with the 
\citetext{\probname{Matching} problem}{%
  The \probname{Drummer} problem described earlier is a special case
  of this for bipartite graphs.}.\index{Matching problem@\probname{Matching} problem} % makeindex doesn't like it: \index{Marriage problem@\probname{Marriage} problem|see \probname{Matching} problem} 
It starts with a graph whose vertices represent people 
and such that pairs of vertices are connected if those people are compatible. 
We want a set of edges that is maximal, and such that no 
two edges share a vertex.
The naive algorithm tries all possible match sets, which takes
$2^m$ checks where~$m$ is the number of edges.
Even with only a hundred people there are 
\citetext{more things to try than
atoms in the universe}{%
  There are about $10^{80}$ atoms in the universe.
  A graph with $100$ vertices has the potential for $\binom{100}{2}$
  edges, which is about $100^2$.
  Trying every edge would be $2^{10\,000}\approx 10^{10\,000/3.32}$ cases, 
  which is much greater than $10^{80}$. 
}.
But \citetext{since the 1960's we have an algorithm}{%
  Due to J~Edmonds.} 
that runs in polytime.
%</discussion:PvsNPiv>

%<*discussion:PvsNPv>
And every day  on the 
\citetext{Theory of Computing blog feed}{\autocite{blog:TOC}} 
there are examples of researchers producing
algorithms faster than the ones previously known.
A person can certainly have the sense that we are only just starting
to explore what is possible with algorithms.
\citetext{R~J~Lipton captured this feeling}{\autocite{Lipton}}.
\begin{quotation}
Since we are constantly discovering new ways to program our ``machines,''
why not a discovery that shows how to factor? or how to solve $\SAT$?
Why are we all so sure that there are no great new programming methods
still to be discovered?
\ldots{}
% Methods that could allow us to \ldots{} solve $\SAT$?
I am puzzled that so many are convinced that these problems could not
fall to new programming tricks, yet that is what is done each and every
day in their own research.
\end{quotation}
%</discussion:PvsNPv>
%<*discussion:PvsNPvi>
\citetext{D~Knuth has a related but somewhat different take}{\autocite{Knuth}}.
\begin{quotation}
Some of my reasoning is admittedly naive: It's hard to believe that
$\P\neq\NP$ and that so many brilliant people have failed to discover why.
On the other hand if you imagine a number $M$ that's finite but incredibly
large \ldots{} then there's a humongous number of possible algorithms that
do $n^M$ bitwise addition or shift operations on $n$ given bits,
and it's really hard to believe that all of those algorithms fail.

My main point, however, is that I don't believe that the equality
$\P=\NP$ will turn out to be helpful even if it is proved,
because such a proof will almost surely be nonconstructive.
Although I think $M$ probably exists, I also think human beings will never
know such a value.
I even suspect that nobody will even know an upper bound on $M$.

Mathematics is full of examples where something is proved to exist,
yet the proof tells us nothing about how to find it.
Knowledge of the mere existence of an algorithm is completely different
from the knowledge of an actual algorithm.
\end{quotation}
%</discussion:PvsNPvi>

Of course, 
\citetext{all this is speculation}{%
  Arthur C Clarke's celebrated First Law is, ``When a distinguished but elderly 
  scientist states that something is possible, he is almost certainly right. 
  When he states that something is impossible, he is very probably wrong.''
  \autocite{wiki:Clarke}}.
Speculating is fun, and in order
to make progress in their work, people must have some intuition.
But in the end, we
look to settle the question with proof.\footnote{%
  There is also in the air what we could think of as a third side to this
  debate.
  There is some recent evidence, as outlined by two Topics at the end 
  of this chapter, that the fact that a problem is $\protect\NP$ complete
  may not mean that in practice it is intractable even for reasonably
  large-scale instances.
  Of course this does not change that the $\P$ vs~$\NP$ question
  is about the limit as the instance size goes to infinity, but 
  in that theory should be at least a rough guide for practice
  a person can reasonably ask whether the attention given to the question 
  puts the focus of the community in exactly the right place.}
\index{P versus NP@$\P$ versus $\NP$|)}



\begin{exercises}
% TODO:
% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/19884/50343

\begin{exercise}
This diagram is an extension of one from
\zcref{fig:LangDecisionProblemsWithNPHard}. 
It orders the universe of problems by $\leq_p$.
(Recall that if $A\leq_p B$ then $A\leq_T B$, so it also orders
the universe by $\leq_T$. 
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  It also assumes that $\P\neq\NP$.
}{%
  It also assumes that $\P\neq\NP$ and so 
  the two collections are shown as separate, with $\P$ as a strict
  subset of $\NP$.
  As a reminder, \compclass{Rec} is the collection of computable languages
  and \compclass{RE} is the collection of computably enumerable languages.
} 
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2012.pdf}}
\end{center}
On that, locate these languages.
\begin{exparts}
\item $K=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents in binary $x\in\N$ where $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}}$
\item $E=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents in binary an even number}}$
\item $\lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,B}\suchthat\text{there is a path from $v_0$ to $v_1$ of length at most $B$}}$
\item $\SAT$
\end{exparts}
What about the trivial sets $\emptyset$ and $\kleenestar{\B}$?
\begin{answer}\verified
Here is the figure with the sets.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp2013.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Every computably enumerable set reduces to~$K$.
  So the language~$K$ is at the top of \probname{RE}.
\item Every set can compute the set~$E$ of (representations of) even numbers
in the time that it takes to read in the input
(for that matter, just in the time it takes to read in the least significant
bit of the input).
So it is is all the way at the bottom.
\item There are a variety of algorithms for the shortest path problem,
but all are fast (quadratic or faster), so $\lang$
is in the middle of~$\P$.
\item The $\SAT$ problem is $\NP$~complete so it is at the
top of the $\NP$ problems.
\end{exparts}
Each trivial sets is $\leq_p$ related to no set other than itself,
so they would sit off to the side, as just two lone points.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your study partner emails you with, ``The 
$\probname{Satisfiability}$ problem is $\NP$ 
because it is not computable in polynomial time, so far as we know.''
It's a short sentence but find three mistakes.
\begin{answer}\verified
The number one mistake is that ``not computable in polynomial time'' 
isn't the criteria for membership in~$\NP$.
Instead, the criteria for a problem being a member of~$\NP$
is that it has a polytime verifier.

Another thing wrong is that the statement seems to confuse $\NP$ with the 
set difference $\NP-\P$.
That is, the speaker seems to mean something like, ``experts suspect that
$\probname{Satisfiability}$ is a problem in~$\NP$ but not in~$\P$.''
(Along with that, the speaker may well mean $\NP$~complete, not just
an element of~$\NP$.)

One more thing wrong is that the statement seems to say that the problem 
is in that class because there is no algorithm yet known.
It is not a question of whether we know of such an algorithm, it is
a question of whether one exists.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  William Gasarch, \url{https://www.cs.umd.edu/~gasarch/COURSES/452/F14/poly.pdf}
\end{credit}
Someone in your class says, 
``I can show that the \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem is not
in~$\P$, which will demonstrate that $\P\neq\NP$.
The algorithm is:~given an instance~$\mathcal{G}$,
generate all permutations of $\mathcal{G}$'s vertices, 
test each to find if it is a circuit, and if any
circuits appear then accept the input, else reject the input.
For sure this is not polynomial.''
Where is their mistake?
\begin{answer}\verified
This particular algorithm is indeed not polynomial.
But how to prove that there does not exist another algorithm,
and that algorithm is polynomial?
No one knows.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
