\end{tabular}
\quad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems45.pdf}}

    \caption{An exhaustive search for the \probname{Traveling Salesman} problem.}
    \label{fig:ExhaustiveSearchTSP}
\end{figure}
Basically, you can go around the outside for $14$ or go through the middle for 
$15$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}


% https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concorde_TSP_Solver
% from
% https://rjlipton.wordpress.com/2020/11/19/traveling-salesman-problem-meets-complexity-theory/
% Video: 
% https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q8nQTNvCrjE

% Vertex cover?
% Some other example selected for accessibility?





% ===============================================================
\extra{\texorpdfstring{$\SAT$}{SAT} solvers as oracles} \label{extra:SATSolvers}

% Our characterization of $\NP$ complete problems as hard may 
% give the mistaken impression that for such a problem any algorithm is
% terribly slow.
% But the situation is more nuanced than that.
The prior Extra section gives an example of a problem where
the best algorithm we know is
super-polynomial, but in practice that problem is solvable for 
reasonably large instances.

Our touchstone hard problem is $\SAT\!$.
This section 
demonstrates a 
\definend{$\SAT$ solver},\index{SAT solver@$\SAT$ solver}
and uses that as an oracle to solve others.

A problem reduction $\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ gives a way to transfer problem 
domains, 
to change questions about $\prob_1$ into questions about $\prob_0$.
Here we take $\prob_0$ to be $\SAT$ and
for $\prob_1$ we will use Soduku.

\begin{center}\small\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabular}{|*{3}{c}|*{3}{c}|*{3}{c}|}
  \hline
  $$  &$$  &$9$ &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$1$ &$5$ \\
  $5$ &$$  &$$  &$4$ &$$  &$9$ &$7$ &$$  &$$ \\
  $4$ &$7$ &$3$ &$5$ &$6$ &$1$ &$9$ &$$  &$$ \\ 
  \hline
  $$  &$$  &$$  &$7$ &$4$ &$$  &$$  &$9$ &$6$ \\
  $$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$8$ &$$ \\
  $$  &$$  &$4$ &$8$ &$3$ &$$  &$1$ &$5$ &$$ \\ 
  \hline
  $1$ &$3$ &$5$ &$9$ &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$2$ \\
  $$  &$$  &$6$ &$2$ &$5$ &$7$ &$$  &$3$ &$$ \\
  $7$ &$2$ &$$  &$$  &$1$ &$$  &$$  &$$  &$9$ \\ 
  \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In case it is unfamiliar,
the popular Soduku puzzle 
starts with a 9-by-9 array, with some of the cells 
already filled in.
An example is above.
Players solve it by filling in the blanks while satisfying th3
restrictions that every row, column, and subsquare must contain 
each of the numbers $1$--$9$ once and only once. 

As to how hard the problem is,
% if we restrict our attention to the $9\times 9$ puzzle only then there are
% finitely many gameboards. 
% In principle 
% we could write a routine that inputs
% each initial puzzle boards and outputs the associated solutions.
% Going from input to output is then just a lookup table and runs in constant time.
% 
% But in another sense this puzzle is hard.
studying the computational complexity of a problem requires that
we describe how a solution algorithm's use of some resource grows 
with the input size.
So we reframe the problem to allow instances of
different input sizes.
The natural way to generalize the puzzle is:~instead of eighty one
variables $x_{1,1}, \ldots{}\, x_{9,9}$ 
we could use an arbitrary number,  $x_1, \ldots{}\, x_n$. 
Instead of those variables taking on $1$--$9$, they could take on
a value in $1$--$k$
(we can call these `colors'). 
And, in place of rows, columns, and subsquares that are driven by the 
geometry, 
a problem instance could have arbitrary sets,  
$S\subseteq \set{x_1,\ldots\, x_n}$ of size~$k$. 
With those adjustments,
we can show that
\citetext{the \probname{Soduku} problem is $\NP$~complete}{%
  First proved in the MS thesis of Takayuki Yato,
  from the Department of Information Science at the University of Tokyo
  in 1987.
  That document seems to have disappeared from the web; for a place to
  start looking for it, see the Soduku Wikipedia page.}.
% Thus we can fairly consider this problem to
% be quite hard.

However, having noted this generalization, here we limit our attention to
the traditional $9\times 9$ board.

To solve puzzle instances using the $\SAT$ solver as an oracle we
need a reduction $\probname{Soduku}\leq_p\SAT\!$.
We will produce a
function that inputs game boards and outputs 
Propositional Logic expressions.
Those expressions will be in Conjunctive Normal form, 
CNF.\index{Conjunctive Normal form}\index{CNF}\index{Propositional logic!Conjunctive Normal form}
These are the conjunction of clauses
where each clause is the disjunction of literals.
% (a literal is either a single atom such as $x_i$ 
% or the negation of an atom such as $\neg x_i$).
Two examples are
$(x_1\vee x_2)\wedge (\neg x_2\vee x_3)$  and  
$(x_1\vee x_2\vee \neg x_3)\wedge x_4\wedge (\neg x_2\vee x_3)$. 
% The $\SAT$ solver can require that input be in this form because
% for any Boolean function there is a CNF expression giving that
% behavior; 
More is in \zcref{sec:PropLogic}.

The $\SAT$ solver that we use needs its input file
formatted to a standard called DIMACS.
This file format starts with comment lines, 
with the initial character~\str{c}. 
Next comes a problem line, which starts with a~\str{p},
followed by a space and the problem type \str{cnf}, 
then followed by a space and the number of variables, 
and then followed by a space and the number of clauses.
After that line, the rest of the file consists of the clause descriptions.

To describe a clause the file lists its indices.
Thus we describe $x_2\vee x_5\vee x_6$ with the list \str{2 5 6}.
Negations are described with negatives, so that 
$\neg x_5\vee x_7\vee \neg x_9$ matches \str{-5 7 -9}.
These clause descriptions are separated by \str{0}'s.\footnote{%
  Since DIMACS uses $0$ to separate clauses, 
  in this section we don't follow our usual practice of 
  starting with the first variable named $x_0$.
  Rather, we start with $x_1$.}

We will have many variables. 
For instance, for the  Soduku array's 
row~$1$, column~$1$ entry, we will have a Boolean 
variable $x_{1,1,1}$, another variable $x_{1,1,2}$, etc., up to 
$x_{1,1,9}$. 
Only one of these nine will be \true\!. 
The variable $x_{1,1,v}$ is \true{} if in our solution the number in 
row $1$ and column $1$ is $v$. 
% Otherwise this variable is \false.
Thus, if for instance in the first row and first column the solution has the
value~$5$ then $x_{1,1,5}$ is \true{} while all other $x_{1,1,i}$ are~\false\!.

Thus  
for each row, column, and value triple $r,c,v\in\set{1,\ldots{}9}$
we will have a variable $x_{r,c,v}$.
That's $9^3=729$ variables.

The CNF expression that we will produce has clauses of two kinds. 
One describes the general rules of the game Soduku while the other
is specific to the particular starting board instance. 
% It is as though we bought a puzzle book and opened first to the 
% introduction describing the rules, and later opened to the page containing the 
% specific partial board.
% 
To describe the general rules we need lot of clauses describing 
relationships among the variables.
An example of such a rule is that exactly one of 
$x_{1,1,1}$, $x_{1,1,2}$, \ldots{} $x_{1,1,9}$ is $\true\!$.
There are too many of these rules to write by hand so 
we will get the computer to do them.

The Racket
file starts with some constants that make the code easier to read.
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=8,lastline=12]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}

Each row of the puzzle must contain each number $1$, \ldots{} $9$,
so that is nine restrictions.
For instance, to express that the first row contains an entry with 
the value $2$, in the Propositional Logic statement we include this clause.
\begin{equation*}
x_{1,1,2}
  \vee x_{1,2,2}
  \vee x_{1,3,2}
  \vee x_{1,4,2}
  \vee x_{1,5,2}
  \vee x_{1,6,2}
  \vee x_{1,7,2}
  \vee x_{1,8,2}
  \vee x_{1,9,2}
\end{equation*} 
So the DIMACS format requires this corresponding list: 
\str{( (1 1 2) (1 2 2) (1 3 2) (1 4 2) (1 5 2) (1 6 2) (1 7 2) (1 8 2) (1 9 2) )}. 
The Racket code below produces expresses 
all of the row restrictions with one such list for each row and value.
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=104,lastline=115]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}
% Running that routine produces a list of lists.
% This is a typical member,
% requiring that at least one entry in row~$3$ must be an~$8$.
% \begin{lstlisting}
% ((3 1 8) (3 2 8) (3 3 8) (3 4 8) (3 5 8) (3 6 8) (3 7 8) (3 8 8) (3 9 8))
% \end{lstlisting}

The column restrictions are much the same.
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=117,lastline=128]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}
Here is a typical output line,
requiring that at least one entry in column~$7$ must be an~$8$.
\begin{lstlisting}
((1 7 8) (2 7 8) (3 7 8) (4 7 8) (5 7 8) (6 7 8) (7 7 8) (8 7 8) (9 7 8))
\end{lstlisting}

For the subsquares, the restrictions are the same in principle
but the form of the code is a bit different.
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=130,lastline=144]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}
Here is one of the box restrictions produced by that code, 
saying that some entry in the upper-right box has the value~$8$.
\begin{lstlisting}
((7 1 8) (7 2 8) (7 3 8) (8 1 8) (8 2 8) (8 3 8) (9 1 8) (9 2 8) (9 3 8)) 
\end{lstlisting}

Running the $\SAT$ solver with just the restrictions above 
finds that they can be satisfied. 
But there is a surprise.
It finds a satisfying assignment by putting more than one
value in some boxes and no value at all in some other boxes.
Thus we need one more set of restrictions, that 
no entry can contain two values. 

We add clauses like  $\neg x_{3,4,1}\vee \neg x_{3,4,2}$, 
meaning that the row~$3$ and column~$4$ entry cannot be both 
a $1$  and a~$2$ (it could be neither).
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=93,lastline=102]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}
This is one of the resulting lines,
enforcing that the entry in row~$8$ and column~$8$ cannot be both
$6$ and~$9$
(again, the negative means logical negation).
\begin{lstlisting}
((8 8 -6) (8 8 -9))
\end{lstlisting}

To finish, we must specify the initial board.
For example, to tell the $\SAT$ solver that there is a $9$ 
in row~$1$ and column~$3$ 
we include the one-literal clause $x_{1,3,9}$.
Here are the first few lines of that routine.
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=155,lastline=160]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center} % full last line is 184

In this development, and in the Racket file, 
we work in  $x_{r,c,v}$'s.
But DIMACS wants a single index.
So 
we need to convert each of our variables to some $x_k$ and back again.
The formula is $k=1+81\cdot(\sizeof{v}-1)+9\cdot(r-1)+(c-1)$.
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=32,lastline=56]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}

Here are a couple of example conversions using these routines.

\begin{lstlisting}
> (triple->varnum 3 4 5)
346
> (varnum->triple 346)
'(3 4 5)
\end{lstlisting}

Now we can write all of this to the DIMACS-format file.
This will convert the clauses. 

\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=61,lastline=90]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}

And this gathers the clauses together and then calls the
above routine.

\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=196,lastline=211]{\satsolverfile soduku-dimacs.rkt}
\end{center}

After running this
\begin{lstlisting}
 > (dump-to-file) 
\end{lstlisting}
the first few lines of the file
\pathname{soduku.cnf} look like this.
% This is the DIMACs format file. 
\begin{center}
  \lstinputlisting[firstline=1,lastline=14]{\satsolverfile soduku.cnf}
\end{center}
Remember that the \str{0}'s separate clauses.

We can now run the $\SAT$ solver, MiniSat \autocite{MiniSat}.
\begin{lstlisting}

It took far less than a second, on an ordinary  laptop.
The algorithms for $\SAT$ solvers are exponential in the worst case but
they seem to do very well in practice.

To see a solution entry, pick a positive number from the output.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (varnum->triple 8)
'(1 8 1)
\end{lstlisting}
Here is a positive output number that doesn't show in the above line.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (varnum->triple 107)
'(3 8 2)
\end{lstlisting}
Thus, row~$3$ and column~$8$ holds~$2$.

Some simple routines built on what is above 
to show the entire board are in \pathname{sat-solver.rkt}.
% \begin{center}
%   \lstinputlisting[firstline=5,lastline=47]{\satsolverfile sat-solver.rkt}
% \end{center}
% Running that final routine gives this.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (show-solved-board)
'#(#(2 6 9 3 7 8 4 1 5)
   #(5 8 1 4 2 9 7 6 3)
   #(4 7 3 5 6 1 9 2 8)
   #(8 1 2 7 4 5 3 9 6)
   #(3 5 7 1 9 6 2 8 4)
   #(6 9 4 8 3 2 1 5 7)
   #(1 3 5 9 8 4 6 7 2)
   #(9 4 6 2 5 7 8 3 1)
   #(7 2 8 6 1 3 5 4 9))
\end{lstlisting}

In summary, we can in reasonable time solve
instances of $\SAT$ that are not toy exercises.

% (As an aside, writing CNF expressions for Propositional 
% Logic and writing Turing machines are analogous.
% For both, the simplicity of the format makes the resulting input large,
% and a bit awkward.
% On the other hand, for both 
% the simplicity of the format makes them easily understandable.)

\begin{exercises}  % see additional exercise at https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/155049/50343 
\begin{exercise}
This board is described online as an especially hard Soduku.
Use the routines from this section to solve it.
\begin{center}\small\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabular}{|*{3}{c}|*{3}{c}|*{3}{c}|}
  \hline
  $$  &$3$ &$4$ &$5$ &$$  &$6$ &$9$ &$$  &$$ \\
  $$  &$$  &$5$ &$4$ &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$ \\
  $$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$8$ &$$  &$$  &$1$ &$$ \\ 
  \hline
  $$  &$$  &$$  &$8$ &$2$ &$3$ &$$  &$$  &$7$ \\
  $1$ &$$  &$8$ &$7$ &$$  &$$  &$3$ &$$  &$$ \\
  $$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$9$ &$$  &$$  &$$ \\ 
  \hline
  $$  &$8$ &$7$ &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$ \\
  $$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$8$ &$$  &$7$ &$2$ \\
  $4$ &$$  &$9$ &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$  &$$ \\ 
  \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
We can adjust the initial board to match the new one.
Here are the first few lines.
\begin{lstlisting}
; Exercise 
(define INITIAL-CLAUSES
  (list (list '(1 2 3)) ; there is a 3 in position (1,2)
        (list '(1 3 4))
        (list '(1 4 5))
        (list '(1 6 6))
        (list '(1 7 9))
\end{lstlisting}
Then hitting the Run button in Dr Racket gives the output of all the clauses.
This command drops them all to \pathname{soduku.cfg}.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (dump-to-file)  
\end{lstlisting}
With the data in the external file we can run the command to implement
the SAT solver.

Finally, in the file sat-solver.rkt are some more routines, including this one.
\begin{lstlisting}
> (show-solved-board)
'#(#(7 3 4 5 1 6 9 2 8)
   #(8 1 5 4 9 2 7 6 3)
   #(6 9 2 3 8 7 4 1 5)
   #(9 4 6 8 2 3 1 5 7)
   #(1 2 8 7 5 4 3 9 6)
   #(5 7 3 1 6 9 2 8 4)
   #(2 8 7 6 3 1 5 4 9)
   #(3 5 1 9 4 8 6 7 2)
   #(4 6 9 2 7 5 8 3 1))
\end{lstlisting}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}



% ========= Bounded Halting problem ===========
\extra{The \probname{Bounded Halting} problem}  \label{extra:BoundedHalting}
% Cite Ege Erdil https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8Af3X8b7f5piqtqZx/computability-and-complexity

This chapter's final section developed the intuition that the class
of \NP~complete problems forms a transition
between the solvable problems and the unsolvable ones.
\citetext{Here we support that view}{%
  This material developed from a post by Ege Erdil, 
  \url{https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8Af3X8b7f5piqtqZx/computability-and-complexity}.} by providing a connection.

The signature unsolvable problem is the \HP.
% This is an easily-solved variant.
% 
% \begin{problem} % [\definend{\probname{Simulation} problem}]\index{Simulation problem@\probname{Similation} problem}
% Given an index, an input, and a step bound, 
% $\sequence{e,x,S}\in \N\times\kleenestar{\B}\times\N$,
% decide whether Turing machine $\TM_e$ halts on~$x$ within $S$ steps.
% \end{problem}
% 
% We can solve this by just running the machine for $S$~steps to see
% if it halts.
% The next variant is not so trivial.

\begin{problem}[\definend{\probname{Nondeterministic Bounded Halting} problem}]\index{Nondeterministic Bounded Halting problem@\probname{Nondeterministic Bounded Halting} problem}
Given an index and a step bound, $\sequence{e,S}$, 
where $S$ is specified in unary,
decide whether the nondeterministic Turing machine $\TM_e$ 
halts on an empty tape within $S$~steps.
That is, decide whether $\TM_e$'s computation tree contains a 
maximal path that 
reaches a halting state in no more than $S$~many transitions.
\end{problem}

This problem is solvable:~for instance, we could use a deterministic machine to 
simulate all of $\TM_e$'s
at most $2^S$ possible paths and just check each for a halting state.

Thus, on a serial machine the naive approach to this problem is exponential.  
Naturally we ask if we can find a faster way.

\begin{theorem}  \label{th:BHPIsNPComplete}
The \probname{Nondeterministic Bounded Halting} problem is \NP{} complete. 
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Denote the \probname{Nondeterministic Bounded Halting} problem's 
language with $\lang[B]$. 

We first argue that $\lang[B]$ is in~\NP.
We can write a verifier that inputs a problem instance 
$\sequence{e,S}$ and a witness
that represents a halting branch 
(it might be a bitstring, where \str{0} and \str{1} describe the
two ways that the branch can go).
Clearly $\sequence{e,S}$ is in~$\lang[B]$ if and only if
there exists such a witness.
Also, the verifier can run in time polynomial 
in~$\lh{\sequence{e,S}}$ because $S$ is given in unary
(in time~$S$ there are at most $\sizeof{S}$ branchings).

What remains to argue is that $\lang[B]$ is \NP~hard.
We will show that any $\prob\in\NP$ has $\prob\leq_p\lang[B]$.
Each such $\prob$ has an associated polytime verifier,
a deterministic Turing machine~$\TM[V]$
where $\sigma\in\lang$
if and only if
there exists an~$\omega$ so that
$\TM[V]$ accepts $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$
and where $\TM[V]$ runs in time bounded by $p(\lh{\sigma})$ 
for some polynomial~$p$.  

For the reduction we need a computable function~$f$ so that
$\sigma\in\prob$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang[B]$.
On the left below is a nondeterministic Turing machine
(recall that one of our mental models is that such machines guess
values, or receive them from a demon).
Let this machine have index~$e_0$.
By the \smn~theorem the one on the right is $\TM_{s(e_0,\sigma)}$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts004.pdf}}
  \quad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{\asydir flowcharts/flowcharts005.pdf}}
\end{center}
Then $\sigma\in\prob$ if and only if 
$f(\sigma)=\sequence{ s(e_0,\sigma), p(\lh{\sigma}) }\in\lang[B]$, as required.
\end{proof}

% \end{problem}
